Code is available at https://github.com/hdubasullivan/AdaptivePnP.|[2611.09845](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09845)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Super-resolved digital terrain models for GEDI geolocation correction: agreement with airborne LiDAR DTM-based corrections at the beam level, not the footprint level**|Natsuki Yuda et.al.|The Global Ecosystem Dynamics Investigation (GEDI) mission measures three-dimensional forest structure with a LiDAR aboard the International Space Station. GEDI observations have geolocation errors of about 10 m, which cause misalignment with other remote sensing data and reduce the accuracy of downstream tasks. Matching GEDI ground elevations against high-resolution digital terrain models (DTMs) from airborne LiDAR is one way to correct these errors, but such DTMs exist in few regions. To widen this method's applicability, we super-resolved a more widely available low-resolution DTM and used it for the matching. Our question is whether a super-resolved DTM yields the same correction as a 1 m-resolution airborne LiDAR DTM and, if not, why. In steep forests of western Japan, we super-resolved an existing 10 m-resolution DTM to 1 m. Improving its accuracy required fusion with a global 30 m-resolution DTM from a different source. Using the resulting DTM, we corrected GEDI observation positions first by a single shift per ground track, then per observation. For most tracks, the shifts nearly matched those from the airborne LiDAR DTM, likely because DTM errors canceled out across many observations. Per-observation corrections, however, did not match. At a single observation, the error does not cancel out and is too large for the correction. This error likely originates mainly from the input DTM. Therefore, even without a 1 m-resolution DTM, a roughly 10 m-resolution DTM, fused with a global DTM and super-resolved to 1 m, should yield track-wise corrections nearly matching those from a 1 m-resolution DTM.|[2611.09854](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09854)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Flow-Guided Target-Space Alignment via Path Consistency**|Ruizhi Yuan et.al.|Many multimodal alignment problems require mapping a source modality into a fixed space defined by a target modality, where the target space is not merely an intermediate embedding but is used directly for downstream analysis. In this setting, successful alignment should preserve both the global structure of the target distribution and the instance-level correspondence observed in paired source--target training data. Existing objectives typically address only part of this goal: contrastive losses encourage relative similarity, distribution-matching losses improve marginal agreement, and pointwise regression fits each source sample to its paired target without explicitly modeling target-space path geometry. We propose FlowAlign, a flow-guided target-space alignment method that repurposes conditional flow matching as a training-time alignment signal rather than an inference-time transport model. FlowAlign uses the learned flow field to guide the alignment of source-side representations with the fixed target space, encouraging them to preserve paired correspondence while remaining compatible with the global target-space structure. We further provide theoretical analysis showing how FlowAlign helps improve paired correspondence by controlling endpoint accuracy and capturing local target-space geometry. Experiments on synthetic data, paired single-cell multiome benchmarks, and image--text alignment demonstrate that FlowAlign improves instance level correspondence while preserving target-space structure across different modality settings.|[2611.09863](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09863)|
|**2026-11-09**|**On Agentic Manipulation Policies**|Chen Chen et.al.|Recent work on robot manipulation has focused on specialist policies trained on demonstrations. We show that generalist AI models can control manipulators more reliably than specialist policies such as vision-language-action models (VLAs) and world-action models (WAMs). We systematically analyze agentic manipulation policies (AMPs), in which a generalist AI model commands the manipulator directly, adapting its actions as the task unfolds. We present AMP1, a reference harness that runs unchanged across single-arm, bimanual, and mobile manipulation. AMP1 achieves state-of-the-art results on the RoboLab-120 and CaP-Gym benchmarks, with no demonstrations or task-specific training. On RoboLab-120, AMP1 reaches an 85.6\% success rate zero-shot, compared to 49.2\% and 42.9\% for leading VLA and WAM policies. The agent succeeds zero-shot in 104 of 108 real-world trials spanning twelve tasks and three robot instances, reducing failure rates by 20x and 15x relative to strong VLA and WAM baselines. The basic harness supplies no grasping, pushing, or placing skills; the agent develops its own strategies, such as searching for hidden objects, using objects as tools, and calibrating its cameras. With the addition of a persistent memory store, the agent can also learn from experience: an augmented harness with a memory representation that is carried across episodes lowers the failure rate on RoboDojo bimanual manipulation tasks by 4.6x relative to zero-shot deployment. Our analysis suggests that AMPs are substantially more general and robust than specialist manipulation policies, providing a promising foundation for robot manipulation.|[2611.09892](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09892)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Stay on the Manifold: Hierarchical Bayesian Trajectory-Family Transfer for Sparse-Target Reconstruction and Sampling**|Elvis Han Cui et.al.|Where should reconstructions and new samples lie when a few observations reveal only fragments of a trajectory? We propose hierarchical Bayesian trajectory principal-manifold sampling (HB-TPM), which learns a family of smooth curves from ordered source tasks and adapts it to sparse targets. For unordered vectors, alternating progression assignments and coefficient updates estimate trajectory structure between and beyond the observed shots under the learned source family. Observation-aware extensions connect the trajectory prior to photon arrivals and image-encoded cardiac cycles. Our analysis characterizes the coefficient directions supplied by the prior and bounds sampling error conditional on curve recovery. Across 30 held-out vector tasks, HB-TPM reduces mean sliced-Wasserstein distance by 21.0-41.8% relative to a source-adapted VAE. Prior-strength and sampling ablations identify coefficient shrinkage as the main supported mechanism. On 100 simulated photon records, adaptation lowers mean relative trajectory RMSE by 50.2% versus target-only fitting and 23.6% versus source-mean prediction. Across three training seeds and five folds on 98 cardiac sequences, HB-TPM with latent-MSE training achieves the best mean reconstruction among the tested configurations. Together, the results demonstrate trajectory-family transfer for sparse-target reconstruction and trajectory-centered sampling when source and target tasks share comparable progression structure.|[2310.07801](http://arxiv.org/abs/2310.07801)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Source-Free Domain Adaptive Object Detection with Semantics Compensation**|Song Tang et.al.|Strong data augmentation is a fundamental component of state-of-the-art mean teacher-based Source-Free domain adaptive Object Detection (SFOD) methods, enabling consistency-based self-supervised optimization along weak augmentation. However, our theoretical analysis and empirical observations reveal a critical limitation: strong augmentation can inadvertently erase class-relevant components, leading to artificial inter-category confusion. To address this issue, we introduce Weak-to-strong Semantics Compensation (WSCo), a novel remedy that leverages weakly augmented images, which preserve full semantics, as anchors to enrich the feature space of their strongly augmented counterparts. Essentially, this compensates for the class-relevant semantics that may be lost during strong augmentation on the fly. Notably, WSCo can be implemented as a generic plug-in, easily integrable with any existing SFOD pipelines. Extensive experiments validate the negative impact of strong augmentation on detection performance, and the effectiveness of WSCo in enhancing the performance of previous detection models on standard benchmarks.|[2410.05557](http://arxiv.org/abs/2410.05557)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Domain Adaptive Diabetic Retinopathy Grading with Model Absence and Flowing Data**|Wenxin Su et.al.|Domain shift (the difference between source and target domains) poses a significant challenge in clinical applications, e.g., Diabetic Retinopathy (DR) grading. Despite considering certain clinical requirements, like source data privacy, conventional transfer methods are predominantly model-centered and often struggle to prevent model-targeted attacks. In this paper, we address a challenging Online Model-aGnostic Domain Adaptation (OMG-DA) setting, driven by the demands of clinical environments. This setting is characterized by the absence of the model and the flow of target data. To tackle the new challenge, we propose a novel approach, Generative Unadversarial ExampleS (GUES), which enables adaptation from a data-centric perspective. Specifically, we first theoretically reformulate conventional perturbation optimization in a generative way--learning a perturbation generation function with a latent input variable. During model instantiation, we leverage a Variational AutoEncoder to express this function. The encoder with the reparameterization trick predicts the latent input, whilst the decoder is responsible for the generation. Furthermore, the saliency map is selected as pseudo-perturbation labels. Because it not only captures potential lesions but also theoretically provides an upper bound on the function input, enabling the identification of the latent variable. Extensive comparative experiments on DR benchmarks with both frozen pre-trained models and trainable models demonstrate the superiority of GUES, showing robustness even with small batch size.|[2412.01203](http://arxiv.org/abs/2412.01203)|
|**2026-11-09**|**PolSAM: Polarimetric Scattering Mechanism Informed Segment Anything Model**|Yuqing Wang et.al.|PolSAR data presents unique challenges due to its rich and complex characteristics. Existing data representations, such as complex-valued data, polarimetric features, and amplitude images, are widely used. However, these formats often face issues related to usability, interpretability, and data integrity. Most feature extraction networks for PolSAR are small, limiting their ability to capture features effectively. To address these issues, We propose the Polarimetric Scattering Mechanism-Informed SAM (PolSAM), an enhanced Segment Anything Model (SAM) that integrates domain-specific scattering characteristics and a novel prompt generation strategy. PolSAM introduces Microwave Vision Data (MVD), a lightweight and interpretable data representation derived from polarimetric decomposition and semantic correlations. We propose two key components: the Feature-Level Fusion Prompt (FFP), which fuses visual tokens from pseudo-colored SAR images and MVD to address modality incompatibility in the frozen SAM encoder, and the Semantic-Level Fusion Prompt (SFP), which refines sparse and dense segmentation prompts using semantic information. Experimental results on the PhySAR-Seg datasets demonstrate that PolSAM significantly outperforms existing SAM-based and multimodal fusion models, improving segmentation accuracy, reducing data storage, and accelerating inference time. The source code and datasets will be made publicly available at https://github.com/XAI4SAR/PolSAM.|[2412.12737](http://arxiv.org/abs/2412.12737)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Align Your Query: Representation Alignment for Multimodality Medical Object Detection**|Ara Seo et.al.|Medical object detection suffers when a single detector is trained on mixed medical modalities (e.g., CXR, CT, MRI) due to heterogeneous statistics and disjoint representation spaces. To address this challenge, we turn to representation alignment, an approach that has proven effective for bringing features from different sources into a shared space. Specifically, we target the representations of DETR-style object queries and propose a simple, detector-agnostic framework to align them with modality context. First, we define modality tokens: compact, text-derived embeddings encoding imaging modality that are lightweight and require no extra annotations. We integrate the modality tokens into the detection process via Multimodality Context Attention (MoCA), mixing object-query representations via self-attention to propagate modality context within the query set. This preserves DETR-style architectures and adds negligible latency while injecting modality cues into object queries. We further introduce QueryREPA, a short pretraining stage that aligns query representations to their modality tokens using a task-specific contrastive objective with modality-balanced batches. Together, MoCA and QueryREPA produce modality-aware, class-faithful queries that transfer effectively to downstream training. Across diverse modalities trained altogether, the proposed approach consistently improves AP with minimal overhead and no architectural modifications, offering a practical path toward robust multimodality medical object detection.|[2510.02789](http://arxiv.org/abs/2510.02789)|
|**2026-11-09**|**ReefNet: A Large-Scale Dataset and Benchmark for Fine-Grained Coral Reef Recognition**|Abdulwahab Felemban et.al.|Coral reef monitoring requires scalable recognition of visually similar taxa across heterogeneous underwater surveys. We introduce ReefNet, a public dataset that aligns approximately 925K hard-coral point annotations from 76 CoralNet sources and an independently collected Al-Wajh dataset with the World Register of Marine Species taxonomy. A stratified expert audit of 8,962 patches guides filtering to a 479,027-annotation benchmark with 39 labels: 38 genera and the family Fungiidae. Expert agreement is 92% on the retained audit subset, rather than an exhaustive accuracy estimate. We evaluate pretrained models through zero-shot recognition, few-shot adaptation, and full fine-tuning, each on within-source ReefNet and held-out-source Al-Wajh tests. Biological vision-language pretraining provides the strongest zero-shot results; supervised adaptation substantially improves recognition, with DINOv2 reaching 65.79% macro recall on Al-Wajh. Matched text-description experiments improve all four evaluated MLLMs on Al-Wajh, while a GPT-5.4 reasoning ablation does not improve over direct classification. Long-tail recognition, label uncertainty, and geographic imbalance remain challenges. ReefNet provides a taxonomically grounded resource for studying these challenges; the Al-Wajh test is a regional stress test rather than evidence of global robustness. Public data and resource links are documented in the supplement.|[2510.16822](http://arxiv.org/abs/2510.16822)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Predictive Representation Learning for Manipulation via Streaming Egocentric 3D Hand Forecasting**|Ruicong Liu et.al.|Learning embodied manipulation from egocentric human data offers a scalable path, reducing the cost of collecting robot-specific demonstrations. However, existing representation learning methods largely stop at perceptual understanding, such as extracting what a scene contains or what an action is. A capable embodied agent is more than understanding: it predicts the next motion. We propose that forecasting 3D human hand motion is one suitable pretraining task, as the representation jointly learns how to manipulate objects and how the motion will unfold next. Building on this hypothesis, we introduce SFHand, the first representation learning method via streaming egocentric 3D hand forecasting. SFHand learns by autoregressively predicting future 3D hand states, including hand type, 2D bounding box, 3D pose, and trajectory, from a continuous stream of video and language instructions. Its autoregressive architecture is equipped with an ROI-enhanced memory layer, capturing temporal context while focusing on salient hand-centric regions. To enable this research, we also introduce SFHand, the first open-source large-scale dataset pairing synchronized 3D hand poses with fine-grained language instructions (247K clips, 3.95M frames). Transferred to downstream embodied control, the learned representation shows state-of-the-art performance on both simulation and real-world manipulation benchmarks, improving success rate by up to 13.4%. Our method also achieves leading 3D hand forecasting accuracy, with 35.8% lower ADE than baseline methods. These results suggest that forecasting hand motion is a better pretraining task for representation learning in embodied manipulation. Projec page: https://ruicongliu.github.io/sfhand/.|[2511.18127](http://arxiv.org/abs/2511.18127)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Vision to Geometry: 3D Spatial Memory for Sequential Embodied MLLM Reasoning and Exploration**|Zhongyi Cai et.al.|Embodied agents are expected to assist humans by actively exploring unknown environments and reasoning about spatial contexts. When deployed in real life, agents often face sequential tasks where each new task follows the completion of the previous one and may include infeasible objectives, such as searching for non-existent objects. However, most existing research focuses on isolated goals, overlooking the core challenge of sequential tasks: the ability to reuse spatial knowledge accumulated from previous explorations to guide subsequent reasoning and exploration. In this work, we investigate this underexplored yet practically significant embodied AI challenge. Specifically, we propose 3DSPMR, a 3D SPatial Memory Reasoning framework that utilizes Field-of-View (FoV) coverage as an explicit geometric prior. By integrating FoV-based constraints, 3DSPMR significantly enhances an agent's memory, reasoning, and exploration capabilities across sequential tasks. To facilitate research in this area, we further introduce SEER-Bench, a novel Sequential Embodied Exploration and Reasoning Benchmark that spans two foundational tasks: Embodied Question Answering (EQA) and Embodied Multi-modal Navigation (EMN). Extensive experiments verify that 3DSPMR achieves substantial performance gains on both sequential EQA and EMN tasks.|[2512.02458](http://arxiv.org/abs/2512.02458)|
|**2026-11-09**|**OpenView: Empowering MLLMs with Out-of-view VQA**|Qixiang Chen et.al.|Recent multimodal large language models (MLLMs) show great potential in natural image understanding. Yet, they perform well, mainly on reasoning in-view contents within the image frame. This paper presents the first study on out-of-view (OOV) understanding, i.e., the ability to reason about objects, activities, and scenes beyond the visible frame of a perspective view. Our technical contributions are threefold. First, we design OpenView, a four-stage pipeline that leverages panoramic imagery for large-scale and diverse multi-choice VQA synthesis, where full-scene coverage enables flexible view framing while preserving global scene awareness. Second, we curate OpenView-Dataset, a high-quality synthetic dataset from diverse real-world panoramas to empower MLLMs upon supervised fine-tuning. Third, we build OpenView-Bench, a benchmark that jointly measures choice and rationale accuracy for interpretable and diagnosable evaluation. Experimental results show that despite having a large gap from human performance in OOV VQA answer selection, multiple MLLMs, upon empowered by OpenView, can consistently boost their performance, uplifted from 48.3% to 62.3% on average. Code, benchmark, and data are available at https://github.com/q1xiangchen/OpenView.|[2512.18563](http://arxiv.org/abs/2512.18563)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Remote Sensing Change Detection via Weak Temporal Supervision**|Xavier Bou et.al.|Semantic change detection in remote sensing aims to identify land cover changes between bi-temporal image pairs. Progress in this area has been limited by the scarcity of annotated datasets, as pixel-level annotation is costly and time-consuming. To address this, recent methods leverage synthetic data or generate artificial change pairs, but transferring change detection models across datasets and acquisition conditions remains challenging. In this work, we introduce a weak temporal supervision strategy that leverages additional temporal observations of existing single-temporal datasets, without requiring any new annotations. Specifically, we extend single-date remote sensing datasets with new observations acquired at different times and train a change detection model by assuming that real bi-temporal pairs mostly contain no change, while pairing images from different locations to generate change examples. To handle the inherent noise in these weak labels, we employ an object-aware change map generation and an iterative refinement process. We validate our approach on extended versions of the FLAIR and IAILD aerial datasets, showing competitive zero-shot and low-data regime performance. Lastly, we showcase results over large areas in France, highlighting the scalability potential of our method.|[2601.02126](http://arxiv.org/abs/2601.02126)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Attachment Anchors: A Novel Framework for Laparoscopic Grasping Point Prediction in Colorectal Surgery**|Dennis N. Schneider et.al.|Accurate grasping point prediction is a key challenge for autonomous tissue manipulation in minimally invasive surgery, particularly in complex and variable procedures such as colorectal interventions. Due to their complexity and prolonged duration, colorectal procedures have been underrepresented in current research. At the same time, they pose a particularly interesting learning environment due to repetitive tissue manipulation, making them a promising entry point for autonomous, machine learning-driven support. Therefore, in this work, we introduce attachment anchors, a structured representation that encodes the local geometric and mechanical relationships between tissue and its anatomical attachments in colorectal surgery. This representation reduces uncertainty in grasping point prediction by normalizing surgical scenes into a consistent local reference frame. We demonstrate that attachment anchors can be predicted from laparoscopic images and incorporated into a grasping framework based on machine learning. Experiments on a dataset of 90 colorectal surgeries demonstrate that attachment anchors improve grasping point prediction compared to image-only baselines. There are particularly strong gains in out-of-distribution settings, including unseen procedures and operating surgeons. These results suggest that attachment anchors are an effective intermediate representation for learning-based tissue manipulation in colorectal surgery.|[2602.17310](http://arxiv.org/abs/2602.17310)|
|**2026-11-09**|**SegMate: Asymmetric Attention-Based Lightweight Architecture for Efficient Multi-Organ Segmentation**|Andrei-Alexandru Bunea et.al.|State-of-the-art models for medical image segmentation achieve excellent accuracy, but require substantial computational resources, limiting deployment in typical resource-constrained clinical settings. We present SegMate, an efficient 2.5D framework that achieves state-of-the-art accuracy, while considerably reducing computational requirements. Our efficient design is the result of meticulously integrating asymmetric architectures, attention mechanisms, multi-scale feature fusion, slice-based positional conditioning, and multi-task optimization. We demonstrate the efficiency-accuracy trade-off of our framework across three modern backbones (EfficientNetV2-M, MambaOut-Tiny, FastViT-T12). We perform experiments on three datasets: TotalSegmentator, SegTHOR and AMOS22. Compared with the vanilla models, SegMate reduces computation (GFLOPs) by up to 2.5x and memory footprint (VRAM) by up to 2.1x, while generally registering performance gains of around 1%. On TotalSegmentator, we achieve a Dice score of 93.51% with only 295 MB peak GPU memory. Zero-shot cross-dataset evaluations on SegTHOR and AMOS22 demonstrate strong generalization, with Dice scores of up to 86.85% and 89.35%, respectively. SegMate variants in this study empower single GPU setups to process up to 10,000 volumes per hour with just 74 J per volume using batch inference, enabling population-scale CT processing on commodity hardware. We release our open-source code at https://github.com/andreibunea99/SegMate.|[2602.23903](http://arxiv.org/abs/2602.23903)|
|**2026-11-09**|**SimpliHuMoN: Simplifying Human Motion Prediction**|Aadya Agrawal et.al.|Human motion prediction combines the tasks of trajectory forecasting and human pose prediction. For each of the two tasks, specialized models have been developed. Combining these models for holistic human motion prediction is non-trivial, and recent methods have struggled to compete on established benchmarks for individual tasks. To address this, we propose a simple yet effective transformer-based model for human motion prediction. The model employs a stack of self-attention modules to effectively capture both spatial dependencies within a pose and temporal relationships across a motion sequence. This simple, streamlined, end-to-end model is sufficiently versatile to handle pose-only, trajectory-only, and combined prediction tasks without task-specific modifications. We demonstrate that this approach achieves state-of-the-art results across all tasks through extensive experiments on a wide range of benchmark datasets, including Human3.6M, AMASS, ETH-UCY, and 3DPW.|[2603.04399](http://arxiv.org/abs/2603.04399)|
|**2026-11-09**|**HyDRA: Hybrid Domain-Aware Robust Architecture for Heterogeneous Collaborative Perception**|Minwoo Song et.al.|Collaborative perception assumes that participating agents share compatible feature representations, yet this assumption often fails in practice. Beyond explicit differences in backbone architecture or sensor modality, independently trained models can exhibit latent domain shifts and cause naive feature fusion to contaminate rather than complement each agent's representation. To address this, we propose HyDRA (Hybrid Domain-Aware Robust Architecture), a training-free framework that uses a lightweight domain classifier to identify whether each agent shares a compatible feature domain, and adaptively select the fusion strategy. Homogeneous agents are combined through intermediate fusion to maximize information gain, whereas heterogeneous agents are routed to late fusion to avoid contamination. To further mitigate localization errors inherent in late fusion, HyDRA incorporates anchor-guided pose graph optimization. Extensive experiments show that HyDRA matches state-of-the-art methods under architecture heterogeneity, and improves overall AP@0.7 over late fusion by nearly 25% under latent domain heterogeneity.|[2603.23975](http://arxiv.org/abs/2603.23975)|
|**2026-11-09**|**PR3DICTR: A modular AI framework for medical 3D image-based detection and outcome prediction**|Daniel C. MacRae et.al.|Three-dimensional medical image data and computer-aided decision making, particularly using deep learning, are becoming increasingly important in the medical field. To aid in these developments we introduce PR3DICTR: Platform for Research in 3D Image Classification and sTandardised tRaining. Built using community-standard distributions (PyTorch and MONAI), PR3DICTR provides an open-access, flexible and convenient framework for prediction model development, with an explicit focus on classification using three-dimensional medical image data. By combining modular design principles and standardization, it aims to alleviate developmental burden whilst retaining adjustability. It provides users with a wealth of pre-established functionality, for instance in model architecture design options, hyper-parameter solutions and training methodologies, but still gives users the opportunity and freedom to ``plug in'' their own solutions or modules. PR3DICTR can be applied to any binary or event-based three-dimensional classification task and can work with as little as two lines of code.|[2604.03203](http://arxiv.org/abs/2604.03203)|
|**2026-11-09**|**BLaFA: Bridging Language to 3D Functional Affordances for Dexterous Robotic Grasping in Tool Use**|Fan Yang et.al.|In industrial assembly, equipment maintenance, and everyday tool-use scenarios, robots need not only to grasp objects stably but also to establish task-appropriate contact configurations according to task intentions and tool functionality. However, existing methods lack explicit mappings from open-vocabulary instructions to 3D functional contact structures and dexterous grasp configurations, limiting robots' adaptability to diverse tools and functional tasks. To address this limitation, we propose BLaFA (Bridging Language to 3D Functional Affordances for Dexterous Robotic Grasping), a structured framework for language-driven functional dexterous grasping. First, a Knowledge-guided Language Parsing (KLP) module leverages structured knowledge to transform natural-language instructions into semantic sextuples of manipulation constraints. Second, a 3D Gaussian Splatting based hierarchical semantic representation and a Triangular Functional Point Localization (TriLocation) module are developed to localize 3D functional contact keypoints under task-tool topology constraints. Finally, a 3D Keypoint Grasp Matrix Transformation (KGT3D+) module maps the localized keypoints and semantic constraints to executable wrist poses and finger-level joint and force commands. Evaluations on 100 open-vocabulary language instructions, 14 task-tool combinations covering four functional intents, and real-world robotic experiments in representative scenes demonstrate that BLaFA can effectively translate natural-language instructions into task-aligned 3D functional contact structures and corresponding dexterous grasps, thereby enabling language-driven robotic tool use across diverse tasks. The source code will be made publicly available. Code will be publicly available at https://github.com/PopeyePxx/BLaFA.|[2604.08410](http://arxiv.org/abs/2604.08410)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Deep Learning-Based Tracking and Lineage Reconstruction of Ligament Breakup**|Vrushank Ahire et.al.|The disintegration of liquid sheets into ligaments and droplets involves highly transient, multi-scale dynamics that are complex to quantify from high-speed shadowgraphy images. Identification of droplets, ligaments, and blobs formed by the breakup of a liquid sheet, and their tracking from frame to frame, is an essential part of spray analysis. Analysis of such processes poses a fundamental challenge for conventional multi-object tracking frameworks, which enforce strict one-to-one temporal associations and cannot represent one-to-many fragmentation events. We present a two-stage deep learning framework that detects droplets and ligaments and identifies their temporal relationships across successive frames, modeling ligament deformation, fragmentation, and parent-child lineage. In the first stage, a Faster R-CNN detector with a ResNet-50 backbone and Feature Pyramid Network classifies ligaments and droplets in recordings of two impinging Carbopol gel jets. A morphology-preserving synthetic data generation strategy enriches the training set without physically implausible configurations, achieving a held-out test F1 score of up to 0.872 across fourteen original-to-synthetic data configurations, with robustness tested on different atomization conditions and liquid compositions. In the second stage, a Transformer-augmented multilayer perceptron classifies inter-frame object associations into continuation, fragmentation (one-to-many), and non-association using physics-informed geometric features. |**2026-11-09**|**Source-Free Domain Adaptation with Vision-Language Prior**|Song Tang et.al.|Source-Free Domain Adaptation (SFDA) seeks to adapt a source model, which is pre-trained on a supervised source domain, for a target domain, with only access to unlabeled target training data. Relying on pseudo labeling and/or auxiliary supervision, conventional methods are inevitably error-prone. To mitigate this limitation, in this work we for the first time explore the potentials of off-the-shelf vision-language (ViL) multimodal models (e.g., CLIP) with rich whilst heterogeneous knowledge. We find that directly applying the ViL model to the target domain in a zero-shot fashion is unsatisfactory, as it is not specialized for this particular task but largely generic. To make it task-specific, we propose a novel DIFO++ approach. Specifically, DIFO++ alternates between two steps during adaptation: (i) Customizing the ViL model by maximizing the mutual information with the target model in a prompt learning manner, (ii) Distilling the knowledge of this customized ViL model to the target model, centering on gap region reduction. During progressive knowledge adaptation, we first identify and focus on the gap region, where enclosed features are entangled and class-ambiguous, as it often captures richer task-specific semantics. Reliable pseudo-labels are then generated by fusing predictions from the target and ViL models, supported by a memory mechanism. Finally, gap region reduction is guided by category attention and predictive consistency for semantic alignment, complemented by referenced entropy minimization to suppress uncertainty. Extensive experiments show that DIFO++ significantly outperforms the state-of-the-art alternatives. Our code and data are available at https://github.com/tntek/DIFO-Plus.|[2604.17748](http://arxiv.org/abs/2604.17748)|
|**2026-11-09**|**From Points to Targets: Online Foreground Affinity and Background Learning for Infrared Small Target Detection**|Qiancheng Zhou et.al.|Infrared small-target detection must identify a few faint target pixels in extensive background clutter while keeping false alarms low. Under single-point supervision, each target has only one labeled foreground pixel with unknown spatial extent during training, whereas the detector must recover complete targets from the infrared image without any point guidance at inference. First, for online foreground affinity learning, each annotated target point serves as a feature anchor, and local spatial proximity and intensity similarity weight the feature alignment between the point and surrounding pixels, guiding target-related representations without hard boundary labels. Second, for online background learning, we exclude the ambiguous neighborhood around each target point and select high-response false alarms outside this region as active negatives. Averaging the background loss over this active set keeps sparse target-like clutter influential as easy background pixels are suppressed. Jointly optimizing these objectives in a single stage updates the detector directly without generating or refreshing persistent pseudo-masks. Across three public datasets and the combined SIRST3 benchmark, OABL improves detection probability by 1.06--7.82 percentage points over matched PAL training at a false-alarm area rate of $2 \times 10^{-5}$ and avoids PAL's multi-stage full-pool label updates.|[2605.00722](http://arxiv.org/abs/2605.00722)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Local Intrinsic Dimension Unveils Hallucinations in Diffusion Models**|Bartlomiej Sobieski et.al.|Diffusion models are prone to generating structural hallucinations - samples that match the statistical properties of the training data yet defy underlying structural rules, resulting in anomalies like hands with more than five fingers. |**2026-11-09**|**RigidFormer: Learning Rigid Dynamics using Transformers**|Zhiyang Dou et.al.|Learning-based simulation of multi-object rigid-body dynamics remains difficult because contact is discontinuous and errors compound over long horizons. |**2026-11-09**|**PGID: Progressive Guided Inversion and Denoising for Robust Watermark Detection**|Minh Quoc Duong et.al.|With the proliferation of AI-generated images, digital watermarking has become an essential safeguard for protecting intellectual property and mitigating malicious exploitation. |**2026-11-09**|**What Concepts Lie Within? Detecting and Suppressing Risky Content in Diffusion Transformers**|Chenyu Zhang et.al.|The rise of text-to-image~(T2I) models has increasingly raised concerns regarding the generation of risky content, such as sexual, violent, and copyright-protected images, highlighting the need for effective safeguards within the models themselves.Although existing methods have been proposed to eliminate risky concepts from T2I models, they mainly focus on the erasure of risk-associated textual or latent representations, limiting their ability to defend against adversarial prompts without explicit risk words, especially in recent Diffusion-Transformer-based T2I models. |**2026-11-09**|**Hide to See: Reasoning-prefix Masking for Visual-anchored Thinking in VLM Distillation**|Seonghoon Yu et.al.|Recent think-answer approaches in VLMs, such as Qwen3-VL-Thinking, boost reasoning performance by leveraging intermediate thinking steps before the final answer, but their computational cost becomes substantial, especially for larger VLMs. To distill such capabilities into compact think-answer VLMs, a primary objective is to improve the student's ability to utilize visual evidence throughout its reasoning trace, as long think-answer traces suffer from visual forgetting issues. |**2026-11-09**|**No One Knows the State of the Art in Geospatial Foundation Models**|Isaac Corley et.al.|Geospatial foundation models (GFMs) have been proposed as generalizable backbones for disaster response, land-cover mapping, food-security monitoring, and other high-stakes Earth-observation tasks. |**2026-11-09**|**Beyond MMSE: Enhancing PnP Restoration with ProxiMAP**|Kenta Vert et.al.|Plug-and-Play (PnP) methods for imaging inverse problems replace the proximal operator of the prior, a maximum a posteriori (MAP) denoiser, with a pretrained minimum mean squared error (MMSE) denoiser. |**2026-11-09**|**DiRotQ: Rotation-Aware Quantization for 4-bit Diffusion Transformers**|Sayeh Sharify et.al.|Diffusion Transformers (DiTs) achieve state-of-the-art image generation quality but incur substantial memory and computational costs at inference. |**2026-11-09**|**Rethinking the State Update Gate for Long-Sequence Recurrent 3D Reconstruction**|Kejun Ren et.al.|Streaming 3D reconstruction under a strict constant-memory budget hinges on how the recurrent state is updated as the stream evolves. |**2026-11-09**|**Probability-Conserving Flow Guidance**|Parsa Esmati et.al.|Diffusion and flow-based generative models dominate visual synthesis, with guidance aligning samples to user input and improving perceptual quality. |**2026-11-09**|**Oracle Supervision Transfers for Hyperparameter Prediction in Model-Based Image Denoising**|Jianmin Liao et.al.|Hyperparameter prediction is a critical practical bottleneck for model-based image denoisers, ranging from classical TV/TGV variational solvers to modern diffusion-based models such as DiffPIR. |**2026-11-09**|**SliceWorld: A Predictive and Controllable World-State Model for CT Report Generation**|Yuanhe Tian et.al.|CT report generation (CTRG) requires models to summarize three-dimensional anatomical context and pathological findings from hundreds of axial slices. |**2026-11-09**|**No Safe Dose: How Training Data Drives Unsafe Image Generation**|Felix Friedrich et.al.|Text-to-image models are trained on web-scale data that inevitably contains unsafe images, yet whether this contamination directly drives unsafe generation has not been tested under controlled conditions. |**2026-11-09**|**EGOSTREAM: A Diagnostic Benchmark for Streaming Episodic Memory in Egocentric Vision**|Rosario Forte et.al.|Continuous episodic memory is a core capability for autonomous agents operating in dynamic, real-world environments, yet current streaming video benchmarks provide limited tools for diagnosing what models remember and for how long. |**2026-11-09**|**Text-Driven Fusion for Infrared and Visible Images: Achieving Image Scene Adaptation on Hyperbolic Space**|Huan Kang et.al.|Infrared and visible image fusion aims to integrate complementary modalities, while existing Euclidean methods impose rigid distance metrics that distort multi-modal interactions and parent-to-child semantic hierarchies. |**2026-11-09**|**Beyond Benchmarks: Continuous Edge Inference for Traffic Sign Recognition on Embedded Vehicular Hardware**|Aditya Mishra et.al.|Traffic sign recognition (TSR) systems deployed on embedded vehicular hardware face deployment effects that image-based benchmark evaluation does not capture, including frame-to-frame prediction instability in streaming video and load-dependent throughput under sustained operation. |**2026-11-09**|**TACO: Towards Task-Consistent Open-Vocabulary Adaptation in Video Recognition**|Minghao Zhu et.al.|Adapting CLIP for open-vocabulary video recognition necessitates a delicate balance between newly acquired video knowledge and the pretrained generalization. |**2026-11-09**|**Emergence of a Shared Canonical Object Frame from In-the-Wild Videos**|Tom Fischer et.al.|Comparing object orientations and positions across different instances requires their poses to be expressed in a shared canonical frame. |**2026-11-09**|**Steering Training Trajectories in Diffusion Representation Learning**|Rajat Rasal et.al.|Across training runs of a diffusion autoencoder, we observe similar image reconstruction quality, but substantially different levels of disentanglement in the learned representations. |**2026-11-09**|**LPM: Industrial-Scale Generative Video Restoration**|Bichuan Zhu et.al.|We present the Large Processing Model (LPM), a diffusion-based generative framework for photorealistic video restoration under complex, in-the-wild degradations. |**2026-11-09**|**Latent Riemannian Flow Matching for Geometry-Grounded 3D Foundation Models**|Lisa Weijler et.al.|Geometric foundation models such as the Visual Geometry Grounded Transformer (VGGT) provide strong 3D priors from unposed images, but operate deterministically and cannot generate plausible geometry beyond what the observations directly support. |**2026-11-09**|**Accuracy potential of visual localization exploiting high-end street-level imagery**|Jonas Meyer et.al.|Many applications, such as surveying, autonomous navigation, robotics and augmented reality, require accurate pose information with respect to a reference frame. |**2026-11-09**|**Hold-Out Self-Validation Cannot Certify the Accuracy of Photogrammetric Reconstructions**|Behnam Asadi et.al.|Photogrammetric reconstructions used for inspection are often delivered without an independent accuracy check. Taking the correspondences from the verified matches lowered the score below 0.95 in 14 of 18 corrupted runs, against 4 for the standard form and 10 when its failed resections count as errors, with all seven nominal runs at 1.00. Mapping the block again without the withheld images also responds, but mapping the same corrupted data twice without withholding gave a similar disagreement, so the signal is mainly that a corrupted reconstruction is not reproducible. Neither test can see an error that every reconstruction repeats, and neither can replace check points.|[2607.24852](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.24852)|
|**2026-11-09**|**What to Preserve, Where to Adapt: A Depth-Wise Analysis of Forgetting in Continual Gynecological Image Segmentation**|Amal Saqib et.al.|The clinical management of gynecological diseases often relies on medical imaging for diagnosis, treatment planning, and follow-up. Under a shared learning rate, adaptation near the bottleneck largely preserves previous-task performance but provides limited current-task learning, whereas broader adaptation improves current-task performance but sharply increases forgetting. This trade-off persists even when the average change in trainable backbone parameters is approximately comparable. |**2026-11-09**|**MEDR: Query-Independent Frame Selection via Multi-Signal Event Modeling and Dynamic Rescoring**|Xinlei Pu et.al.|Frame selection is a fundamental component of multimodal large language models, enabling long videos to be processed under limited visual-token and computational budgets. Dynamic Rescoring then iteratively reevaluates each candidate relative to the current selected set, updating its score according to frame-level signal strength, additional event coverage, and balanced temporal coverage. |**2026-11-09**|**GenScale: A Benchmark for Relative Object Scale in Image Generation and Editing**|Lingxiao Li et.al.|Modern image generation and editing systems can produce photorealistic, prompt-aligned images, but still often render familiar objects at implausible relative sizes. |**2026-11-09**|**Beyond Landmark Extraction: A Framework for Robust Geometric Feature Evaluation in Structured Image Classification**|Saravana Mauree et.al.|Much of the literature on structured image recognition has disproportionately focused on the comparison of classification algorithms. Rather than investigating which classifier performs best, this paper instead asks: what should a classifier know before it ever makes a prediction? |**2026-11-09**|**Exemplar: Few-Shot Biomedical Segmentation That Fits in Seconds**|Michal Pr\r{u}\v{s}ek et.al.|Segmenting a new microscopy dataset usually means training a deep network on its annotations, and training again for every added image. |**2026-11-09**|**VideoGen-Agent: Reinforcing Video Generation Agents**|Binxu Li et.al.|Recent advances in video generative models have enabled high-fidelity, temporally coherent video generation. However, these models often struggle to satisfy prompts requiring specialized knowledge, specific identities, physical consistency, or ordered events. The agent coordinates augmentation, generation, and verification tools through multi-turn interactions, using the prompt and intermediate observations to guide its decisions. Project page: https://andyca111.github.io/VideoGen_Agent/|[2609.24997](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.24997)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Panoptic Scene Program Diffusion Transformer**|Chika Maduabuchi et.al.|Modern text-to-image models produce high-fidelity images but still struggle with compositional prompts that require instance identity, attribute ownership, counting, spatial ordering, and role-sensitive relations. |**2026-11-09**|**PhyProbe: Rethinking Physical Consistency Evaluation in Generated Videos**|Max Ku et.al.|Evaluating the physical consistency of generated videos remains a fundamental challenge. |**2026-11-09**|**When Predicting Nothing Beats SAM 3: Revisiting Evaluation in Video Object Segmentation**|Jihwan Hong et.al.|Video Object Segmentation (VOS) in complex and long videos is increasingly important for real-world applications, where target objects often appear only intermittently within long temporal horizons. Project page: https://aidaslab.github.io/FaVOS.|[2610.02946](http://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02946)|
|**2026-11-09**|**ELMA: Benchmarking Anaphoric Compositions for Long-Term Text-to-Motion Generation**|Shun Kato et.al.|Text-guided long-term human motion generation is essential for applications such as continuously operating digital avatars. |**2026-11-09**|**Sparse All-Layer Connector for Domain Generalised Semantic Segmentation**|Ruoyu Guo et.al.|Domain generalised semantic segmentation (DGSS) has recently benefited from combining multiple foundation models, such as vision-language and vision foundation models, to leverage their diverse representations for generalisation. The code is available at https://github.com/RuoyuGuo/SALC.|[2610.15660](http://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15660)|
|**2026-11-09**|**FlowSim: Rendering-Free Closed-Loop Simulation for Evaluating End-to-End Driving Systems**|Hanfeng Wu et.al.|Closed-loop evaluation of end-to-end (E2E) driving systems typically relies on generating sensor observations and re-processing them through the perception encoder, introducing substantial computational cost and a sim2real gap between simulated and real sensor observations. Our project page is https://hanfeng-wu.github.io/flowsim_page/.|[2610.18030](http://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18030)|
|**2026-11-09**|**I2V-DETACH: Source Grounding Detachment for Unauthorized Image-to-Video Generation**|Chanhui Lee et.al.|Recent diffusion models have advanced image-conditioned video generation, but their accessibility raises risks of unauthorized manipulation. |**2026-11-09**|**TriTD: Tri-Partite Trajectory-Distribution Distillation for Real-Time Autoregressive Video Generation**|Jia Li et.al.|Autoregressive (AR) video diffusion has emerged as a leading paradigm for efficient video generation, yet its practical deployment remains bottlenecked by the trade-off between generation quality and inference efficiency. |**2026-11-09**|**ReCoG: Relational Concept Graph for Training-Free Personalization**|Changseon Yu et.al.|Training-free personalization adapts a frozen vision-language model (VLM) to recognize user-defined concepts from only a few exemplars by retrieving concept entries at inference time. Project Page: https://csy-98.github.io/ReCoG/|[2610.31282](http://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31282)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Whispers from the Neighbors: Rethinking Caption Consensus as Inference-Time Evidence for Vision-Language Retrieval**|Khang Nhat Hoang Vo et.al.|An image is rarely described only once. We show that this advantage arises because the correct candidate's supports are built differently from those of the negative candidates, with set size and query removal each contributing about half, and that under a fixed support index the residual reduces to a reweighting of what the captions already provide. The code is available at: https://github.com/nhatkhangcs/clip_consensus|[2610.33798](http://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33798)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Semantic Concept Steering Breaks the Explanation Drift Loop in Continual Learning**|Yehonatan Elisha et.al.|Catastrophic forgetting in continual learning (CL) manifests not only as accuracy degradation but also as \emph{explanation drift}: models maintain predictive accuracy while silently shifting attention from diagnostic features to spurious correlations. We identify that our concept-based objective, combined with approximate LRP relevance conservation, induces a \emph{crowding-out effect}: since concept and background regions compete for the relevance of the same prediction, enforcing high relevance within concept regions implicitly suppresses spurious background correlations without explicit penalization. \method does not require human-annotated concept sets, as it employs a fully automated concept discovery and spatial grounding. |**2026-11-09**|**DEQ-Stereo: Deep Euilibrium Video Stereo Matching via Adaptive Convergence**|Kiljoon Han et.al.|Iterative refinement models have significantly improved stereo matching accuracy, but their rigid, fixed update schedules incur heavy computational costs and a strict trade-off between speed and accuracy. |**2026-11-09**|**Dynamic Merging Mutually-Distilled Multi-View Networks for RGB-Event-based Representation Learning**|Futian Wang et.al.|Multi-modal fusion algorithms combining conventional frame-based cameras and event cameras have recently attracted substantial research attention. |**2026-11-09**|**MoRE-GS: Motion-Regime Experts for Future Gaussian Splatting**|Shima Salehi et.al.|Future dynamic novel-view synthesis requires extrapolating multiple motion regimes within the same scene. |**2026-11-09**|**A Straight Line Is Not a Camera Pose: Auditing Short-Clip Pose Benchmarks**|Xinyi Shi et.al.|The absolute trajectory error (ATE) is the standard measure of camera-motion accuracy, from SLAM to feed-forward 3D reconstruction. On the common Sintel protocol, an image-free constant-velocity line has a lower mean ATE than 10 of 11 recent models re-run with official code and than 85 of 110 published methods. On Lightspeed it beats 5 of 9 models and 10 of 11 published methods, while on TUM, ScanNet, DyCheck and ADT every model beats it. Two properties of ATE's alignment explain this. First, it maps any moving constant-velocity prediction onto the line that best fits the true path, so the line scores the path's regression residual. A residual signal-to-noise ratio (SNR) criterion tells when moving a prediction towards its constant-velocity fit lowers ATE. With the alignment's first-order refit, it accounts for the outcome of all but one of 4010 model-clip evaluations, including all 504 on three benchmarks registered before any model ran. The true path's straightness flags Sintel and Lightspeed. Second, the alignment ignores where cameras look: rotating a trajectory by 30 degrees against its orientations leaves ATE unchanged yet worsens the static-point reprojection of every model on Sintel, TUM and ScanNet, and the line reprojects worst on Sintel by geometric mean. Fitting the alignment's rotation to the predicted orientations gives pose-anchored ATE (PA-ATE), a one-function change at about ATE's cost (37 vs. 34 $\mu$s per clip, and 61 vs. 66 $\mu$s with the rotation error). It is never below ATE and, as we prove, equals the translational relative pose error over all frame pairs divided by $\sqrt{2}$ when the orientations agree. |**2026-11-09**|**PixelBench: Can LLMs Understand and Generate Images Pixel by Pixel?**|Haoquan Zhang et.al.|Large language models (LLMs) can generate visual assets through text-based formats such as SVG and CAD, but these symbolic representations describe images using high-level primitives rather than explicit raster values. |**2026-11-09**|**Lookahead Prediction-Guided Adaptive Visual Refocusing for Interleaved Multimodal Reasoning**|Mengzhao Wang et.al.|Interleaved multimodal chain-of-thought reasoning improves the visual reasoning capabilities of multimodal large language models by dynamically incorporating visual evidence during reasoning. However, existing methods primarily select evidence from historical context. When the reasoning focus shifts, history-guided evidence may remain focused on prior regions and fail to cover the visual information required by the next step. We refer to this lag between visual evidence and subsequent reasoning demands as Temporal Evidence Myopia. To address this issue, we propose Lookahead Prediction-guided Adaptive Visual Refocusing (LPAVR), a training-free test-time framework. LPAVR transforms Multi-Token Prediction (MTP) from a decoding acceleration mechanism into a lookahead signal for anticipating upcoming reasoning intent. During MTP decoding, the draft head generates multiple future tokens, while the target model verifies them in parallel and retains the longest prefix consistent with its decoding trajectory. This target-verified prefix contains near-future semantics, which LPAVR uses to construct lookahead-guided visual evidence and assess whether history-guided evidence covers the indicated regions. If the coverage is sufficient, the history-guided evidence is retained; otherwise, LPAVR switches to the lookahead-guided evidence. This better aligns the visual context with upcoming reasoning and improves consistency between draft predictions and target-model decoding. Experiments on multiple multimodal reasoning benchmarks show that LPAVR improves both reasoning accuracy and the acceptance rates of MTP speculative tokens, jointly enhancing visual reasoning and speculative decoding efficiency.|[2611.02780](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02780)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Mesh-Agnostic Refinement Models for Latent Space Encoding of 3D Human Body Shapes**|Chaima Zouaoui et.al.|Parametric human body models such as the Skinned Multi-Person Linear (SMPL) model provide compact representations of human shape and pose, but their reconstructions remain affected by both errors in estimated latent parameters and limitations in the decoder's expressive capacity. We propose a framework for refining these reconstructions by exploiting the geometric discrepancy between an initial decoded surface and a target mesh, without requiring vertex correspondences between them. The framework comprises three complementary approaches. Latent-VaRefine uses varifold distance gradients to correct the estimated latent code while preserving the original parametric representation. Vertex-VaRefine processes multiscale varifold-gradient features with a DiffusionNet backbone to predict vertex displacements in a canonical pose, yielding an approximately pose-equivariant refinement pipeline. ElaOpt directly optimizes surface deformations under elastic regularization and normal direction constraints. On AMASS, Vertex-VaRefine reduces the mean vertex-to-vertex error of OmniFit reconstructions from 1.181 to 0.342 cm, a 71.0 percent reduction, while combining Latent-VaRefine with ElaOpt reduces the asymmetric Chamfer distance of ArtEq reconstructions from 1.681 to 0.717 cm, a 57.3 percent reduction. Evaluations with SMPL-H and SMPL-X demonstrate the framework's adaptability to different body models, while additional experiments on remeshed targets and raw scans assess its robustness to changes in discretization and geometry beyond the parametric model. These results highlight the complementary strengths of latent space correction, learned vertex refinement, and elastic optimization, offering different trade-offs between preserving the model parameterization and improving geometric fidelity.|[2611.03285](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03285)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Structured Visual Landscape: Generating Preferred Representations in Biological and Artificial Neural Networks**|Yusong Zhao et.al.|Understanding how neurons respond to visual stimulus is an important question in both deep learning and neuroscience. It has significant implications for enhancing the interpretability of black-box artificial neural networks and for understanding the visual representation in the human brain. We propose a structured visual representations landscape and design an activation-based prior that allows us to effectively regularize the landscape with activations from brain regions or neural network units. Our model Vis-Lens integrates a variational autoencoder and a diffusion model into an image generation framework. It enables the generation of natural, realistic preferred images by directly modifying the activation-regularized latent codes without per-image iterative optimization. We demonstrate the effectiveness of our framework on both artificial neural networks and human visual cortex using functional magnetic resonance imaging (fMRI) data. Our framework improves activation modulation and image realism compared with a recent state-of-the-art method for generating preferred visual representations of these neural targets. Code is available at https://github.com/NeuroAIHub/Vis-Lens.|[2611.03597](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03597)|
|**2026-11-09**|**An Agentic Framework for Quality-Aware and Scenario-Adaptive Image Compression**|Zhenghao Chen et.al.|Despite decades of progress, image codecs remain difficult to adapt to diverse real-world compression scenarios for two main reasons. First, most image codecs are optimized for a specific objective and require substantial redevelopment when the compression goal changes. Second, they typically assume clean inputs, whereas sensor-captured images often contain degradations that alter rate-quality behavior. To address these challenges, we propose Practical Image Coding Agents (PICA), a multi-agent framework that adapts existing codecs to diverse user requirements and potentially degraded inputs. PICA consists of four collaborative agents: a Perception agent that interprets user intent and analyzes image content, an Improvement agent that jointly considers bitrate and reconstruction quality to guide pre-coding restoration, a Coding agent that refines quality-control settings from global to regional levels, and an After-coding improvement agent that addresses residual degradations and compression-induced artifacts through restoration guided solely by image quality. To enable codec-agnostic regional adaptation, we introduce Agentic ROI Coding (AROIC), which treats the codec as a black box and allows the agent to automatically schedule, execute, evaluate, and reflect ROI coding plans without architectural modifications or codec-specific retraining. We integrate these four agents with a Scenario-Guided Quality-Rate (SGQR) evaluation strategy to enable more effective scenario-adaptive compression across heterogeneous codecs. Comprehensive experiments on clean, noisy, and blurred images across standard, perceptual, and machine-vision compression scenarios demonstrate that PICA consistently enhances the compression performance of both the conventional H.266/VVC codec and the neural Cheng2020 codec, outperforming competing compression methods on the Kodak, CLIC2020, and HEVC datasets.|[2611.04960](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04960)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Route First, See Later: Enabling Vision Agents to Reason Scientifically by Separating Routing Policy from Action Spaces**|Shubhabrata Mukherjee et.al.|Scientific image-analysis workflows require agents to select among diverse capabilities like description, detection, counting, and segmentation. Current multi-tool architectures conflate intent interpretation with visual execution, masking whether a failure arises from flawed reasoning (misrouting) or failed perception (invalid tool execution). This lack of auditability renders agents unreliable for high-stakes scientific automation. We introduce ZETA, an extensible architecture that operationalizes the decoupling of routing policy from action execution. ZETA resolves scientific intent before invoking specialized visual tools, treating measurement as a deterministic operation grounded in explicit geometry rather than generative output. This architecture exposes the "black box" of agent decision-making, allowing routing correctness and downstream execution to be evaluated as independent failure modes. To our knowledge, we provide the first controlled study in scientific vision-agent routing that isolates explicit routing-policy specification while holding the legal action interface fixed. Evaluation on a zero-shot benchmark of 51 scientific-intent queries shows that explicit routing semantics can substantially improve routing accuracy, particularly at semantically adjacent action boundaries, although improvements vary across models. For Qwen 3.8, explicit routing-policy specification improves segmentation routing accuracy from 6.2% to 75.0% and detection routing accuracy from 60.0% to 100%. ZETA's execution traces make routing decisions and downstream artifacts separately observable, providing a basis for distinguishing intent-selection errors from visual execution failures. ZETA establishes the necessary infrastructure to isolate execution errors from interpretive ones, a critical requirement for deploying reliable scientific vision agents.|[2611.06760](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06760)|
|**2026-11-09**|**From Pixels to Actions -- Towards Native Vision-Language-Action Policies**|Weichen Fan et.al.|Vision-language-action models are typically built on a simple premise: perception is first compressed by visual encoders, and action reasoning begins only afterwards. Yet manipulation often hinges on precisely the visual signals most vulnerable to this abstraction, e.g., local geometry, boundaries, depth, and subtle state changes. This raises a fundamental question: should action policies inherit a fixed visual endpoint, or directly access evolving visual computation? We introduce NEO-VLA, a structurally encoder-free vision-language-action framework. Images are tokenized by lightweight patch convolutional layers and evolve jointly with language throughout the backbone, allowing a flow-matching action expert to draw from instruction-conditioned visual representations at different depths. This simple change reveals a consistent pattern. Native architectures outperform encoder-based counterparts under controlled comparisons; full fine-tuning reduces but does not erase the gap, and additional scale alone cannot recover it. More strikingly, layer-wise probing shows that the action-critical signals often peak well before the final visual representation. Across diverse manipulation settings and distribution shifts, NEO-VLA consequently achieves stronger and more robust control. Together, these results suggest that embodied control benefits from access to visual representations across the computational hierarchy, rather than a fixed encoder output.|[2611.06918](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06918)|
|**2026-11-09**|**TraCE: Trajectory-Aware Concepts for Explainable Video Understanding**|Wooil Lee et.al.|Video understanding models often rely on motion, yet their explanations can obscure which motion sources support a prediction. We identify motion-source under-specification as a limitation of existing video XAI: region-centric explanations can be source-ambiguous, while motion vocabularies restricted to single-person pose sequences are source-incomplete. We propose TraCE (Trajectory-Aware Concepts for Explainable Video Understanding), a concept-based framework that discovers source-flexible motion concepts by clustering optical-flow trajectories from training videos. These concepts represent dynamic evidence from people, objects, and interactions, while object and scene concepts account for static context. The resulting typed trajectory/object/scene interface supports class prediction for action recognition and question-conditioned concept matching for video question answering (VQA). We evaluate TraCE across five random seeds on six benchmarks: NTU RGB+D Mutual, Chiral SSv2, UCF-101, KTH, TOMATO, and STAR. Relative to non-interpretable baselines, TraCE maintains competitive task performance, with an average accuracy drop of 1.0 points for action recognition and a nominal gain of 0.4 points for VQA. Motion perturbation and concept-removal tests provide complementary evidence of directional semantic alignment and decision faithfulness, while sample-level interventions illustrate targeted model debugging. Our project page is available at https://leewooil.github.io/TraCE_project_page/.|[2611.07009](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07009)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Training-Free Predictive Spatio-Temporal Token Pruning for Few-Step Autoregressive Video Diffusion**|Jia Li et.al.|Few-step autoregressive (AR) video diffusion models generate videos chunk by chunk and have made efficient video generation practical. However, further accelerating these models toward real-time generation remains challenging, as few-step distillation leaves little cross-step redundancy for existing training-free caching methods to exploit. We revisit this regime and identify two token-level redundancy patterns: cross-frame redundancy from static content shared across adjacent frames, and cross-step redundancy from tokens that converge early during denoising. Crucially, we find that early clean estimates along the model's own denoising trajectory provide an effective online signal for locating both patterns, requiring no retraining, auxiliary predictor, or reference video. Based on these findings, we propose PreSTP (Predictive Spatio-Temporal Token Pruning), a training-free framework that constructs a unified token-wise redundancy mask from cross-frame similarity and cross-step convergence. Tokens marked as redundant skip subsequent computation under a consistent last-computed-value restoration rule, and a content-adaptive mask reuse strategy further reduces dense probing cost. On 5-second generation, PreSTP achieves 1.52x-3.20x speedups and a better efficiency-fidelity trade-off than coarse-grained caching baselines such as TeaCache and FlowCache. On 30-second generation, it reaches up to 1.96x speedup while improving VBench-Long and suppressing long-horizon drift. Notably, it continues to reduce inference cost even in the extreme one-step regime, where cross-step redundancy vanishes entirely and coarse step-wise caching is no longer applicable.|[2611.07551](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07551)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Generator-Aligned Reward Modeling with Target-Aware Bounded Optimization for Visual Generation**|Ziqiao Weng et.al.|Post-training aims to enhance desired attributes in visual generative models while preserving their general generation capabilities. Reward-guided post-training faces two challenges: real training data may mismatch the generator's output distribution, and attribute supervision may be confounded by unrelated content. Unbounded reward maximization can also cause overoptimization and degrade generation quality. We propose a framework combining Generator-Aligned Reward Modeling (GARM) and Target-Aware Bounded Optimization (TABO). GARM mitigates distribution mismatch through generator-support matching and reduces content confounding using content-matched real contrasts, yielding generator-relevant, attribute-focused reward supervision. TABO maps the learned reward to a smooth, upper-bounded function calibrated from real target-positive examples. This transformation is independent of the optimization algorithm and supports both direct reward-gradient and policy-based optimization. We further introduce Target-Aware GRPO (TA-GRPO), an adaptation of Flow-GRPO that combines fixed stage-wise advantage scaling, a generator-to-target curriculum, and timestep-balanced credit assignment for flow-based video optimization. Experiments show that GARM improves target-attribute expression while maintaining competitive generation quality. TABO better balances attribute enhancement and visual quality than unconstrained reward maximization, while TA-GRPO improves target enhancement over vanilla Flow-GRPO and reduces unintended attribute leakage on neutral prompts. These results highlight the importance of generator-aligned reward supervision and controlled, data-calibrated optimization for targeted visual generation.|[2611.07648](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07648)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Spatiotemporal Dynamics-Aware Industrial Anomaly Detection and Reasoning**|S-Wei Chen et.al.|Industrial Anomaly Detection (IAD) is a long-established problem that aims to detect defects in industrial products during manufacturing for quality control. Most existing studies focus on static visual patterns as the basis for detection. However, in real manufacturing environments, many anomalies are dynamics-based, such as irregular movements that cannot be perceived from static cues alone. We observe that even recent Multimodal Large Language Model (MLLM)-based approaches fall short in identifying such dynamic anomalies due to limited temporal modeling capability. To address this, we introduce MUSTARD: MUltimodal SpatioTemporal Anomaly Reasoning and Detection, a framework that equips MLLMs with fine-grained spatiotemporal dynamics awareness for detecting and reasoning about dynamics-based defects. MUSTARD introduces optical-flow-guided keyframe selection and augmented modalities to capture critical motion irregularities through local motion patterns and global dynamic evolution. Its normal pattern-based reasoning pipeline requires only a few normal reference samples as the detection basis, avoiding any model updates. This enables efficient adaptation across various products and provides high decision interpretability. MUSTARD is the first MLLM-based framework for dynamics-based IAD. Experiments demonstrate its superior performance in detecting spatiotemporal anomalies and providing plausible explanations. Our code is available at https://github.com/racercode/Mustard|[2611.08080](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08080)|
|**2026-11-09**|**A Theoretical Bridge Between Long-Tailed Recognition and Continual Learning**|Mahdiyar Molahasani et.al.|We theoretically and empirically establish a previously unexplored connection between Long-Tailed Recognition (LTR) and Continual Learning (CL). Specifically, we show that training on a long-tailed dataset with imbalance factor $\mathrm{IF}$ drives model parameters into an $O(1/\sqrt{\mathrm{IF}})$ neighborhood of the dominant-class solution, under both uniform and exponentially decaying cardinality distributions. Building on this result, we establish an objective-level reformulation of LTR as a sequential learning problem and prove that the CL objective upper-bounds the balanced LTR loss up to a constant determined by the residual Head loss. Motivated by this connection, we introduce Continual Learning for Long-Tailed Recognition (CLTR), a principled framework that leverages standard off-the-shelf CL methods to sequentially learn Head and Tail classes while mitigating catastrophic forgetting. Extensive experiments on CIFAR100-LT, CIFAR10-LT, ImageNet-LT, and Caltech256 validate our theoretical predictions and demonstrate strong performance across diverse LTR benchmarks. In the foundation-model regime, CLTR with DualPrompt on a CLIP backbone outperforms standard adaptation baselines and is competitive with specialized methods using external semantic supervision. Our work bridges LTR and CL both theoretically and empirically, providing a principled approach for addressing long-tailed learning using standard CL strategies.|[2306.13275](http://arxiv.org/abs/2306.13275)|
|**2026-11-09**|**From Images to Signals: Are Large Vision Models Useful for Time Series Analysis?**|Ziming Zhao et.al.|Transformer-based models have gained increasing attention in time series research, driving interest in Large Language Models (LLMs) and foundation models for time series analysis. As the field moves toward multi-modality, Large Vision Models (LVMs) are emerging as a promising direction. In the past, the effectiveness of Transformer and LLMs in time series has been debated. When it comes to LVMs, a similar question arises: are LVMs truely useful for time series analysis? To address it, we design and conduct the first principled study involving 4 LVMs, 8 imaging methods, 18 datasets and 26 baselines across both high-level (classification) and low-level (forecasting) tasks, with extensive ablation analysis. Our findings indicate LVMs are indeed useful for time series classification but face challenges in forecasting. Although effective, the contemporary best LVM forecasters are limited to specific types of LVMs and imaging methods, exhibit a bias toward forecasting periods, and have limited ability to utilize long look-back windows. We hope our findings could serve as a cornerstone for future research on LVM- and multimodal-based solutions to different time series tasks.|[2505.24030](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24030)|
|**2026-11-09**|**A Cytology Dataset for Early Detection of Oral Squamous Cell Carcinoma**|Garima Jain et.al.|Oral squamous cell carcinoma OSCC is a major global health burden, particularly in several regions across Asia, Africa, and South America, where it accounts for a significant proportion of cancer cases. Early detection dramatically improves outcomes, with stage I cancers achieving up to 90 percent survival. However, traditional diagnosis based on histopathology has limited accessibility in low-resource settings because it is invasive, resource-intensive, and reliant on expert pathologists. On the other hand, oral cytology of brush biopsy offers a minimally invasive and lower cost alternative, provided that the remaining challenges, inter observer variability and unavailability of expert pathologists can be addressed using artificial intelligence. Development and validation of robust AI solutions requires access to large, labeled, and multi-source datasets to train high capacity models that generalize across domain shifts. We introduce the first large and multicenter oral cytology dataset, comprising annotated slides stained with Papanicolaou(PAP) and May-Grunwald-Giemsa(MGG) protocols, collected from ten tertiary medical centers in India. The dataset is labeled and annotated by expert pathologists for cellular anomaly classification and detection, is designed to advance AI driven diagnostic methods. By filling the gap in publicly available oral cytology datasets, this resource aims to enhance automated detection, reduce diagnostic errors, and improve early OSCC diagnosis in resource-constrained settings, ultimately contributing to reduced mortality and better patient outcomes worldwide.|[2506.09661](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.09661)|
|**2026-11-09**|**prNet: Data-Driven Phase Retrieval via Stochastic Refinement**|Mehmet Onurcan Kaya et.al.|Phase retrieval is an ill-posed inverse problem in which existing methods often struggle to balance reconstruction fidelity and perceptual quality. We propose a novel Langevin-based framework for phase retrieval that bridges model-driven and data-driven approaches by combining stochastic posterior sampling, learned denoising, and model-based updates to better navigate the perception-distortion tradeoff. The framework consists of three progressively more advanced variants. The first, prNet-Small, provides a theoretically grounded and lightweight pipeline that integrates Langevin dynamics with learned noise and denoising processes and alternating projection-based updates. Building upon this foundation, prNet-Large exploits parallel sampling of diverse reconstructions and aggregates them to approximate the MMSE solution, significantly improving distortion metrics. Finally, prNet-Large-Adversarial enhances perceptual quality by replacing simple averaging with an adversarially trained aggregation network while preserving reconstruction fidelity. In addition, we incorporate test time augmentation to further improve the performance. Extensive experiments demonstrate that the proposed framework consistently outperforms classical, deep learning-based, and diffusion-based baselines across multiple benchmarks in both reconstruction fidelity and perceptual quality. The source code and trained models are available at https://github.com/METU-SPACE-Lab/prNet-for-Phase-Retrieval|[2507.09608](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.09608)|
|**2026-11-09**|**VLD: Visual Learned Distance as an Interface for Reinforcement Learning Navigation**|Lazar Milikic et.al.|Training end-to-end policies from image data to directly predict navigation actions for robotic systems has proven inherently difficult. Existing approaches often suffer from either the sim-to-real gap during policy transfer or limited action-labeled training data. To address this problem, we introduce Visual Learned Distance (VLD), a scalable framework for image-goal navigation that decouples perception learning from policy learning. We first train a self-supervised distance-to-goal predictor on about 3,000 hours of internet-scale and simulated video, providing a distance signal that a reinforcement learning (RL) policy can minimize. The RL policy is trained separately in simulation using privileged geometric distances perturbed by GeoNoise, a learned noise model that reproduces the viewpoint- and overlap-dependent errors of the trained distance predictor. At deployment, the policy consumes VLD predictions zero-shot, without retraining, inheriting ``where to go'' from large-scale visual training while retaining the robust navigation behaviors learned in simulation. This error modeling is key to the transfer: with VLD inputs, GeoNoise-trained policies reach $58.7\%$ success on HM3D and $76.2\%$ on Gibson, whereas policies trained with generic temporally correlated noise collapse to $4.9\%$ and $11.5\%$.
