|**2026-11-09**|**Temporal Pair Consistency for Flow Matching**|Chika Maduabuchi et.al.|Flow-matching-style generative models learn time-dependent vector fields, but standard objectives supervise each timestep independently, leaving shared-path temporal structure unused. We introduce Temporal Pair Consistency (TPC), a simple training-time objective that couples velocity predictions at paired timesteps along the same probability path. TPC leaves the architecture, probability path, sampler, and inference-time computation unchanged. Under matched backbones, training budgets, solvers, and numbers of function evaluations, TPC improves sample quality across flow matching, OT-CFM, rectified flow, DiT-style backbones, and MeanFlow. Mechanism diagnostics show lower measured gradient variance, higher paired-gradient correlation, and reduced temporal roughness, while random and local pairing controls are substantially weaker. Higher-resolution ImageNet experiments further show consistent gains for DiT-XL/2 at 256 and 512 resolution and for MeanFlow at 256 resolution, all at matched inference cost.|[2602.04908](http://arxiv.org/abs/2602.04908)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Preconditioned Flow Matching**|Shadab Ahamed et.al.|Flow matching (FM) learns vector fields by regressing stochastic velocity targets along intermediate distributions $p_t$. We identify a geometric optimization bottleneck in this regression problem: when the covariance $\Sigma_t$ of $p_t$ is ill-conditioned, gradient-based training rapidly fits high-variance directions while making slow progress along low-variance ones. In an exactly solvable Gaussian setting, we prove that the excess risk is weighted by $\Sigma_t$, and that both gradient descent and stochastic gradient descent inherit condition-number-dependent convergence. We then extend the analysis to Gaussian mixtures, showing that multimodality does not average away this effect; instead, the slowest and worst-conditioned component can control optimization. Motivated by this analysis, we propose $\textit{preconditioned flow matching}$, a precondition-then-match framework that transforms the target distribution into a more isotropic representation, trains the main flow in the transformed space, and maps generated samples back through the inverse transformation. We show theoretically that preconditioning reshapes the intermediate FM path and improves its conditioning. Across controlled Gaussian and Gaussian-mixture experiments, latent MNIST and other high resolution image datasets up to $512{\times}512$ resolution, preconditioning improves path-conditioning diagnostics, low-eigenvalue recovery, FID, MMD, precision, and recall. Compute-matched baselines and preconditioner-quality ablations further show that the gains are not explained merely by additional preconditioner parameters, but by improved geometry of the downstream flow matching problem. GitHub: https://github.com/ahxmeds/preconditioned-flow-matching.git.|[2603.02337](http://arxiv.org/abs/2603.02337)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Signals Are Present, Predictions Fail: Probing Diagram Representations in Large Vision-Language Models**|Haruto Yoshida et.al.|Large vision-language models (LVLMs) demonstrate strong performance on diagram understanding benchmarks, yet they still struggle to understand relationships between elements, particularly those represented by directed edges between nodes. To investigate the causes of this limitation, we probe the internal representations of LVLMs using a synthetic diagram dataset based on directed graphs. Our linear probing experiments reveal that signals about nodes and edges are linearly decodable from the hidden states of LVLMs, even for aspects that LVLMs struggle with in VQA, such as edge direction and connectivity between nodes. These results reveal a gap between internal signals and model outputs. We further perform causal interventions on the model's hidden states, showing that, for some aspects, the signals identified through probing causally contribute to the model's final predictions. These findings also hold on flowcharts, not only on synthetic diagrams.|[2603.02865](http://arxiv.org/abs/2603.02865)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Dual-Rate Diffusion: Accelerating diffusion models with an interleaved heavy-light network**|Grigory Bartosh et.al.|Diffusion models achieve state-of-the-art generative performance but suffer from high computational costs during inference due to the repeated evaluation of a heavy neural network. In this work, we propose Dual-Rate Diffusion, a method to accelerate sampling by interleaving the execution of a heavy high-capacity context encoder and a light efficient denoising model. The context encoder is evaluated sparsely to extract high-dimensional features, which are effectively reused by the light denoising model at every step to refine the sample efficiently. This approach significantly accelerates inference without compromising sample quality. On ImageNet benchmarks, Dual-Rate Diffusion matches the performance of standard baselines while reducing computational cost by a factor of $2$-$4$. Furthermore, we demonstrate that our method is compatible with distillation techniques, such as Moment Matching Distillation, enabling further efficiency gains in few-step generation.|[2605.18190](http://arxiv.org/abs/2605.18190)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Platonic Representations in the Human Brain: Unsupervised Recovery of Universal Geometry**|Pablo Marcos-Manch\'on et.al.|The Strong Platonic Representation Hypothesis suggests that representational convergence in artificial neural networks can be harnessed constructively: embeddings can be translated across models through a universal latent space without paired data. Using fMRI data from the Natural Scenes Dataset, we propose a self-supervised encoder that learns subject-specific embeddings from brain data alone by exploiting repeated stimulus presentations. We show that these independently learned spaces can be translated across subjects using unsupervised orthogonal rotations, without paired cross-subject samples or intermediate model representations. Synchronizing pairwise rotations into a single shared latent space further improves cross-subject retrieval. These results provide evidence for a shared geometry in learned representations of human visual responses: subject-specific fMRI embeddings can be aligned across individuals within a common coordinate system.|[2605.20496](http://arxiv.org/abs/2605.20496)|
|**2026-11-09**|**From Senses to Decisions: The Information Flow of Auditory and Visual Perception in Multimodal LLMs**|Wish Suharitdamrong et.al.|Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) can listen and see, but how do audio and visual signals actually travel through the network to shape an answer? Despite their growing role in research and real-world applications, the internal pathways through which audio and visual tokens influence the final prediction remain poorly understood. In this study, we examine audio-visual information flow inside Audio-Visual Large Language Models (AVLLMs), tracing how AVLLMs route, utilize, and integrate audio and visual information across two input configurations, audio-visual video and multiple interleaved audio-visual items. We find that for audio-visual video, AVLLMs follow the sequential information flow pathway established for VLMs and VideoLLMs, with audio and visual contribution flowing along this pathway in proportion to the task's reliance on each modality. In settings with multiple interleaved audio-visual items, this routing shifts to different parallel streams. Furthermore, we demonstrate that audio-visual and other token types can be discarded once their information is transferred to LLM, with minimal impact on the model's prediction or even slight improvement, generalizing across multiple tasks and datasets, enabling more efficient inference. These findings hold across multiple models and scales, Qwen2.5-Omni and Video-SALMONN2 Plus at 3B and 7B scales, leading to hypotheses on why these flow structures emerge. Together, these results deliver the first coherent picture of how AVLLMs orchestrate sound and sight inside the network and lay the groundwork for the next wave of interpretability, design, and efficiency advances in audio-visual and broader MLLMs.|[2606.10147](http://arxiv.org/abs/2606.10147)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Diffusion-Steering-Based MPC with a JEPA World Action Model**|Shashank Hegde et.al.|World action models (WAMs) predict actions and future observations, typically from a reconstruction-based representation that carries noisy, redundant information which can complicate downstream predictions. We introduce LeWAM, a bidirectional transformer for forward, backward, inverse dynamics and policy prediction, on a decoder-free JEPA latent trained end-to-end through all four modes. We see the following benefits: 1) Alignment: linear probes read robot and object state from LeWAM's latent better than from a regular Le World Model (a forward-only JEPA world model), while the latent ignores visual distractors as well as LeWM does and far better than a reconstruction-based WAM. 2) Acting: Closed-loop evaluations of LeWAM match a regular flow-matching policy trained on the same encoder at matched size, while also providing a world model. 3) Planning: Sampling raw actions when planning with WAMs lets MPC exploit dynamics-model inaccuracies; planning in the noise space of the policy head instead improves the closed-loop performance of these WAMs.|[2610.12407](http://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12407)|
|**2026-11-09**|**MemWM: Associative Memory-Enhanced Latent World Models for Long-Horizon Planning**|Yuwei Sun et.al.|World models trained via latent dynamics offer a scalable path for embodied planning, yet existing methods suffer from representational collapse, computational overhead, and limited long-horizon capability. We introduce Memory World Model (MemWM), an associative memory-enhanced latent world model that augments JEPA-style prediction with a content-addressable memory bank. MemWM learns high-level historical representations directly from observations and leverages them to condition predictions via adaptive layer normalization, complementing low-level action-conditioned dynamics within a shared latent space. On PushT, TwoRoom, Reacher, and OGBCube, MemWM attains an average success rate of 89.9%, matching DINO-WM (89.3%) while using roughly 4$\times$ fewer trainable parameters and no pretrained encoder, and improving over LeWM by 4.1%. |**2026-11-09**|**Learnable Hodge-Laplacian Driven Mixture of Experts for Steady-PDE Surrogates on Coarse Unstructured Meshes**|Stavros Nousias et.al.|Surrogate models approximate the solutions of partial differential equations given a certain geometry and boundary conditions. Traditional transformer-based approaches employ a single mixing mechanism to the whole solution. although the solution fields exhibit variations across multiple spatial scales. We propose a mixture of transformer experts that combines a routing mechanism with multiscale supervision. Specifically, the individual experts are routed through a controller that utilizes learnable Laplacian matrices to compute weights that are subsequently used to define a weighted sum of the experts for the output feature vectors at each point of the domain. Subsequently, a decoder receives the weighted sum of the experts output and predicts the solution fields. During training, the reference fields are decomposed into components at different spatial scales by repeated smoothing with a mesh Laplacian. We evaluate our approach on the official AirFRANS dataset using remeshed solution domains leading to unstructured triangular meshes of $4.9$k vertices. The experimental outcomes reveal lower mean relative $L_2$ error and interior mean squared errors among the compared models of this study.|[2610.20585](http://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20585)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Erase the Mark, Preserve the Motion: Temporally Consistent Removal of Video Watermarks**|Yifan Liao et.al.|Recent advances in video generation have increased the demand for reliable watermarking for copyright protection, yet the robustness of video watermarks against removal attacks remains underexplored. Most watermark-removal methods originate from the image domain: signal optimization injects imperceptible pixel-domain perturbations, whereas generative resynthesis produces a visually similar instance through diffusion synthesis. When extended to videos, the former introduces temporally varying noise that causes visible flickering, while the latter regenerates fine-grained structures inconsistently across frames, leading to structural motion inconsistency. To evaluate video watermark robustness, we propose PoseRegen, a pose-consistent reconstruction framework that removes watermarks while preserving the original content and motion. PoseRegen first applies a temporally shared viewpoint transformation to disrupt watermark-bearing spatial patterns and then jointly reconstructs the transformed sequence with a pose-conditioned video model. A frozen video DiT processes the complete sequence with its reference-video latent, and full-sequence Flow Matching supervision keeps identity and background consistent across frames. To further preserve motion structure, we introduce a lightweight Pose-Conditioned Motion Consistency Adapter that adapts the frozen backbone to temporally ordered pose guidance and constrains body-part trajectories throughout reconstruction. Experiments on eleven video watermarking schemes show that PoseRegen reduces the average bit accuracy to 0.505, near random guessing, while outperforming the strongest baseline by 9.34% on motion-related VBench metrics. These results reveal a practical vulnerability of current video watermarks and highlight the need to evaluate robustness jointly with temporal and motion quality.|[2610.22457](http://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22457)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Self-Distilling Future Video Representations for World Action Models**|Huiwon Jang et.al.|World Action Models (WAMs) have emerged as a promising approach to learning generalist robot policies using rich spatiotemporal priors from pre-trained video diffusion models. A recent line of WAMs improves inference efficiency by conditioning a separate action decoder on intermediate future video representations extracted from a single denoising step, avoiding costly iterative denoising required for video generation. This design makes action prediction depend directly on the quality of these future representations. In this paper, we introduce Future REpresentation learning via self-Distillation with temporal context (FRED), a framework that trains WAMs to improve the quality of future video representations from noise in a single denoising step. Specifically, FRED uses self-distillation to predict future representations from noise, using representations extracted from ground-truth future observations as targets. We further condition the video model on historical observations, providing temporal context for predicting future representations better. Across diverse real-world manipulation scenarios, FRED consistently outperforms competing WAMs. It achieves particularly large gains on long-horizon and out-of-domain tasks under zero-shot environment generalization, and substantially improves human-to-robot skill transfer, reaching 50.0% task success on tasks absent from robot training data and demonstrated only in human videos, compared with 19.4% for the best competing WAM.|[2610.30320](http://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30320)|
|**2026-11-09**|**DecoyCount: Evaluating Visual-Reasoning Misalignment in Counting**|Qiuyi (Richard) et.al.|Visual counting benchmarks report whether vision-language models produce the correct number, but not whether errors arise from perception or reasoning. We introduce DecoyCount, a benchmark built from 1,586 real-world images with designed decoys across seven mechanisms, along with paired prompts: a neutral question, a matched alignment cue, and a novel multi-hop, multi-image question that situates the same counting problem in a realistic scenario. Using this design, we find that counting failures are induced partly by visual misalignment: models can execute the intended counting process when cue-guided but fail to select it unaided, and we term this difference in accuracy the reasoning-alignment gap. Despite being highly capable on general reasoning, frontier models remain below 50% accuracy on our benchmark, while alignment gaps range from 12.1-22.1 percentage points; strikingly, we observe that more capable models, greater thinking budgets, and agentic processes often induce larger gaps. Because humans may bring context that models lack, we test whether textual underspecification contributes to misalignment using a scenario-based multi-hop condition without explicit hinting, but this added context does not improve aggregate performance. Our analysis concludes that stronger general reasoning does not by itself generalize to aligned visual reasoning, leaving misalignment with human visual expectations as a substantial component of the remaining deficit. DecoyCount and the evaluation code are released at https://github.com/elorian-ai/decoycount.|[2610.34855](http://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34855)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Seeing What Matters, Verifying What Pays: Load-Adaptive Speculative Decoding for Vision-Language Models**|Hong Nie et.al.|Speculative decoding accelerates vision-language model (VLM) inference without altering the output distribution, yet two factors limit its gains. Existing drafters either lack the image evidence required for visually grounded tokens or pay to condition on every visual token, although a short draft block typically depends on only a few image regions. Moreover, the value of verifying additional candidates varies with GPU load, so no fixed verification structure is efficient across serving conditions. We present Uni-LaSpec, a block-parallel speculative decoding framework that addresses both factors. To improve draft quality, Uni-LaSpec reuses the verifier's own attention: the query retained from the preceding verification ranks the cached, int8-quantized visual keys, and a cross-layer vote selects a few visual tokens that the drafter fuses through gated cross-attention. Acceptance-aware training further weights each draft position by its contribution to the accepted prefix. To allocate verification, a marginal-cost criterion selects the structure per request and per round: draft trees exploit idle verifier capacity under light load, whereas a dynamic head truncates linear drafts under heavy load. Across six VLMs from three model families and 17 text and multimodal benchmarks, selecting eight visual tokens retains at least 98.6% of the acceptance obtained from the full image at sub-millisecond selection cost. With greedy decoding at concurrency one, Uni-LaSpec achieves up to 5.11x text and 3.24x multimodal throughput over autoregressive decoding. Across six load levels and three GPU types, it outperforms autoregressive decoding in every setting and matches or exceeds the best fixed verification strategy in 123 of 144 settings; under bursty arrivals, it reduces mean end-to-end latency by 47.6% relative to linear verification.|[2611.01617](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01617)|
|**2026-11-09**|**A Novel Conditional Adversarial Diffusion Model for High-Fidelity OCT-to-OCT Image Translation and Precise Anti-VEGF Therapy Response Prediction in Diabetic Macular Edema**|Reza Rasti et.al.|Diabetic macular edema (DME) is a leading cause of severe vision loss among working-age adults. Although intravitreal anti-vascular endothelial growth factor (anti-VEGF) injections currently serve as the first-line therapy for DME, a notable subset of patients fails to respond despite the substantial cost and clinical burden, suggesting they may benefit more from alternative interventions. Automated, noninvasive pre-treatment response assessment could therefore spare non-responders from the current trial-and-error treatment paradigm. In this study, we propose a novel conditional adversarial diffusion framework that synthesizes post-treatment optical coherence tomography (OCT) images from baseline scans, enabling the derivation of each patient's initial response to anti-VEGF therapy. The model was developed with subject-level 5-fold cross-validation on a US cohort (n = 127) and externally validated on an independent Iranian cohort (n = 44). Benchmarked against fourteen baseline and state-of-the-art translation models, the proposed model achieved the best treatment-response prediction performance among the methods evaluated for that task on both cohorts, with balanced accuracies of 85.7% and 93.0% on the US cohort and 85.2% and 95.3% on the external Iranian cohort at the 10% and 20% central subfield thickness reduction thresholds, respectively. Ultimately, this framework offers a decision-support tool to help ophthalmologists anticipate initial anti-VEGF outcomes in DME prior to treatment initiation, toward personalized therapy selection. The source code will be made publicly available upon acceptance.|[2611.03958](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03958)|

|**2026-11-09**|**Enhancing Academic Advisement with Case-Based Reasoning**|Tanat Tonguthaisri et.al.|This research involved the study of an application of Case-Based Reasoning (CBR) in the field of academic advising. Academic advisement has been recognised as an important function of higher education. However, there is a widespread desire to improve the advisement services provided by many educational institutions (e.g., colleges and universities) around the world. In the last ten years, CBR expert systems have made important contributions in many domains by applying CBR methodology to a variety of areas of human problem solving. However, to date, expert advisement systems have not been successfully applied to academic advisement, which seems to be a very appropriate domain for this technology. Academic advising is an important and time consuming task, and different tools and techniques must be used to make it an effective and efficient process. This thesis describes the design, development, and validation of a prototype case-based academic advisement recommender system that supplements the conventional advising process, and could eventually evolve to replace human academic advisors when its full potential is realised.|[2611.08572](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08572)|
|**2026-11-09**|**NormGym: Evaluating Normative Competence through Social Interaction**|Binze Li et.al.|Normative competence, the ability to infer socially enforced expectations from interaction and adjust behavior accordingly, is essential for agents operating in unfamiliar human communities. Yet existing evaluations largely test whether agents can recognize or apply norms they may already know, rather than whether they can acquire previously unknown norms from social interaction. We introduce NormGym, an interactive benchmark for normative competence in which a newcomer must infer a hidden, arbitrary norm while pursuing its own task in a multi-agent community. Success requires distinguishing what is possible from what is permitted, inferring the norm from observed behavior and sanctions, and applying it in new situations. NormGym contains diverse norms and distinct environments, allowing systematic evaluation of current agents, including large language model (LLM) agents. We find that normative competence remains challenging: the strongest evaluated LLM achieves a joint task-and-compliance success rate of 55.3%. Our analysis reveals that models can sometimes revise their behavior following sanctions for their own actions, but struggle to use other agents' behavior and the sanctions they receive to infer the underlying norm. We further find that fine-tuning models may be insufficient to improve normative competence. These results suggest that current models lack a robust, transferable capability for acquiring normative knowledge through social interaction.|[2611.08613](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08613)|
|**2026-11-09**|**LLM-Based Feature Extraction for Multi-Criteria Inventory Classification**|Humza Akhtar et.al.|E-commerce supply chains generate large volumes of customer feedback and operational data that remain underutilized in inventory management. Traditional inventory classification techniques such as ABC analysis rely primarily on annual dollar usage, while multi-criteria inventory classification (MCIC) methods extend this but still depend on structured quantitative data and do not utilize unstructured information available in modern supply chains. This paper proposes an LLM-based feature extraction approach that transforms unstructured sources such as customer reviews into quantitative features using vector embeddings and LLM-generated evaluation criteria. The enriched feature set is incorporated into an MCIC framework based on the Hadi-Vencheh model. Experimental results show measurable reclassification effects when customer sentiment signals are included, demonstrating that integrating unstructured data into MCIC can enhance inventory prioritization.|[2611.08626](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08626)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Aspect-wise Pairwise Tournaments for Test-Time Selection**|Hannah Guan et.al.|Scaling test-time compute has proven highly effective at improving LLM reasoning. A popular method is best-of-n sampling, where a model generates many candidate solutions and one is selected. This motivates the question: how can we select the best candidate? When humans make choices, we find it more natural to compare two options than judge each in isolation. Additionally, when the options become complex, we like to judge the options one aspect at a time, such as comparing two job candidates on experience, communication, etc. In the test-time setting, candidate solutions should be judged in the same way. Here we introduce Aspect-wise Pairwise Tournaments (APT), which verifies by comparison and compares by aspect. Each matchup of a single elimination tournament is decided by a vote over multiple off-the-shelf aspect verifiers, each expressing a pairwise preference along its own aspect. Across five domains and three generator LLMs, APT improves over binary multi-aspect approval, self-consistency and reward model verification, with the largest gains on open-ended tasks. We find that both components matter; neither binary approval over the same aspects nor a generic pairwise judge at a matched budget matches APT. Because aspects are specified in natural language, APT also lets users steer selection toward properties they care about, better aligning with user preferences without needing to regenerate solutions. No component is trained, so APT transfers to new domains by simply rewriting prompts.|[2611.08640](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08640)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Decision Context: Persistent Behavioral Learning for AI Agents**|Nima Vahdat et.al.|LLM agents do not learn from corrections across sessions. A user fixes the same mistake at the start of every conversation. Existing agent memory systems primarily handle facts, such as preferences, entities, and conversational history, rather than procedural knowledge. We present Forge Decision Context (forge_dc), a system that captures behavioral signals from human corrections and self-assessed tool-call failures, compiles them into imperative RULE directives, and injects them at the decision points where they apply. We evaluate forge_dc alongside four predecessor approaches on DC-Bench, a new benchmark of 71 scenarios across nine behavioral dimensions, built from real executed agent traces in a deterministic mocked-tool environment. forge_dc reaches 95% compliance, up from 55% with naive retrieval, while injecting approximately 10 times fewer tokens per query. Across the five-stage progression, the strongest systems represent behavioral memory as explicit directives and deliver it at the decision points where it applies. The results identify representation, activation, and placement as central design choices for persistent behavioral learning.|[2611.08644](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08644)|
|**2026-11-09**|**IWIBench: Evaluating Gradient-Free Model Driven Intra-weight Injection**|Chris von Csefalvay et.al.|Intra-weight injection lets one model change another model's behaviour by editing its weights directly, without gradients. Such edits can range from altering lexical associations to enforcing output formats or updating factual knowledge. Despite growing interest in these approaches, there has been no systematic way to evaluate how well models perform them. We introduce IWIBench, which measures a large editor model's ability to modify the weights of a smaller target model (typically Gemma 3 4B IT) to achieve one of ten classical text-editing objectives while preserving its other capabilities. Editors are limited to four tools for reading and writing weights, with bounded tensor access and a fixed budget for generating and inspecting rollouts. A validator applies each proposed patch in a clean environment and compares the edited model against the unmodified target. Evaluating frontier closed- and open-weight models reveals sharply diverging capabilities, both across models and across tasks. A small number of models, most notably Astra and GPT-6.1 Sol, perform far ahead of the rest. The recency of this ability (little of it is evident in GPT-5.6 Sol, for instance), together with qualitative analysis, suggests it has been acquired through recent training. Tasks also vary widely: structured response resisted every attempt, and answer-length control yielded only one successful solution, whereas alias acquisition and recoding proved most amenable. Success in one task family correlated poorly with success in others, indicating an early and uneven capability frontier. We discuss strategies that editors repeatedly adopted and conclude with an outlook on intra-weight injection as an emerging but already potent approach to gradient-free behaviour modification.|[2611.08647](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08647)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Pdb or Not to Be: Comparing Debugging Interfaces With Pass $_{X}$@$k$**|Ryan Magnuson et.al.|Interactive debuggers are indispensable to human software development, yet coding agents overwhelmingly rely on edit-execute-observe loops, using common bash tools, test scripts, and print statements to debug via repeated shell commands. Stepping through a debugger provides live access to runtime state, which may enable different problem-solving capabilities. However, prior work typically introduces the debugger as an additional tool or through a new subagent, making its individual contribution difficult to isolate from the associated scaffold changes. To address this, we compare a debugger-only agent against a bash-only agent on all 500 SWE-bench Verified instances, holding the rest of the scaffold fixed. We evaluate both architectures on two models, GPT-OSS-120B and Nemotron-3-Ultra-550B, with three independent runs per architecture $\times$ model configuration. Crucially, to distinguish interface specialization from expanded coverage under repeated sampling, we introduce pass$_X$@$k$, a cross-agent pass@$k$ that provides an unbiased estimator of the probability that at least one of $k$ agents is successful. Although the bash and debugger agents solve partially disjoint subsets of the benchmark in any single pair of runs, our pass$_X$@$2$ estimator reveals that this is largely due to run-to-run variance rather than tool complementarity. Across both models, we find that two bash rollouts outperform the combination of one bash and one debugger rollout. Our findings highlight the importance of accounting for stochastic variance in evaluating agent and tool diversity, and we hope that pass$_X$@$k$ will serve as a useful framework for the community.|[2611.08666](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08666)|
|**2026-11-09**|**ChainFlow: Few-Step Latent Flow Matching for Speculative Decoding**|Selim Aktas et.al.|Speculative decoding has become a standard way to accelerate LLM inference: a lightweight drafter proposes tokens that the target verifies in one parallel pass. Existing drafters face trade-offs that limits their speedups: autoregressive drafters generate tokens one at a time, so drafting cost grows with draft length, while diffusion drafters generate tokens in parallel but predict each position independently, ignoring inter-token dependencies. We introduce ChainFlow, a new speculative decoding paradigm and the first flow-matching drafter conditioned on the target's hidden states. ChainFlow treats drafting as a short transport over hidden states, needing one or two flow steps instead of the many denoising steps of generation from noise. A variational autoencoder compresses these states so drafting cost barely grows with target size, lightweight feedback heads restore inter-token dependencies, and tree drafting raises acceptance. On Qwen3.5 models, at matched drafter size and training data, ChainFlow outperforms EAGLE-3 and DFlash in vLLM by 39% and 11%, respectively. These gains further widen as drafters are adapted to new languages sequentially. Our ablations cover drafter design and concurrent serving, and we open-source our code and drafter models.|[2611.08669](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08669)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Transporting the Past: An Optimal Transport View of Backtracking Counterfactuals**|Zhangyi Liu et.al.|We study backtracking counterfactual inference for Structural Causal Models (SCMs) through the lens of optimal transport. In SCMs, a factual observation generally induces a posterior distribution over exogenous variables, making counterfactual inference inherently distributional. We introduce two complementary frameworks that transport this posterior onto the set of exogenous configurations satisfying a desired counterfactual query. The first constructs a natural pointwise projection and is suitable when the transported exogenous distribution can be used directly. The second enforces mutual independence among counterfactual exogenous variables, so that the transported variables remain interpretable as genuine exogenous noises of the same SCM. We show that both frameworks are consistent with the original backtracking counterfactual principle, and we propose marginal, penalty-based, and hard-constrained solvers for finite-posterior settings. Synthetic experiments on non-bijective SCMs illustrate the geometry of the problem and reveal trade-offs among transport cost, counterfactual constraint satisfaction, and preservation of exogenous independence.|[2611.08682](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08682)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Cancellation Is Not Enough: What Retries Leave Behind in an Overloaded LLM Engine**|Xindan Zhang et.al.|Clients and proxies in front of an LLM engine retry calls that time out. After a transient overload, these retries can keep the engine from recovering even when calls arrive below its capacity. Since the engine may keep computing for clients that have left, propagating cancellation looks like the natural remedy. In this paper, we test it on vLLM with overload experiments on three open models, and with scheduler traces of one that follow each client attempt to the execution it creates in the engine. We find that with retries on, cancellation alone did not restore service in any run, and only 12-38% of the calls that arrived after the overload were delivered. The traces suggest why: retries that vLLM ran typically started prefill less than half a second before they timed out. Executions that vLLM later aborted took 79-85% of the prompt tokens it computed, and vLLM's prompt-token counter missed a third to half of all computed prompt tokens. A retry budget restored service by shedding retries but left many of the overload's calls undelivered. Request coalescing delivered every call from the overload onward, some late, unless cancellation had already aborted the running execution. Overload controls should thus be judged by the executions that retries add and the calls that clients receive.|[2611.08684](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08684)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Memory-Aware Multi-Agent Architecture with LLM-based Orchestration for Conversational Recommendation**|Emilly Pitman de Castro et.al.|Conversational recommender systems often treat user sessions in isolation, limiting their ability to capture how user intent evolves over time. While large language models (LLMs) have improved semantic understanding, they are typically used as isolated components, without addressing how memory, retrieval, and decision-making should be integrated at the system level. In this work, we propose a memory-aware multi-agent architecture for conversational recommendation, in which LLMs act as orchestration layers that coordinate intent refinement, query construction, and interaction flow. A key design principle is the integration of long-term memory as a first-class component, influencing both routing decisions and semantic query formation. We evaluate the system through cross-session memory experiments, multi-turn conversational simulations, and supervisor-driven interaction studies on the Amazon Reviews dataset. Results show that memory-enabled interactions recover up to 63\% of the gap toward explicit-query scenarios and prevent semantic degradation in extended dialogues. In addition, supervisory orchestration increases user acceptance by +28.9 percentage points, highlighting the importance of structured orchestration in guiding interaction and output quality. We also observe an exposure bottleneck between retrieved candidates and displayed recommendations. While LLM-reranking does not improve Hit@K, it consistently enhances semantic consistency and diversity. To capture these effects, we introduce the Semantic Consistency Score (SCS), a complementary evaluation measure. Overall, the results emphasize the importance of system-level design choices, suggesting that effective conversational recommendation benefits from explicit memory integration, orchestration, and evaluation beyond traditional ranking metrics.|[2611.08718](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08718)|
|**2026-11-09**|**AlterNet: Adaptive-Depth Mixture-of-Experts with Learned Halting for Elastic Inference**|Alexander Kastius et.al.|Most LLM architectures have fixed compute demands determined at training time. We propose a novel architecture that combines the Mixture-of-Experts architecture with shallow experts and a novel dynamic-depth system to make the extent of a forward pass dynamically adjustable. Our approach lets the model automatically determine the compute needed for a given task, paired with a system to scale it down after training without retraining when the execution environment demands faster or cheaper inference. To achieve this, we introduce an iterative computation mechanism, sequence-level depth-based routing steered by a halting mechanism within the experts, a depth encoding that lets the model track its progress in the chain, and a regularization mechanism that incentivizes the model to use allocated compute efficiently. We present initial benchmarks and discuss future research directions.|[2611.08741](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08741)|
|**2026-11-09**|**AttentionSwitch: Jailbreak Defense for LLMs via Joint Learning of Sparse Attention-Head Selection and Scaling**|Doniyorkhon Obidov et.al.|Large language models have achieved remarkable performance across a wide range of tasks, yet they remain susceptible to jailbreak attacks that circumvent safety mechanisms and elicit harmful or unintended outputs. Although safety can be improved through model fine-tuning, such approaches can be computationally expensive and may degrade previously acquired capabilities through catastrophic forgetting. Recent jailbreak defenses have therefore explored targeted interventions on specific model components associated with unsafe behavior. However, existing approaches typically decouple two inherently related decisions: where to intervene and how to modify the selected components. Such sequential optimization may be suboptimal because the optimal intervention location can depend on the intervention applied there, and vice versa. In this work, we introduce AttentionSwitch, the first jailbreak defense to jointly optimize intervention sites and strengths within a frozen LLM. Specifically, AttentionSwitch learns a sparse set of attention heads together with query-conditioned multiplicative scaling factors, allowing the outputs of the selected heads to be either attenuated or amplified without altering the directions of their representations. Across popular language models and jailbreak attacks, AttentionSwitch outperforms existing defenses, with particularly strong results when training data is scarce.|[2611.08752](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08752)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Debate to Deceive: Poisoning LLM's Fact-Checking via Game-Theoretic Multi-Agent Debate**|Liting Huang et.al.|Knowledge attacks on Large Language Models, such as manipulating fact-checking judgments, pose significant risks to AI reliability. Existing approaches -- adversarial suffix/template injection and single-agent self-reflection -- lack robustness across diverse defenses and are prone to confirmation bias and costly trial-and-error, respectively. We propose Debate-to-Deceive (D2D), a multi-agent debate framework grounded in human debate structure and rhetorical style, enabling agents to generate adversarial passages that are semantically coherent, rhetorically effective, and resistant to early confirmation bias. D2D formalizes the adversarial process as an evolutionary game in which agents adaptively evolve their rhetorical strategies (Logos, Pathos, Ethos) through competition. To manage the intractably large LLM output space, we decouple agent behavior into a strategic layer, which selects a rhetorical action from a compact strategy space via evolutionary-inspired learning dynamics, and a generation layer, where the LLM produces a concrete passage conditioned on that strategy and the debate context. On four fact-checking benchmarks (FEVER, HealthVer, CLIMATE-FEVER, and SciFact), D2D substantially outperforms existing attacks and evades most defenses.|[2611.08756](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08756)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Jacobian-Causal Memory Control for Persistent Memory in Language Model Systems**|Marvin Danig (Achiral Research) et.al.|Persistent-memory systems choose not only which records are retrieved but which prior information can alter current inference. We propose Jacobian-causal memory control (JCMC), which represents each authorized memory intervention by a predicted influence signature in a shared behavioral measurement space, selects interventions against a target effect and side-effect budget, and compares predicted with observed effects before retaining or rolling back the intervention. Under explicit smoothness and bounded-estimation assumptions, we prove a quadratic local-approximation bound, characterize the target effects reachable by a set of signatures, bound selection regret under signature error, and bound non-additive interactions between simultaneous memories. The paper reports no benchmark measurements; it defines a paired counterfactual estimator and an evaluation protocol comparing JCMC with relevance, graph, and policy-filtered retrieval baselines. The central claim is narrowed or falsified if those simpler baselines match effect targeting, side-effect control, and rollback utility under equal model, memory, policy, and cost budgets.|[2611.08779](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08779)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Aligning, Fast and Slow: A Control-Theoretic Oversight Framework for Long-Horizon Trajectories and Maintained Deployments**|Stephen Peng et.al.|AI agents can pass checks on individual tasks while retaining memory or code changes that alter their later behavior. We introduce a control-theoretic framework that couples a fast loop controlling proposed actions with a slow loop reviewing persistent changes and reassessing configurations after model or tool updates. When measurement errors and changes between checks are bounded and controllers can enforce their decisions, we derive a deployment-level bound and a rule for dividing review effort. In simulation, dividing a fixed review budget between both loops reduces mean maximum departure from intended behavior roughly twelvefold compared with review confined to individual runs. In coding stress tests with authored memories and five matched seeds, combined controls reduce mean severity on a 0-3 scale from 0.325 without gates to 0.117, resolving 33 of 120 issues versus 35 without gates; in trading stress tests, combined controls reduce mean shortfall in low-emission purchases from 0.892 without gates to 0.107. The overall framework helps developers and auditors compare and enforce oversight designs, while the agent studies test component controls rather than the full deployment-level guarantee.|[2611.08781](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08781)|
|**2026-11-09**|**The Matthews Correlation Coefficient: Fifty years**|Jesus S. Aguilar-Ruiz et.al.|Fifty years after its introduction, the Matthews Correlation Coefficient (MCC) remains central -and debated- in binary classification. This survey offers a comprehensive retrospective and an in-depth analysis of MCC, covering its historical development, mathematical foundations, and implications for practice. We clarify long-standing misconceptions, formalize its equivalences (to Pearson's correlation and to Chi2 in 2x2 tables), and examine its behavior under class imbalance. Beyond MCC, we develop analytical (derivatives) and graphical (contour plots) frameworks that generalize to any confusion-matrix-based metric. These frameworks expose structural properties -bias, robustness, sensitivity- that scalar summaries conceal, and they provide actionable guidance for selecting and interpreting performance measures in applications where reliability and fairness matter. Together, our results position MCC within a broader toolkit while clarifying when it is informative -and when alternatives may be preferable.|[2611.08799](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08799)|
|**2026-11-09**|**I Think, Therefore I Attack: Evaluating Thought Injection Attacks on Reasoning Agents**|Parth Illendula et.al.|Large Reasoning Models (LRMs) are one of the key components of current agentic systems. LRMs augment their output with an explicit chain-of-thought reasoning stage, introducing a new attack surface for thought injection, in which untrusted text resembling the model's own deliberation is fed back to the model. We present a two-part study of this attack, performed against the prevailing large, production class open-weight models (e.g., GLM-5.3, MiniMax-M2.5, Qwen3.8-Flash-Next). We first evaluate the effectiveness of static thought-injection prompts under different styles as well as tagged and untagged conditions and find that the effects of thinking tags and payload styles are all properties of the specific target model. In the second study, we exploit this model-specificity in a containerized agentic cyber range, using indirect injection prompts styled in the form of the target model's own reasoning traces to achieve attack success rates (ASR) up to 50% on operational objectives such as reverse-shell exfiltration and repository backdooring. We also find that the same malicious objective in standard prompt injection user voice never succeeds, and thinking tags suppress the agent's tendency to disclose the injection. Our results show that imitating a reasoning model's own thought style is effective for this style of attack, and that tag stripping is an unreliable defense.|[2611.08811](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08811)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Notwithstanding Anything to the Contrary, Ignore All Instructions: Measuring Prompt Injection Guards on Legal Contract Review Attacks**|Mark Heimann et.al.|Specialized prompt injection guards are deployed in front of document analysis systems, but no false positive rate has been published for any of them on legal contracts. We measure eleven open-weights guards on two corpora of legal contracts and find that existing guards struggle: document-level false positive rates are as high as 92.39%; two guards fall below chance on paired AUC; and a five-regex baseline is not Pareto-dominated by any guard in the panel. Controlled probes locate model-specific as well as general failure modes. Prompted LLMs resolve the tested failures, including a second threat category (unauthorized scope claims) on which every guard scores zero. A cascaded LLM solution, where the positives of a mid-tier model are verified by a frontier model, is 6.5x cheaper than using exclusively a frontier model and approaches its performance.|[2611.08834](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08834)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Solver-Admitted Metamorphic Testing of Learned Network Optimization Surrogates**|Vineet Jagadeesan Nair et.al.|Equivalent encodings of an optimization problem can produce different learned predictions. Interpreting these differences requires accounting for numerical error and multiple optima. We construct metamorphic tests for optimization solver surrogates with explicit eligibility conditions, output alignment, and numerical thresholds. Reference-solver agreement and independent constraint checks determine which sampled pairs enter the audit. We study alternating-current optimal power flow (AC-OPF), the traveling salesman problem (TSP), the capacitated vehicle routing problem (CVRP), and maximum independent set (MIS). Across five grids, GridSFM-Open violates each of 13 relations on at least one grid, and 12 by more than twice the threshold. Randomly initialized copies of the grid models fail nearly the same relations, so the failures do not require training. Source patches and input canonicalization remove selected discrepancies, but no intervention establishes preserved accuracy and feasibility within the declared margin on every grid. In routing, Policy Optimization with Multiple Optima (POMO) has a single-pass rotation/reflection violation rate that rises from 12.7% to 89.5% between 20 and 100 cities. Graph-Based Diffusion Solvers for Combinatorial Optimization (DIFUSCO) violates the MIS disjoint-union relation on 35.6% of draws. Post hoc normalization within connected components removes these violations on the tested graphs. These findings support reporting metamorphic consistency alongside optimality, feasibility, and accuracy, and validating repair quality separately from consistency.|[2611.08837](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08837)|
|**2026-11-09**|**When F1 Is Not Enough: Auditing Codebooks for LLM-Assisted Annotation**|Yuehong Cassandra Tai et.al.|Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used for text annotation in social science, but standard performance metrics do not explain why errors occur. Low F1 may reflect limitations of the annotation system, defects in the written codebook, or inconsistency in reference labels. We propose a pre-deployment codebook audit for LLM-assisted annotation. The audit uses class-specific metrics and confusion matrices to identify problematic classes and boundaries, structured document-level review to attribute error sources, and targeted codebook revision followed by held-out evaluation. We apply the audit to two published multi-class codebooks from distinct domains. In both applications, the audit identifies under-specified or conflicting coding rules, and targeted revisions reduce the diagnosed errors on held-out data. Analyses with two LLM annotators show that some problematic boundaries and codebook revisions transfer across models, whereas other revision effects are annotator-specific. |**2026-11-09**|**Learning Whom to Trust About the Unseen: Source Credibility and Reliability Transfer in Language Models**|Ruijia Guan et.al.|A source who has been right before deserves attention, but about what? We ask how far a demonstrated record of reliability travels in language models when the new claim cannot be checked, and whether the answer depends on the kind of claim. Three behavioral studies across six models approach the question from three angles. Study 1 adapts the Einstein-effect paradigm and finds that models, unlike the human sample, favor a spiritual leader over a scientist for the same obscure statement, and that the preference largely reverses when the statement is reworded in the vocabulary of physics. Study 2 adapts a paradigm in which a trusted expert calls a scientific or religious explanation unknown or a mystery; models reproduce the human domain asymmetry in exaggerated form. Study 3, the central test, gives two fictional informants different records on checkable river forecasts and then has them endorse matched claims about hidden natural and spiritual causes of the same village events. Most models carry the record to natural causes and barely at all to spiritual ones; one model transfers it weakly to either. Telling the model that the reliable informant forecasts by spiritual guidance rather than by measurement lowers that informant's influence on natural claims in every model and produces no comparable gain on spiritual claims in any. Demonstrated reliability thus has a scope in these models, and the account a source gives of how they came to be reliable can narrow it. Whether this reflects a judgment about relevance or the low prior credence of spiritual claims is the question the design leaves open, and we outline the experiment that would settle it.|[2611.08855](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08855)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Mind the Divergence: Divergence-Guided Reliability-Aware Fusion and Adaptive LLM Prompting for Conversational Emotion Recognition**|Jingwen Wang et.al.|Multimodal emotion recognition in conversations (MERC) integrates textual, acoustic, and visual cues to identify utterance-level emotions. However, existing approaches generally do not explicitly adapt multimodal processing to the degree of inter-modal predictive disagreement. Rather than identifying each source of inconsistency separately, we model their common consequence for emotion recognition as predictive disagreement among unimodal emotion distributions over the same emotion-label set. Such disagreement makes indiscriminate fusion prone to propagating unreliable or competing evidence. Inspired by the Brunswik Lens Model, we propose MiD-ERC, a divergence-centric framework that quantifies inter-modal predictive disagreement using a normalized divergence score and estimates sample-specific modality reliability. MiD-ERC uses these signals to coordinate divergence-guided cross-modal gating, divergence-modulated reliability weighting, and divergence-conditioned adaptive LLM prompting. The framework is trained end-to-end using only standard emotion annotations, without requiring additional conflict labels, auxiliary data construction, or external retrieval modules. |**2026-11-09**|**Rethinking Agent Trajectory Compression: A Workflow-Aware Perspective**|Qijia Zhuang et.al.|As long-horizon agents increasingly rely on multi-step tool interactions to accomplish complex tasks, their execution trajectories continuously accumulate structured outputs and intermediate artifacts, leading to growing context overhead. WAAC structurally models successful historical trajectories to extract reusable workflow compression experience and incorporates online backward dependency analysis to dynamically identify information retention requirements. Experiments on PinchBench and Odysseys demonstrate that WAAC achieves an overall score of 87.6 and a perfect completion rate of 19.0%, respectively, while reducing execution costs by 19.7% and 29.1% compared with the strongest baseline methods. Code and resources are available at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/waac-6527/.|[2611.08865](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08865)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Toward LLM Self-Play Under Data Scarcity: Effectiveness, Generalizability, and Trustworthiness**|Zhiyin Yu et.al.|The rapid development of large language models (LLMs) has been driven largely by scaling model size and human-curated training data, yet this paradigm is increasingly constrained by data scarcity and diminishing returns from human supervision. Self-play offers a promising pathway for autonomous data generation and improvement, but current frameworks remain unsatisfactory due to a lack of a data-centric perspective, limited cross-domain generalization, and growing trustworthiness and security concerns. In this paper, we argue that real-world LLM self-play systems should achieve effectiveness, generalizability, and trustworthiness under data scarcity. To address these challenges, we outline three blue-sky research directions for future self-play systems: (1) data-centric self-play for effective experience generation, (2) generalizable self-play for open-world and unverifiable tasks, and (3) trustworthy self-play for reliable self-evolution. We further discuss research opportunities and solutions within each direction. This paradigm shift will establish a sustainable and safe AI ecosystem by transforming finite human knowledge into inexhaustible high-quality experience through self-play architectures.|[2611.08908](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08908)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Frame: Form-Restricted Authoring with Model-Based Evaluation**|Nan Jiang et.al.|Many professional reports reuse the structure of prior editions while updating the content. We formalize this setting as form-restricted report generation: given a sample report and new grounding documents, generate a report that preserves the sample's form while accurately reflecting the new evidence. Across five LLMs, open-source models nearly match proprietary models on structure but lag on coverage and faithfulness; all models perform better on financial than academic reports, and performance declines with longer outputs and more complex grounding.|[2611.08921](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08921)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Glyph: An Interpretability Engine for AI-Driven Scientific Discovery in Clinical Data**|Robbie Holland et.al.|Automated scientific discovery has the potential to advance understanding of disease mechanisms and accelerate identification of early disease markers. Manual data annotation is infeasible at population scale, motivating methods that automatically extract pre-defined phenotypes in multimodal and unstructured clinical data. We identified 397 predictive concepts supported by 4,463 Bonferroni-significant associations. |**2026-11-09**|**Flex-RSI: Benchmarking Recursive Self-Improvement of Frontier Agents**|Jingdong Zhang et.al.|Frontier language models are increasingly deployed as agents, and how well an agent performs depends not only on its model but also on its harness: the prompts, tools, memory, and control loop through which the model acts. We introduce Flex-RSI, a benchmark for the recursive self-improvement of agents on vision and robotics tasks under a fixed budget. |**2026-11-09**|**PhysioWave-T: Token-Efficient Wavelet Foundation Models for Biosignals**|Yanlong Chen et.al.|We present PhysioWave-T, a token-efficient wavelet foundation model family for EEG, ECG, and sEMG. Across four EEG routes, scale folding cuts Transformer latency by 4.0--7.2x and peak memory by 6.8--12.7x over scale expansion without accuracy loss. Our EEG encoder leads 8 of 9 metrics against BENDR, BIOT, LaBraM, and EEGPT, and surpasses LUNA-L on wearable seizure detection with fewer parameters. Reliability-aware latent fusion (RALF) further integrates summary tokens from independently pre-trained encoders via sample-adaptive weights. On SeizeIT2, RALF raises event sensitivity at 11 false alarms/h from 61.4% to 74.4% and window AUPRC from 0.321 to 0.399 over the best unimodal baseline, outperforming weighted-logit and concatenation fusion across all variants. Code and models: https://github.com/ForeverBlue816/PhysioWave.|[2611.08969](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08969)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Rethinking Optimization Methods for Hierarchical Feature Selection**|Xinyuan Song et.al.|Rare-event forecasting often depends on interpretable conjunctions: a single keyword, location, or temporal cue may be weak, while their joint occurrence can identify a meaningful precursor. However, the resulting optimization problem is challenging, making optimization design critical to its practical performance. We give a unified diagnosis of four HTNL optimization families: original majorization-minimization (MM), variational quadratic form (VQF), alternating direction method of multipliers (ADMM), and weighted group lasso (WGL). Our analysis distinguishes three key factors that jointly determine HTNL optimization behavior. First, VQF and MM are algebraically equivalent under matched variational updates and optimize a squared-penalty formulation. Second, ADMM optimizes the unsquared objective through consensus splitting; we replace its truncated FISTA proximal solver with a scalar bisection update (ADMM-Bisect) for more accurate treatment of near-threshold blocks. |**2026-11-09**|**SIMBAD: Spatio-Temporal Traffic Forecasting Robust to Aperiodicity**|Daniel Y. Lee et.al.|Accurate traffic prediction is essential for urban planning but remains challenging due to the irregular patterns caused by unexpected events. While Spatio-Temporal Graph Neural Networks (STGNNs) effectively model periodic data, they often struggle with these aperiodic fluctuations. To address this, we propose SIMBAD, which improves generalization irregular patterns via two key mechanisms: 1) adaptive regulation of periodic signals to prioritize recent signals, and 2) dynamic spatial modeling that adjusts influence between connected nodes based on contextual relations. Built upon simple similarity metrics to adapt to aperiodic patterns, SIMBAD outperforms existing methods on real-world benchmark datasets, demonstrating superior performance in predicting unseen and irregular traffic events.|[2611.09015](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09015)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Scout Before You Route: Predicting Task Success and Token Count from a Small LLM Prefill**|Aristides Milios et.al.|Routers typically choose a model per query by trading predicted correctness against cost. Most price each model at a fixed cost, as if every query used the same number of output tokens, but the output length of reasoning models varies by an order of magnitude across queries. We predict both the success probability and the output length of candidate models from the prefill activations of a single small frozen LLM, the scout. On those eight datasets, our predicted costs save 9-33% at matched accuracy relative to median-length pricing, up to 69% of what perfect knowledge would save. They are significantly cheaper than each estimator we reimplemented or adapted from published work, including ZeroRouter and a MixLLM-style predictor, on a majority of datasets. Output length depends mostly on the problem. On most math and code datasets, harder problems are longer, so predicted success alone prices cost nearly as well. On knowledge and mixed-format datasets they are not, and predicted success falls well behind a probe trained on length. |**2026-11-09**|**CARGO: Causal Attribution and Responsibility-Guided Preventive Safe Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning for Cooperative Driving**|Jingjia Teng et.al.|This study presents Causal Attribution and Responsibility-Guided Optimization (CARGO), a multi-agent reinforcement learning framework for cooperative on-ramp merging in mixed traffic. |**2026-11-09**|**BMMS: Policy-Conditioned Measurement of Behavioral Compliance in Language Models**|Anthony Rhodes et.al.|Behavioral evaluation of language models fixes its dimensions in advance: hallucination, sycophancy, toxicity, over-refusal. Applying it excluded one of the quantities the suite itself reported. |**2026-11-09**|**Learning When to Think: Dual-Reference Offline Optimization for Adaptive VLM Reasoning**|Hongbin Lin et.al.|Reasoning traces improve vision-language models on complex multimodal tasks, but forcing every input to follow a long reasoning process leads to overthinking and unnecessary inference cost. Our code is available at https://github.com/HongbinLin3589/DR-BOLT.|[2611.09154](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09154)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Evaluation of a Multi-Link Machine Learning Approach for Rainfall Detection Using Satellite Microwave Links**|Mostafa Ftouni et.al.|The impacts of climate change have increased the need for reliable real-time rainfall monitoring systems. This study investigates rainfall detection using a network of opportunistic sensors based on satellite microwave links (SMLs) combined with machine learning (ML) classifiers. The results demonstrate that leveraging information from multiple links substantially enhances the detection of both wet and dry periods compared to single-link approaches. Moreover, the RF model consistently provides a more stable and reliable trade-off than CNN, improving rainfall detection and reducing misclassifications overall, with particularly strong performance during harsh weather conditions. The study spanned up to 32 rainy days between 2021 and 2024.|[2611.09156](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09156)|
|**2026-11-09**|**TailDART: Compute-Efficient Foundation Models for Financial Time-Series Forecasting**|Chiang Qin Zhi et.al.|Although time-series foundation models (TSFMs) achieve strong zero-shot transferability across high-information-density physical systems, applying overparameterized TSFMs to quantitative finance is fundamentally constrained by low signal-to-noise ratios, non-stationarity, and prohibitive autoregressive decoding latency. |**2026-11-09**|**Analyzing Code Provenance in LLMs for Programming Education**|Subhankar Maity et.al.|Large language models (LLMs) play an increasing role in programming education, challenging traditional assessment methods as they can generate fully functional code instantly. We extend the Min-K% probability approach by incorporating synthetic non-member code generation and apply it to Python code for membership inference and provenance tracking. The results show that free models enable more sensitive code memorization detection than commercial models. By systematically comparing these LLMs, we assess trade-offs between detection accuracy and privacy protection, taking an initial step toward effective assessment of AI-assisted programming education through combined provenance detection and process visibility.|[2611.09177](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09177)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Large Language Model Consensus and Corpus-Adaptive Topic Modeling for Classifying and Mapping Patent Innovation**|Sercan Ozcan et.al.|Patent analytics increasingly relies on transformer representations and large language models (LLMs), yet linking conceptual classification, topic formation and interpretation requires explicit decisions about model roles and the evidence retained at each stage. In this design-oriented study, we propose and demonstrate a patent analytics pipeline that links these tasks and keeps each decision open to audit. Three LLMs separately classify each patent by novelty (radical or incremental) and innovation type (product, process, service). Across five category corpora ranging from 591 to 29,215 patents, the pipeline produced 74 category-specific topic instances with a macro-average document coverage of 98.1% and topic diversity of 0.900--1.000, and fewer than 1% of classification outputs returned UNKNOWN. |**2026-11-09**|**Safe Reinforcement Learning via Probabilistic Option Shielding**|Gian Luca Ascari et.al.|Shielding is a safe reinforcement learning technique that provides safety guarantees while learning, by blocking unsafe actions. Traditional shielding relies on a complete model of the environment to provide long-term guarantees. Probabilistic logic shielding (PLS) lifts the requirement of having such model, but only considers safety in the short term, typically looking only one or few steps ahead. We propose Probabilistic Option Shielding (POS), which extends PLS to the option level, recovering long-horizon safety without reintroducing the need for a complete environment model. |**2026-11-09**|**Fused-Softmax for efficient quantized attention**|Tianxing Wu et.al.|Low-precision matrix throughput on modern GPUs is growing far faster than vector-arithmetic throughput. Attention therefore cannot realize the available GEMM performance because softmax has become the pipeline bottleneck. We introduce Fused-Softmax Attention, a training-free algorithm that reformulates and approximates softmax to fold its dominant operations into the attention GEMMs, moving work from vector units to matrix cores. Implemented for the AMD CDNA 4 architecture, our MXFP8, MXFP6 and MXFP4 kernels reach approximately 3.0, 4.0 and 4.6 PFLOP/s on an AMD Instinct MI355X GPU, yielding speedups of $2.1\times$, $2.8\times$ and $3.2\times$ over the BF16 attention.
