Presenting 951 Qwen-verified questions under every cyclic rotation of their options (or six random permutations for 12 options), the released checkpoint changes its answer for 25.8% [22.9-28.7] of questions (19.7% on the 605 English-language items alone); accuracy with the gold option first versus last differs by 10.1 points, and the flips concentrate where the model's own confidence is low (54% versus 8% across confidence terciles). Fine-tuning with Laya's trainer on 2,818 teacher-verified rows with option shuffling cuts the flip rate to 7.2%; the identical rows without shuffling give 11.4%, so shuffling is responsible for 4.2 points of the improvement. A confidence-weighted vote over orderings gives 69.7% against 69.1% for a single base pass at 4.1x the compute. Labels are LLM-made and the test sets are teacher-filtered. We also fine-tune with the authors' parallel layout on the same rows: flip rate 0.5%.|[2611.09803](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09803)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Generative Latent Priors for Interpreting and Steering Protein Language Models**|Daniel Hinjos et.al.|Dominant methods for interpreting large models impose strong structural assumptions. Generative Latent Priors (GLP) are a complementary approach: a diffusion model that learns the distribution of a frozen model's activations directly, imposing no assumptions. We port GLP to ESM-2 activations (UniRef50) and evaluate it at two host scales (ESM-2-8M and -650M), both as a lens for protein representations and as a prior for editing them. Generation quality scales smoothly with depth and compute. As a lens, single GLP "meta-neurons" are the strongest probes of discrete Swiss-Prot concepts at both hosts, beating InterPLM's released SAE features, and lead on continuous properties at 8M though not at 650M; falsification tests show this is mostly concentration, not new information. As an editing prior, the on-manifold correction makes DiffMean steering of three biochemical properties effective and restores on-manifoldness, but only in isolation: re-embedding the sequences actually produced after several rounds of decoding, the structural plausibility is compromised. This is mainly reproduced in additional SAE-based clamping structural steering experiments, where only Helix motif succeeds. The naturalness failure is traced to the rewriting coverage and the decoder rather than the prior, highlighting what the method must clear before it can serve for both discovery-by-editing and discovery-by-exploration. Trained GLPs are released on huggingface.co/collections/HPAI-BSC/glprot.|[2611.09818](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09818)|
|**2026-11-09**|**BitDecoding-FP4: Native FP4 KV Cache Decoding via Algorithm-System Co-Design**|Dayou Du et.al.|The key-value (KV) cache dominates memory capacity and bandwidth in long-context LLM serving. While 4-bit microscaling formats (NVFP4, MXFP4) shrink the cache and run attention on FP4 Tensor Cores, a fundamental gap remains between algorithmic quantization and system-aware acceleration: existing FP4 KV systems use vanilla quantization that causes severe accuracy drops, whereas accuracy-oriented 4-bit algorithms rely on vector codebooks whose lookup overhead degrades decoding throughput. We present BitDecoding-FP4, an algorithm--system co-design for accurate and efficient native FP4 KV decoding. We mitigate three error sources: Dual-Residual Query Quantization splits queries into two FP4 tensors, cutting query logit error from 60% to 1%; Underflow-Aware Probability Scaling prevents E4M3 scales from rounding long-context probabilities to zero; and Outlier-Aware Key Quantization combines static smoothing, Hadamard rotation, and scale search to suppress key outliers under token-wise scaling. At the system level, we remove the softmax bottleneck via transposed softmax with fused probability quantization and stacked query MMA on paged KV caches. Across dense and MoE models with GQA and MLA, BitDecoding-FP4 stays within 0.5 points of BF16 KV accuracy with NVFP4 (1 point with MXFP4), and tracks BF16 KV in RL training rollouts. In end-to-end serving on NVIDIA B300 with Qwen3-4B and Qwen3-30B-A3B, it delivers 2.4-3.0$\times$ higher peak serving throughput than BF16 KV. The implementation is available at https://github.com/OpenBitSys/BitDecoding-FP4.|[2611.09825](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09825)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Can Agentic Systems Close the Gap in Radiology with Production AI Tools?**|Aubin Charley et.al.|Radiology AI has developed along two paths: specialized models that perform well on narrow tasks but cover few clinical findings, and generalist models that address a broader range of tasks but remain prone to hallucination and inaccurate measurements. Combining the two through agentic systems offers a route towards comprehensive radiology reporting, but published systems are tied to one modality or body part. We describe a radiology agentic framework that standardizes how an orchestrator calls modular tools to produce a structured report, and use it to measure what such a system can do with the tools from FDA-cleared radiology AI devices. Report generation is simulated on a large multi-modality report corpus, with the orchestrator given either a catalog of finding classes consolidated from the cleared devices, each available on the modalities it is cleared for, or an unlimited catalog in which any class can be requested, and planning either from the clinical indication or from the target report. Tool outputs are simulated from the reference report, so the comparison isolates tool availability and orchestration from tool error. With the cleared catalog and the indication alone, the orchestrator recovers under a third of the clinically actionable findings; recovery improves substantially with the unlimited catalog, and further when the plan is written from the target report. Attributing every missed finding to a cause shows that most are classes no cleared device offers for the study's modality, and that a third are classes or tools that were offered and not called. Adding the most frequently missing tools closes most of the gap to the unlimited catalog on held-out reports, whereas a smaller orchestrator with the unlimited catalog gives up the whole gain. Tool availability and effective orchestration are thus complementary constraints on comprehensive AI-assisted radiology reporting.|[2611.09836](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09836)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Ensemble Methodology for Reference-Free Confidence Estimation in LLM-based Code Summarization**|Marco Colombo et.al.|This paper describes an approach and use case to estimate the correctness of LLM-generated COBOL summaries without access to human-authored references. The core intuition is that inter-model agreement on semantics is informative about truth, and that an LLM-as-judge can provide higher-fidelity pairwise assessments than traditional string- or embedding-based metrics. We operationalise this intuition with (i) controlled summary generation from diverse models; (ii) a LLM evaluator that compares pairs of summaries and emits structured judgments; and (iii) a calibrated aggregation rule that combines supportive evidence while penalising only corroborated contradictions.|[2611.09846](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09846)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Flow-Guided Target-Space Alignment via Path Consistency**|Ruizhi Yuan et.al.|Many multimodal alignment problems require mapping a source modality into a fixed space defined by a target modality, where the target space is not merely an intermediate embedding but is used directly for downstream analysis. In this setting, successful alignment should preserve both the global structure of the target distribution and the instance-level correspondence observed in paired source--target training data. Existing objectives typically address only part of this goal: contrastive losses encourage relative similarity, distribution-matching losses improve marginal agreement, and pointwise regression fits each source sample to its paired target without explicitly modeling target-space path geometry. We propose FlowAlign, a flow-guided target-space alignment method that repurposes conditional flow matching as a training-time alignment signal rather than an inference-time transport model. FlowAlign uses the learned flow field to guide the alignment of source-side representations with the fixed target space, encouraging them to preserve paired correspondence while remaining compatible with the global target-space structure. We further provide theoretical analysis showing how FlowAlign helps improve paired correspondence by controlling endpoint accuracy and capturing local target-space geometry. Experiments on synthetic data, paired single-cell multiome benchmarks, and image--text alignment demonstrate that FlowAlign improves instance level correspondence while preserving target-space structure across different modality settings.|[2611.09863](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09863)|
|**2026-11-09**|**On the Reliability of Majority-Vote Pseudo-Labels in Test-Time Reinforcement Learning**|Haoyu Wang et.al.|Test-time reinforcement learning (TTRL) adapts pretrained language models to unlabeled target-domain data using self-generated pseudo-labels, whose reliability remains poorly understood. We analyze majority-vote supervision for TTRL with online Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) and decompose its error into population-majority bias and prompt-dependent finite-rollout variation. Because population-majority bias is generally unidentifiable without external supervision, we focus on mitigating the downstream effects of finite-rollout variation. We propose \emph{Prompt-Conditional Distributionally Robust DPO (PC-Dr.DPO)}, which conditions within-prompt robust reweighting on observed vote reliability to make updates from sampling-sensitive prompts more conservative. We further introduce a cache-based mechanism to reduce self-supervised forgetting during online adaptation. Across six mathematical and knowledge-intensive reasoning benchmarks and five model backbones, PC-Dr.DPO matches or outperforms the strongest TTRL baseline in 24 of 30 settings.|[2611.09872](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09872)|
|**2026-11-09**|**CPU-ARC: Agentic Reasoning Caching for on-CPU Reasoning Reuse**|Peter Belcak et.al.|Large language models reinvent the wheel all the time. They conflate task-solving with the execution of the solutions, which forces them to solve every problem from scratch. We show that once a task has been reasoned through, the application of its solution to a new problem instance can be done very efficiently and with little involvement from language models. We introduce agentic reasoning caching, a method for constructing robust CPU-executable agentic reasoning caches (ARCs) from LLM reasoning traces. ARCs show on-par performance with frontier LLMs across a range of difficult mathematical and scientific tasks, are fully explainable, continually trainable, robust to input perturbations, and 16-291x cheaper to invoke than the source model.|[2611.09891](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09891)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Toward a Robust Crowd-labeling Framework using Expert Evaluation and Pairwise Comparison**|Faiza Khan Khattak et.al.|Crowd-labeling emerged from the need to label large-scale and complex data, a tedious, expensive, and time-consuming task. One of the main challenges in the crowd-labeling task is to control for or determine in advance the proportion of low-quality/malicious labelers. If that proportion grows too high, there is often a phase transition leading to a steep, non-linear drop in labeling accuracy as noted by Karger et al. [2014]. To address these challenges, we propose a new framework called Expert Label Injected Crowd Estimation (ELICE) and extend it to different versions and variants that delay phase transition leading to a better labeling accuracy. ELICE automatically combines and boosts bulk crowd labels supported by labels from experts for limited number of instances from the dataset. The expert-labels help to estimate the individual ability of crowd labelers and difficulty of each instance, both of which are used to aggregate the labels. Empirical evaluation shows the superiority of ELICE as compared to other state-of-the-art methods. We also derive a lower bound on the number of expert-labeled instances needed to estimate the crowd ability and dataset difficulty as well as to get better quality labels.|[1607.02174](http://arxiv.org/abs/1607.02174)|
|**2026-11-09**|**LLM-Enhanced Self-Evolving Reinforcement Learning for Multi-Step E-Commerce Payment Fraud Risk Detection**|Bo Qu et.al.|This paper presents a novel approach to e-commerce payment fraud detection by integrating reinforcement learning (RL) with Large Language Models (LLMs). By framing transaction risk as a multi-step Markov Decision Process (MDP), RL optimizes risk detection across multiple payment stages. Crafting effective reward functions, essential for RL model success, typically requires significant human expertise due to the complexity and variability in design. LLMs, with their advanced reasoning and coding capabilities, are well-suited to refine these functions, offering improvements over traditional methods. Our approach leverages LLMs to iteratively enhance reward functions, achieving better fraud detection accuracy and demonstrating zero-shot capability. Experiments with real-world data confirm the effectiveness, robustness, and resilience of our LLM-enhanced RL framework through long-term evaluations, underscoring the potential of LLMs in advancing industrial RL applications.|[2509.18719](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.18719)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Relic and Gumi: Governing Memory, Safety, and Continuity in Diegetic Relational Agents**|Daniele Veri' et.al.|Longitudinal relational agents rely on memory for continuity, but remembering can lead to intrusion, clinical overconfidence, emotional manipulation, and opacity. We present Relic, a governance architecture for diegetic relational agents, and Gumi, a subject-specific companion agent initialized and governed through Relic. The architecture separates what the system can observe, what researchers can review, what Gumi can remember, and what Gumi can say. We describe three mechanisms. Relic represents sensitive safety signals as researcher-facing governance objects rather than user-facing explanations or agent memories. Shared Continuity Memory organizes relational markers around subject-confirmed wording, corrections, explicit follow-up permissions, and recall limits. We specify a runtime mediation layer for delivery, proactivity, session identity, output transformation, and platform allowlists; adoption across live paths remains partial. These mechanisms aim to preserve relational continuity without turning Gumi into a clinical assistant, mood tracker, general-purpose assistant, or opaque profiling system. We contribute the architecture, object model, researcher workbench design, and a proposed evaluation framework for governed relational memory. An exploratory pilot involved the author as P1 and one external participant as P2. Weekly self-reports from P1 and researcher-led interviews with P2 provide descriptive observations of early use and perceived naturalness. The pilot does not support claims about clinical efficacy, diagnosis, therapeutic outcomes, general usability, or deployment safety. Relic provides research infrastructure intended to make relational memory, safety constraints, and agent behavior inspectable and testable.|[2611.08504](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08504)|
|**2026-11-09**|**MeshRT: Compile-Time Governed Wafer-Scale Runtime for Low-Latency High-Throughput Inference**|Congjie He et.al.|Wafer-scale accelerators promise ultra-low-latency AI inference, but current system stacks still carry an unsustainably high cost premium. The reason is that many current and emerging inference techniques, such as batching and MoE, introduce runtime dynamism that existing wafer-scale systems cannot support efficiently. As a result, they must choose between two unsatisfactory options: accept suboptimal latency, or preserve low latency by restricting dynamic techniques, thereby limiting the models they can support and reducing aggregate throughput. This tradeoff significantly increases the cost of low-latency serving. We present MeshRT, the first wafer-scale system to achieve both ultra-low latency and high throughput for LLM inference. MeshRT achieves this via a novel system architecture, which we call compile-time governance of runtime dynamism. This architecture features new system abstractions and mechanisms that enable MeshRT to compile dynamic events arising from LLM inference directly into per-core schedules, enabling asynchronous and decentralized execution with low runtime overhead. Implemented on a commodity wafer-scale accelerator, MeshRT preserves ultra-low latency while improving throughput by up to 56$\times$ over the state-of-the-art system for wafer-scale accelerators. Against measured single-instance SGLang results on H200, MeshRT achieves 4.5-7.0$\times$ lower latency. Under ideal linear scaling of those GPU measurements to an approximately power-matched H200 cluster, MeshRT achieves 1.7-13.0$\times$ higher decode system throughput in the low-latency regime.|[2611.08508](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08508)|
|**2026-11-09**|**MorphSched: Workload-Aware Adaptive Scheduler Generation for Linux**|Yifan Zhang et.al.|Modern Linux scheduling increasingly requires workload-specific policies to meet the dynamic performance needs of heterogeneous applications. The recent sched_ext framework enables custom BPF schedulers, but building practical policies still requires coordinating Rust user-space control logic, BPF scheduling callbacks, generated user-kernel interfaces, and strict eBPF verifier constraints. This paper presents MorphSched, an LLM-driven system for synthesizing complete Rust+BPF sched_ext schedulers. MorphSched profiles target workloads under Linux EEVDF to construct dynamic, workload-specific optimization objectives, and uses a scaffold-constrained generation pipeline to translate scheduling intent into deployable scheduler implementations. To improve synthesis reliability, MorphSched separates implementation repair from strategy search: verifier, build, and runtime failures are handled through a DAG-structured repair process, while performance feedback is accumulated in an evolutionary memory pool to refine high-level scheduling strategies across iterations. We evaluate MorphSched across 120 machine/workload/interferer configurations on a real Linux kernel. The generated schedulers compile, pass eBPF verification, load successfully, and remain stable throughout our benchmark runs without manual edits to generated scheduler source code. Across these configurations, MorphSched improves the primary foreground metric over EEVDF by 26.5% on average and up to 87.3%. In selected comparisons, the generated schedulers are also competitive with representative hand-written sched_ext schedulers. These results show that scaffold-constrained, system-grounded LLM generation can be a practical tool for exploring workload-specific Linux scheduling policies.|[2611.08509](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08509)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Subliminal Learning in Vision-Language Models**|Arman Hatami et.al.|Subliminal learning is a phenomenon in large language models (LLMs) where a teacher passes a hidden preference to a student through training data unrelated to that preference. We study whether subliminal learning also occurs in vision-language models (VLMs). We finetune a teacher VLM to prefer a specific animal or facial attribute and use a control teacher from the same base model. Both teachers generate numbers for an unrelated visual task involving geometric shapes. Two students initialized from the same base VLM are finetuned on the same images, using numbers generated by either the biased or control teacher. Across VLMs ranging from 2B to 7B parameters, we find that both animal and facial preferences can transfer through this unrelated task. Students trained on biased-teacher outputs select the target more often than control students, with a difference of up to $\sim$10 percentage points in the animal preference task. Transfer can become stronger with more training data and varies with the target preference, model, and how the numbers are generated. Beyond preferences, we show that the biased student's explanations use more of the same words and phrases as the biased teacher's explanations than those of the control student, even when all three models select the same image. These results matter for training VLMs and show that training on unrelated data may change both a VLM's image preferences and how it explains its choices, and that transfer can become stronger with more training samples.|[2611.08538](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08538)|
|**2026-11-09**|**SAFTAC: Simulation-Augmented Fine-Tuning of Open-Source LLMs for Analog Circuit Design**|Junsheng Huang et.al.|Circuit design for analog integrated circuits (ICs) is challenging because circuit topology, device sizing, and circuit performance are tightly coupled. Existing large language model (LLM)-based methods either rely on inference-time correction around frozen models or train on limited circuit types without directly using simulation outcomes as training signals. A key bottleneck is that existing datasets do not provide sufficient task information for training LLMs on end-to-end specification-conditioned design. To fill this gap, we first construct a large-scale simulation-grounded dataset with 8,626 design tasks across 10 different types of analog circuits, where each task includes a textual description, labeled I/O ports, loading condition, target specifications, a testbench, and a reference netlist. Using this dataset, we present SAFTAC, a simulation-augmented fine-tuning framework that trains LLMs to generate and revise analog circuit design. SAFTAC combines three-stage supervised fine-tuning, which progressively builds the capabilities required for analog circuit design and feedback-guided correction, with simulation feedback-augmented reinforcement fine-tuning, which further optimizes models using simulation-grounded rewards. We evaluate SAFTAC under single-pass generation and two-pass generation with simulation feedback, showing improvements over the strongest closed-source LLM by 15.9% and 7.8%, respectively.|[2611.08543](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08543)|
|**2026-11-09**|**PINNeval: A Comprehensive Evaluation Standard for Physics-Informed Neural Networks**|Kevin von Bargen et.al.|The Physics-Informed Neural Network (PINN) literature has produced a rapidly expanding catalogue of methods. We divide a PINN training pipeline systematically into four components and observe that many existing benchmarks study these components in isolation. These components are domain-importance adaptation, loss balancing, neural network architecture, and optimization scheme. Moreover, the studies set the surrounding and not considered components of the pipeline to a minimal default that is no longer representative of current best practice approaches. Conclusions drawn under such conditions may not transfer to competitive pipelines. In this work, we propose a new evaluation protocol that (1) treats the four components as an integrated system; (2) crucially requires competitive implementations of the components not under investigation; (3) specifies the metrics and reporting format; and (4) is accompanied by structured documentation that ensures the traceability of evaluations across studies. Based on this comprehensive protocol, we release PINNpipe, an innovative JAX package containing 18 different methods from the current literature as well as nine efficiently implemented benchmark problems. Using PINNpipe, we illustrate our evaluation protocol in relation to established benchmark studies and report results that challenge common assumptions.|[2611.08559](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08559)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Towards Label-Efficient Geospatial Foundation Model Adaptation via Positive-Unlabeled Learning for Building Damage Mapping**|Hengwei Zhao et.al.|Geospatial foundation models (GFMs) are reshaping remote sensing by shifting the field from task-specific modeling toward general-purpose geospatial intelligence. Mapping the damage to buildings stands to benefit substantially from this shift, as GFMs pretrained on large-scale Earth observation data provide generalizable geospatial representations that can facilitate effective responses to sudden-onset disasters. Although parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) facilitates GFM adaptation, parameter efficiency does not necessarily translate into label efficiency. Building damage mapping remains challenging due to scarce pre-existing labeled data or limited time for annotation. When weak supervision is introduced to alleviate the labeling bottleneck, we identify the difficulty of learning sufficiently discriminative representations as a key challenge for label-efficient GFM adaptation. To address this challenge, we propose a Positive-Unlabeled GFM Adaptation (PUMA) framework that requires annotations for only a small number of damaged buildings. More specifically, PUMA integrates non-contrastive weakly supervised representation refinement to mitigate the impact of noisy supervision on representation learning from a gradient perspective, and latent-state-based label disambiguation to enable reversible supervision refinement without requiring class prior estimation. Extensive experiments on disaster benchmark datasets from around the world demonstrated the effectiveness and robustness of PUMA. A real-world application to the 2025 Los Angeles wildfires further highlighted its potential to accurately map damage under highly limited supervision. More broadly, these findings suggest that weakly supervised PEFT offers a label-efficient pathway from GFMs to real-world applications.|[2611.08561](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08561)|
|**2026-11-09**|**CascadeRec: Deployed Adaptive LLM Compute Allocation for Multi-Tenant Content Recommendation**|Kai Zhao et.al.|Large language models (LLMs) are attractive rerankers for recommendation. Running an expensive model on every request is costly, and for users with sparse interaction histories it often lowers quality. We present CascadeRec, a two-tier LLM reranking cascade deployed in production at a large financial planning and analysis (FP&A) SaaS provider. It serves content recommendations to enterprise tenants on AWS EKS and GKE. A lightweight Tier-1 model reranks every request. A router escalates only the requests where extra inference-time compute is likely to help, and sends them to a more expensive Tier-2 procedure (self-consistency sampling, multi-path reasoning, or a larger model). The system has run in a production A/B experiment for over a month. Compared with uniform use of the expensive tier, CascadeRec has reduced inference cost by about 10%, with minimal latency impact and no quality regression observed to date. Tenant data cannot be released, so we also evaluate the pipeline on public benchmarks (MovieLens-1M and Amazon-Electronics). Segment-level analysis with paired-bootstrap confidence intervals shows that uniform LLM reranking lowers NDCG@10 for sparse-history users by up to 30%, and its only statistically significant gain appears on the segment with the longest histories. Escalating cold users can make the loss larger instead of repairing it. Which segments benefit from escalation depends on the domain, and this motivates the segment-gated rollout we use in production. We describe the deployment architecture, the measured benefits, and lessons learned, including inter-tenant load imbalance as a driver for cascading and the handling of LLM output-parsing failures at the serving layer.|[2611.08566](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08566)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Q-OIL: Optimistic Exploration in Learning from Human Interventions**|Pranav Teegavarapu et.al.|As robots are deployed in the real world, they will inevitably make mistakes. Human interventions provide valuable feedback for improving their behavior, enabling them to adapt online to new scenarios. However, studying human-in-the-loop robot learning is difficult: real-world experiments are slow and expensive, so most algorithm development relies on simulated human models. We show that common human modeling assumptions do not match how real people intervene in practice. Through analyzing intervention data from human participants, we find that humans tend to intervene when agent progress stalls, and that their corrective actions are often suboptimal. Motivated by these findings, we introduce a progress-based model of human intervention that achieves higher rank correlation with real-user algorithm rankings, enabling realistic algorithm benchmarking and development in simulation. Building on these insights, we propose Q-OIL, a method that adds optimistic bonuses to intervention transitions in reinforcement learning and softly guides exploration toward human corrections. We find that decoupled critics are critical for stability because they prevent the bonuses from backing up to pre-intervention states. As a result, Q-OIL learns more efficiently than competitive baselines, achieving 10-50% higher success in simulation and on real robots, and improving pretrained VLAs on two bimanual manipulation tasks. As we ground both evaluation and algorithms in realistic human behavior, this work provides a practical path toward scalable robot learning from interventions.|[2611.08576](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08576)|
|**2026-11-09**|**HaNDF: Object-Conditioned Geometric Neural Hand Distance Fields**|Zuhao Liu et.al.|We propose, HaNDF, object-conditional neural distance fields as data-driven priors for modeling the plausible hand-object configurations, in the product manifold of hand articulations. While recent unconditional generative priors showed success in modeling human pose, hands are versatile articulated bodies often found in interaction with other objects. This makes it crucial to develop a generative prior which can model the plausible hand poses conditioned on the object under interaction. To this end, our HaNDF proposes a geometry-aware, object-conditioned neural distance field, representing the plausible articulations in the zero-level set of a conditional field. We leverage tools from Riemannian geometry to (i) represent plausible hands in the zero level-set of an object-induced neural field, and (ii) project a given pose onto this field. Using HaNDF, we can also optimize for a plausible condition, determining the object under interaction. Our extensive evaluations demonstrate that our HaNDF provides a flexible and powerful prior for hand-object interaction, suitable for applications such as pose refinement, reconstruction under occlusion, and physically plausible manipulation synthesis.|[2611.08578](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08578)|
|**2026-11-09**|**s $^3$ : Simple Scaling of Swarms via Cooperative Multi-agent RL**|Kunal Jha et.al.|Groups can achieve more than individuals in the same amount of time, but only if their members coordinate: they must decide who works on what and what to share. However, this organization in multi-agent systems today usually comes from arbitrary, static harnesses designed a priori without adapting to agent behaviors or task specifications. We ask whether agents can instead learn to organize themselves. We introduce s$^3$, the first multi-agent reinforcement learning method in which agents decide for themselves how long to work alone and when to share findings or read teammates' messages, with no predefined organization or roles. Our s$^3$ agents learn to take different approaches, read each other's work, exhibit emergent roles that complement each other in the swarm, and generalize to bigger teams. We study both coordination, where agents must combine private information, and parallel exploration, where sharing discoveries can make search more efficient. With matched training and inference budgets, s$^3$, swarms evaluated with twice as many agents as in training solve over 25$\times$ more coordination problems than the strongest baseline, and in exploration, communicating agents reduce repeated work by nearly 50% and outperform best-of-N by up to 9.4 percentage points. Rather than being designed in advance, s$^3$ enables organization to emerge from a shared goal. This opens the door to capable systems built from many models that learn to work together.|[2611.08615](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08615)|
|**2026-11-09**|**FinEvolveKG: Controlled and Attributable Prompt Evolution for Financial Knowledge Graph Construction**|Abhinav Arun et.al.|Financial institutions construct knowledge graphs (KGs) from shared SEC 10-Ks to serve risk analysis, portfolio management, and investment research, whose analytical needs differ. Extraction must adapt without fragmenting shared grounding, typing, and temporal rules. Hand-authored persona prompts and fine-tuning add cost. Weight-free prompt optimization offers an alternative, but unconstrained evolution of a monolithic prompt can rewrite shared rules alongside persona guidance, obscuring changes and complicating review. We introduce FinEvolveKG, a KG-construction harness separating reusable general and persona-policy blocks and mechanically confining GEPA, a reflective prompt optimizer, to the designated block. During persona adaptation, the approved general block remains byte-stable and every optimizer edit is localized to a separately versioned persona policy. A dual objective balances generic temporal-KG quality with persona-relevant facts, motifs, and multi-hop connectivity. We evaluate five mutation scopes on ticker-disjoint 10-K splits, with three seeds for the persona-conditioned arms (v3-v5) across three personas. On the three-seed mean, the complete two-stage block-constrained workflow (v4) improves over the frozen prompt baseline (v1) by 19.4-30.9% and over the tested unconstrained full-prompt workflow (v5) by 2.2-6.4%, leading v5 on both objective components and winning all nine paired-seed comparisons with the lowest seed variance in every persona. Without further optimization, v4 also exceeds v5 across all evaluated out-of-distribution (OOD) shifts. An SME audit supports using the generated triplets as ground truth; a blinded two-SME audit favors v4 over v5 in 70.1% of contrast-enriched judgments. These results show that mechanically attributable evolution can coexist with strong empirical performance, without attributing the gains causally to the boundary.|[2611.08643](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08643)|
|**2026-11-09**|**From Geometry to Function: A Multi-Task LiDAR Benchmark Dataset for Semantic and Functional Parsing of Urban Facades**|Abdiaziz Muse et.al.|Understanding building facades is central to urban digital twins, heritage modelling, and sustainable city analysis, yet existing LiDAR benchmarks stop at geometry and semantic segmentation and offer little support for reasoning about the functional role of each architectural element. We introduce HFX3D, a large-scale urban facade benchmark of approximately 492 million LiDAR points from 26 buildings in Halifax, Canada, captured across four seasons, day and night, and varied weather. Each facade is annotated at three tiers, semantic, instance, and per-instance functional attributes, making it, to our knowledge, the first facade dataset to pair geometry and semantics with functional labels. Annotations are produced by a semi-automatic pipeline that combines geometry-guided heuristics with expert validation. We benchmark representative point-based, graph-based, convolutional, and transformer architectures and find that semantic segmentation is already challenging under severe class imbalance, while instance separation of repetitive structures and functional reasoning from geometry are substantially harder. The benchmark establishes a new foundation for function-aware 3D urban understanding. Project page: https://jijup.github.io/hfx3d-benchmark/ ; code: https://github.com/jijup/hfx3d-benchmark|[2611.08645](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08645)|
|**2026-11-09**|**VoluCompose: Constructing Compact Volumetric Visual Representations for Medical Image Understanding**|Zanting Ye et.al.|Medical vision--language models can interpret volumetric images, but processing full volumes is costly because hundreds of slices produce long, redundant visual-token sequences. Existing compression methods often couple content selection with budget determination. We observe that neighboring slices share visual features and that attention concentration varies across visual encoders. We propose VoluCompose, a training-free, model-agnostic framework that separates what visual content to retain from how many tokens to allocate. Its hyperbolic token composition module groups contiguous slices into ordered support units and uses the Lorentz distance to account for feature magnitude and direction during token allocation, anchor selection, and refinement. Its capacity control module uses visual-encoder attention entropy to allocate more tokens when attention is more dispersed. We evaluate VoluCompose on two tasks across three datasets and four backbones. Across nine calibrated settings, VoluCompose attains the highest mean quality--retention hypervolume among the compared methods. It achieves a mean relative quality of 121.68\% against the uncompressed references while retaining 22.78% of visual tokens on average. Compression remains robust even at a 10% token budget. Across settings, the lowest ROUGE-L ratio to the uncompressed reference is 96.4\% for VoluCompose. The corresponding minima for the compression baselines range from 15.0% to 70.5%. Code is available at https://github.com/yezanting/VoluCompose.|[2611.08649](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08649)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Scoring, Not Learning: Decomposing Catalog-Consolidation Gains in Web-Scraped Retail Product Recognition**|Kaden Leis et.al.|Retail product catalogs assembled from web image search are cheap to build and pervasively noisy: queries return packaging shots instead of products, and entries proliferate under names that cannot be distinguished visually. Consolidating such a catalog can look like an alternative to scaling the model. We show this is not a comparison between two ways of improving a recognizer, but between improving a recognizer and changing what counts as correct. We study a public archive of 165,769 cheese images across 1,824 query-named entries. A packaging filter and near-duplicate removal reduce it to a 998-class recognition set on one fixed, group-aware split. Canonicalizing entries to their base type (844 classes) raises top-1 accuracy by 4.0 points. Scoring the unchanged model against the merged label space, by summing its predicted probability within each canonical group, recovers +4.27 points with no retraining (+4.25 over six seeds). Retraining on the consolidated catalog then adds -0.10$\pm$0.53, +0.08$\pm$0.65 and -0.10$\pm$0.65 points at 28, 50 and 88 M parameters, indistinguishable from zero at every capacity ($\pm$ is the across-seed standard deviation). That component is specific, not mechanical: random merges with the identical size profile yield only +0.68 points, and the three backbones agree to within 0.1 points. But it is overwhelmingly category coarsening, not alias correction: across the 37 merged groups, type coarsenings supply 94% of the gain and alias collapses only 7%. We also detect no interaction between consolidation and backbone size: tripling backbone parameters is worth +1.50$\pm$0.57 points on the raw catalog and +1.42$\pm$00.44 on the canonical one, a within-seed interaction of -0.08 [-0.79,+0.63]. On this archive, the reported consolidation gain primarily reflects the evaluation label space, and separating that out costs one evaluation|[2611.08655](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08655)|
|**2026-11-09**|**JEPAhead: From Inpainting to Causal Forecasting for Urban Driving**|Daniel G. P. Petrini et.al.|We fine-tune a pre-trained V-JEPA1 predictor from bidirectional latent inpainting to action-free causal forecasting while keeping the encoder frozen. Using contiguous past observations, horizon-aligned targets, and group-level causal attention, the model forecasts eight future token groups, corresponding to sixteen frames, in one forward pass. On CARLA Town12, it retrieves the correct future among 591 candidates in 69.0% of cases, compared with 55.3% for persistence, with greater gains at longer horizons and on tokens exhibiting greater change. It also outperforms a compact predictor trained from scratch with six times fewer parameters. Semantic analysis indicates driving-relevant information in predicted features: a fixed linear probe trained on real encoder tokens achieves 0.16 mIoU on patches undergoing semantic-class changes, versus 0.13 for persistence. Vehicle-conditioned retrieval improves over persistence by 15 percentage points at the longest horizon. Pixel-space evaluation shows modest L1 gains but little improvement under LPIPS, motivating direct evaluation through latent and semantic measures alongside an oracle reconstruction reference. These findings support further investigation of latent forecasting for driving perception, while downstream utility remains unverified.|[2611.08657](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08657)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Compression of Emotion in Language Model Representations**|Laya Iyer et.al.|Large language models (LLMs) have been evaluated on their ability to identify emotions expressed in text and produce fluent emotional language. However, output predictions alone do not reveal how fine-grained emotion distinctions are organized internally. We study seven LLMs on GoEmotions, an English Reddit dataset annotated with 27 fine-grained emotion categories plus neutral. We compare model predictions and emotion-prompted final-token representations with the 28 GoEmotions categories and with coarser emotion-family and sentiment groupings. We find a consistent pattern of $\textit{overcompression}$ relative to the fine-grained GoEmotions taxonomy. Under the tested KMeans protocol, when we cluster their hidden representations without using emotion labels, the clearest split is into two groups that largely separate positive from negative emotion. Coarser emotion groupings are also recovered with fewer clusters than the full 28-category taxonomy. Yet linear probes retain substantial fine-grained predictive information. These results suggest that fine-grained emotion information remains accessible even though broader affective structure more strongly organizes the unsupervised geometry.|[2611.08683](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08683)|
|**2026-11-09**|**FoleyForge: Free-form, Controllable, Rectified-flow Editing of Sound Effects**|Giovana Morais et.al.|We present FoleyForge, a generative model for instruction-based editing of sound effects. Given an input clip and a free-form instruction describing the desired change, it applies the edit while preserving the input's identity. A single model handles semantic, stylistic, and DSP-like changes. We introduce a data pipeline that guides an audio language model to author both DSP-based acoustic edits and similarity-preserving semantic edits, thus addressing the lack of publicly available audio editing data. Such data is then used to fine-tune a rectified-flow audio generation model, which we extend to expose attribute controls for loudness change, edit strength, and an input-derived contour envelope, each tunable via classifier-free guidance, yielding a more expressive editing model. FoleyForge achieves state-of-the-art instruction adherence at competitive audio quality and source faithfulness.|[2611.08695](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08695)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Dual-Stream LLM-Guided Reward Design for Reject-Inference-Aware Reinforcement Learning in E-Commerce Payment Fraud Detection**|Bo Qu et.al.|Production fraud-detection models learn from a censored slice of their own traffic: transactions a legacy system blocked never receive fraud labels, so models train on a biased, approved-only distribution. We extend LLM-guided reward design, the framework of Qu et al. (2025) in which an LLM evolves the reward of a two-step REINFORCE fraud-blocking policy, to attack this reject-inference problem inside the reward. A single LLM designs a dual-stream reward: stream 1 applies the standard dollar-weighted precision objective to labeled transactions; stream 2 scores legacy-blocked, unlabeled transactions by contrasting the newest gradient-boosted risk signal against the legacy scores that drove the original block. Rather than prescribe which signals matter, we expose a superset of evidence and let the LLM decide for itself which to use, guided by evaluation feedback alone; because it writes the reward as code, that choice is directly auditable. On a held-out four-month out-of-time test, the dual-stream agent beats the prior labeled-only agent at every target recall, by +4.7, +2.4 and +3.0 points of dollar-weighted precision at 80%, 85% and 90% recall, and the production baseline by +6.8 points on average.
