|**2026-11-09**|**An Educated Guess: Deriving Statistically Aligned Gradient Estimates for Zeroth-Order Optimisation**|Justus Huebotter et.al.|As novel hardware architectures are explored for the purposes of machine learning, including neuromorphic chips, asynchronous systems, and ASICs, effective learning algorithms are highly desirable. While backpropagation is the standard for differentiation on standard hardware, its requirement for global synchrony and exact differentiability limits the exploration of exotic algorithms on non-traditional substrates and devices. Zeroth-order (ZO) optimisation methods, such as SPSA and weight perturbation, offer a compelling alternative by estimating gradients through inference-only passes; however, these methods have historically suffered due to the `curse of dimensionality', where random perturbations become increasingly misaligned with the true gradient in high-dimensional spaces. In this work, we move beyond blind random noise by deriving zeroth-order search directions from activation geometry available during the forward pass. This leads to a family of Spectral Zeroth-Order methods, SZO-$\kappa$, whose directions solve local response-based gradient-guessing objectives under explicit stochastic assumptions. These methods explore feature-supported directions that are more likely to produce informative loss responses. Using multilayer and convolutional networks, we analyse gradient alignment, bias-variance structure, and training stability of these methods relative to standard perturbation baselines and backpropagation.|[2611.08712](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08712)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Patching the Blind Spots: Reversible LLM Rule Overlay for Frozen Fraud Detectors**|Bo Qu et.al.|In industrial tabular fraud classification, gradient-boosted trees remain state-of-the-art, but even a well-trained model has blind spots, and retraining the deployed model is not always safe. We propose a framework that patches such a model without modifying it: LLMs propose rules anchored on features the model underweights by SHAP, and the resulting noisy pool is screened for empirical support and calibrated into an additive, reversible log-lift overlay that runs at the production cut-off. Single-LLM benefit is high-variance and inverts between datasets, so we pool three diverse backbones by equal-weight logit averaging, trading peak gain for a bounded worst case. On a real-world e-commerce fraud detector trained on selection-biased data, continued training regresses even when carefully tuned, whereas the overlay gains on average +2.26% recall at equal precision ($\Delta$R@P) and +1.60% precision at equal recall ($\Delta$P@R) over production cut-offs spanning 30-57% precision and 23-63% recall. On a public benchmark of seven designed bias-shift variants it raises $\Delta$P@R on every variant and keeps $\Delta$R@P near neutral. Each amendment is a readable predicate naming the fraud pattern it covers.|[2611.08714](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08714)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Remembering Is Not Enough: Evaluating Timing and Omission in Longitudinal AI Mental Health Support**|Eli Min et.al.|Evaluations of large language models (LLMs) for mental health support commonly focus on the clinical appropriateness of individual responses, potentially overlooking failures that emerge across extended interactions. As AI assistants accumulate memory of users' circumstances and prior disclosures, this raises an accountability question: Do AI chatbots provide appropriate support as users' needs evolve? We investigate by analyzing 14,256 longitudinal simulations grounded in donated chatbot histories from 22 users, examining how a memory-equipped assistant responds to suicidal ideation, self-harm, and anxiety crises across recovery trajectories and communication styles. We introduce the Context-Aware and Readiness-Evaluative (CARE) framework, developed with mental health professionals, to assess the timing and quality of advice, including omissions. Advice was less aligned with users' readiness after a crisis than in matched control stretches of the same conversation, largely because of premature recommendations, which were most common when recovery stalled. Revisiting a crisis reduced premature advice but not lagging advice, and users' communication style changed how often and when the assistant gave recommendations. Although explicitly unproductive advice was rare, the assistant frequently omitted clinician-expected support such as follow-up and planning for next steps. These findings show the limits of single response-level safety evaluation and the need to assess timing, omission, and consistency across users when holding AI systems accountable for longitudinal support.|[2611.08721](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08721)|
|**2026-11-09**|**How are viral trends different from educational and commercial uses of LLM image generation and editing? A privacy-preserving descriptive analysis**|Will Tucker-Ray et.al.|Gemini's Nano Banana models, first introduced in late 2025, led to a shift in the capabilities and use of image generation and editing. We utilize Urania, a Google-developed framework for generating insights about LLM chatbot interactions with rigorous differential privacy (DP) guarantees, to analyze use cases and intents, techniques and ways of prompting, without ever looking at a single image or individual prompt. We select a subsample of 2.1M conversations in early 2026 which utilized Nano Banana models, into roughly 800 clusters in five categories: (1) Social & Viral Sharing, (2) Brand & Marketing Collateral, (3) Educational & Technical Information Media, (4) Product Visualizations & E-commerce, and (5) Architectural & Spatial Designs. We then compare these clusters on 21 features of use, measuring aspects of prompts' (a) editing techniques, (b) artistic style requests, (c) composition, and (d) chatbot interactions. This allows us to describe a baseline of how people are using image generation and editing tools for different use cases---(including growing and emergent use cases which were not possible before Nano Banana)---in a privacy-preserving manner.|[2611.08749](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08749)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Random Symbolic Boosting: A Geometric Approach to Symbolic Regression**|Jos\'e R. Ortegaa et.al.|Symbolic models offer interpretability, but their construction through generational evolution can be computationally expensive. This article presents Random Symbolic Boosting (RSB), a method that constructs explicit additive symbolic models via boosting over populations of randomly generated syntax trees, eliminating the need for generational evolution. The idea combines two observations: high-dimensional spaces can be spanned by enough linearly independent vectors, and the semantics of syntax trees can be directly manipulated as such vectors. It is shown that, for Ordinary Least Squares, the boosting process guarantees a monotonic reduction of the training error for both regression and classification. Furthermore, an adaptive geometric early stopping mechanism is introduced. It is based on the angle between the prediction and target vectors, is independent of the problems scale, and requires no adjustments across tasks. Evaluated on a standard benchmark for symbolic regression, the method is located at the Pareto-optimal point between predictive accuracy and training time, ranking first on the real-world datasets. Additionally, it was tested on a large tabular classification benchmark with 200 problems, achieving competitive results.|[2611.08753](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08753)|
|**2026-11-09**|**AtariBench: Evaluating Spatiotemporal Reasoning of Vision-Language Models in Atari Gameplay**|Mingjia Huo et.al.|Vision-Language Models (VLMs) have achieved strong performance on static visual understanding benchmarks, yet their ability to act in dynamic environments remains less well understood. We introduce AtariBench, a controlled benchmark that evaluates VLMs across 20 Atari 2600 games, requiring models to make sequential decisions from high-temporal-resolution gameplay streams. By decoupling fast in-game dynamics from wall-clock inference speed, AtariBench focuses on whether models can perceive rapid visual state changes, reason over spatiotemporal dynamics, and ground actions in the current observation. Our experiments reveal substantial limitations in state-of-the-art VLMs: models often fail to infer object motion and direction, exhibit brittle sequential decision-making, and over-rely on textual interaction history despite newly appended visual feedback. We further introduce a separability analysis to identify which in-game scores provide reliable signals of model competence, and use the benchmark to study how memory design and prompting strategies affect performance. Overall, AtariBench exposes a persistent gap between passive visual perception and active decision-making, providing a lightweight and rigorous testbed for developing VLMs that can reason and act in dynamic environments.|[2611.08755](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08755)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Goals as a Representational Layer for Human-AI Collaboration**|Gaia Molinaro et.al.|Goals are central to how people, teams, and organizations structure their work. Yet, while generative artificial intelligence (AI) systems increasingly operate across a worker's context, information about why a person is pursuing a task, beyond local prompts and interactions, is typically missing. We argue that goals should become an explicit representational layer mediating interactions among people, AI systems, and organizations. Because human goals are hierarchical, partially latent, and dynamically evolving, such a layer must be multidimensional, both grounded in human articulation and inferred from available evidence, and continuously updated. A goal layer could connect immediate requests to broader outcomes, improve the organization and retrieval of relevant context, support reflection and coordination, and provide a foundation for training, aligning, and evaluating AI systems in terms of human-centered outcomes. We highlight these opportunities while identifying open challenges involving goal inference, socio-cognitive frictions, alignment, privacy, and governance.|[2611.08757](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08757)|
|**2026-11-09**|**CrowdDirector: Runtime Orchestration of Crowd Simulation through Natural-Language Directives**|Xiaohan Sun et.al.|Natural language offers an intuitive way to direct a virtual crowd while a simulation is running. Handwritten rules execute efficiently but cannot respond flexibly to varied instructions, whereas asking a large language model (LLM) to control the crowd at every update introduces repeated latency, cost, and the risk of unsupported actions. We present CrowdDirector, a framework that uses an LLM only when the user adds or changes an instruction. The LLM translates the request into a structured instruction, and a scene validator checks whether the entities and actions it references exist in the current Unity scene. Accepted instructions are stored and remain continuously active as the simulation is running. At each simulation tick, a graph neural network uses the current scene state and active instruction to choose an available action and target for each agent, while Unity handles movement, interactions, and animation. The network is trained offline on simulated scenarios, with LLM guidance on how agents should respond to instructions. Our evaluations show the scene validator's feasibility decisions matched human judgements more often than the LLM's own assessment, and on a behavioural benchmark CrowdDirector follows user instructions more closely than handwritten rules while avoiding repeated LLM calls. It therefore provides a practical middle ground between fixed rules and continuous LLM control.|[2611.08763](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08763)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Conflux: Preventing Privilege Escalation with Principal Contexts and System-Level Evaluation**|Raya Buckley et.al.|LLM agents combine information from multiple principals while acting externally. We formalise privilege escalation (PE) as the system-level security objective: an action may proceed only if every principal that influenced it would allow it. Model-level defences remain probabilistic, so enforcement must sit outside model judgement. We present Conflux, a principal-context defence. Its kernel, ITES, derives authority from provenance and the organisation's existing access-control system and intersects the permissions of every influencing principal. We prove a conditional authority-confinement theorem: under complete mediation, no-laundering provenance, a known non-empty decision context, fresh permission checks, and action binding, the kernel admits no action that escalates privilege with respect to any recorded influencing principal. The theorem covers the represented value-influence fragment; control influence from tool, branch, recipient, or plan-step selection remains a refinement obligation. Conflux extends the kernel with independent policy ports, argument-aware authorisation, disclosure, consent, delegation, restriction, and exposure planning. We describe two system- level evaluators. SLED is a black-box evaluator: it exhaustively explores a declared finite model of a defence up to a bound. SLED-V is a white-box evaluator: it checks a serialisable intermediate representation against security and provenance properties. The evidence is bounded and partial. The appendix reports retained AgentDojo, SLED, and SLED-V results, including the limits of those runs. No result in this paper is an unbounded deployment guarantee.|[2611.08768](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08768)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Federated training performs as well as centralized for 3D Latent Diffusion for Diagnosis-Conditioned Brain MRI Synthesis**|Abhijith Shaji et.al.|Generative models of brain MRI are useful to understand brain conditions, and to enrich scarce and imbalanced neuroimaging data. However, training these models requires large, multi-site training sets, which is challenging. Federated learning (FL) allows for training deep learning models without sharing data. We show that training a 3D conditional latent model federatedly (where data is kept private at each site) performs similarly to centralized training (where all the data have been copied to a single site). We evaluate three metrics: conditional fidelity (how well generated MRIs perform in classification), diversity (how the generated images match the real data distribution, using MultiScale-SSIM), and realism (how close generated images are to real images, using Fr\'{e}chet Inception Distance). Our results show that federated training is a viable route to develop multi-site generative models of brain MRI.|[2611.08771](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08771)|
|**2026-11-09**|**On Lipschitz Explosion in Deep Neural Networks with Normalization: Consequences for Optimization and Adversarial Robustness**|Ashkan Soleymani et.al.|Can a network look stable at every layer and still be exponentially unstable as a whole? The Lipschitz constant of a neural network is a basic stability quantity appearing in optimization guarantees, generalization bounds, and robustness certificates. Deep networks naturally admit two such notions, input Lipschitzness, measuring sensitivity to data perturbations, and parameter Lipschitzness, measuring sensitivity to weight perturbations. We prove that, for deep networks with normalization layers, with batch normalization as the canonical example, both Lipschitz constants can grow exponentially with depth and hence with parameter dimension, even under strong per-layer norm control such as $\|W_\ell\|_2\le 1$, and already for linear activations. Thus, a mechanism widely used to stabilize training can create severe worst-case instabilities that are invisible locally, i.e., from layerwise norm bounds alone. Parameter-Lipschitz explosion turns Lipschitz-dependent nonsmooth optimization guarantees into exponential-in-dimension bounds for deep normalized networks, while input-Lipschitz explosion makes worst-case Lipschitz-based generalization and robustness certificates vacuous. The latter also yields a concrete rank-separation mechanism for adversarial vulnerability. The theory predicts that perturbations introducing new singular directions should be amplified much more strongly than perturbations at the same noise level that remain within the input's original singular subspace. Experiments on MNIST, Fashion-MNIST, and CIFAR-10 support this prediction, showing that rank-creating perturbations cause substantially sharper drops in accuracy, confidence, and margins than same-subspace perturbations.|[2611.08775](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08775)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Guardrail Erosion in Domain Fine-Tuned Large Language Models: A Paired Audit of Knowledge-Centric Abstention in Biomedical LLMs**|Stephen Peng et.al.|The number of domain fine-tuned models, particularly in the biomedical domain, is rapidly expanding. Fine-tuning promises high-performing models at a fraction of the cost of training a base model, but it can also produce unintended behavioral changes. Multiple studies have shown that safety guardrails can erode with fine-tuning, yet large-scale studies across model architectures, data mixtures, and fine-tuning methodologies remain limited. Where prior work in this space has studied value-centric refusal, this work focuses on knowledge-centric abstention. We audit 25 biomedical domain fine-tuned large language models against 11 paired base checkpoints on MedAbstain and ClinDet-Bench to evaluate the impact of fine-tuning on knowledge-centric abstention. We find that defined appropriate abstention measured by these benchmarks, already low in base models (median 8.3% on MedAbstain), falls further after fine-tuning (median 4.2%, decreasing in 21 of 25 pairs on MedAbstain), and that the magnitude and direction of change are heterogeneous across pairs. These results motivate fine-tuning methods that improve domain-specific capability while preserving appropriate caution.|[2611.08780](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08780)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Learning from How Users Interpret: Personalizing Recommendation Explanation Order**|Deo Munduku et.al.|Personalized explanations typically adapt their content or presentation based on preferences, interaction histories, or other user characteristics. We examine whether regularities inferred from prior textual interpretations can support personalization of explanation organization. In a controlled within participant study with 122 participants, prior interpretations were represented along three dimensions: information selection, relative salience, and relational organization. The relational dimension was partially operationalized through co mobilization. These representations were aggregated into fixed historical profiles and used to organize otherwise identical explanation sentences. Participants encountered four organization procedures: the OWN historical profile, the DONOR historical profile, a population profile called POP, and a language model procedure using the historical profile called PROFILE LLM. Post exposure objective comprehension was the primary outcome. OWN achieved 86.5 percent correct responses, compared with 66.0 percent for DONOR, 63.9 percent for POP, and 73.0 percent for PROFILE LLM. Mixed effects analyses showed higher comprehension with OWN than with each alternative procedure. No reliable overall condition effect was detected for recommendation acceptance. These findings indicate that historical profiles derived from prior interpretations can provide an actionable signal for personalizing explanation order while keeping explanatory content and wording fixed.|[2611.08785](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08785)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Training Stability under Adaptive Curricula**|Johan Obando-Ceron et.al.|Unsupervised Environment Design (UED) approaches train reinforcement learning (RL) agents on diverse curricula that may evolve with their capabilities. While prior work has largely focused on how these curricula are constructed, we study whether their students remain trainable under the resulting distributions. Across multiple UED mechanisms, environment complexities, and extended training horizons, we identify recurring learner-side pathologies, including parameter growth, degraded value--outcome ordering, and loss of representational diversity, that accompany stagnation and performance degradation. These findings connect limitations in UED to optimization failures previously identified in conventional deep RL, while showing how adaptive curricula couple the learner's optimization state to its future training distribution. Controlled interventions show that mitigating selected pathologies improves learning. Stabilizing the learner improves long-horizon performance and zero-shot generalization without changing the curriculum rule or increasing environment interaction. Our results establish learner trainability as a complementary design principle for UED. Effective curricula require students that remain capable of learning from the distributions they produce.|[2611.08787](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08787)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Not All Latents Are Equal: Importance-Weighted Latent Graph Generation**|Biraaj Rout et.al.|Graph generation has shown tremendous effectiveness in modeling complex scientific tasks ranging from molecule generation to optimized circuit design. Most recent advancements have developed models that learn to denoise noisy graphs using either discrete diffusion or flow matching. However, discrete graph diffusion scales quadratically with the number of nodes, making it difficult to use such models on larger graphs. On the other hand, latent graph generation instead seeks to denoise noisy graph latents, which scale better to more nodes due to their lower dimension. However, in practice, latent graph generation models lag behind their discrete counterparts in performance. We study this problem, finding that it is caused by the unequal distribution of that different latent dimensions have on graph reconstruction. the impact. We observe that the vast majority of impact is contained in only a smaller number of latent dimensions. Since equal emphasis is placed on reconstructing the entire latent in the loss function, existing models are optimized to reconstruct information irrelevant to the downstream task. To tackle this problem, we propose Latent Importance Reweighting ({\bf LIR)} which weights each latent dimensions via it's importance to final graph reconstruction. Importance is calculated as the relative influence of a single latent unit on graph reconstruction, which is represented via the Jacobian of the decoder relative to the latent. Our method is model-agnostic and requires no additional model parameters or hyperparameters. Experiments show that LIR can boost performance and lead to faster training convergence when applied to two different autoencoders on various benchmark datasets. Code: https://github.com/biraaj/GraphGenLatentReweight|[2611.08788](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08788)|
|**2026-11-09**|**SteerGR: Steering Generative Recommendation via Sparse Autoencoders for Controllable Game Discovery**|Sunkyung Lee et.al.|Generative recommendation aims to predict item identifiers from user interaction histories, but explicit control over the properties of recommended items remains underexplored. A key challenge is to connect human-readable concepts to internal features whose interventions effectively change the recommended items. To address this, we propose Steerable Generative Recommender (SteerGR), a framework that uses sparse autoencoders for concept-level control in game discovery. SteerGR proceeds through three stages: (i) Concept Definition and Grounding for defining play concepts with an LLM; (ii) Feature Discovery and Selection for discovering candidate features by their concept associations and selecting one by its steering effect; and (iii) Sparse Feature Intervention for amplifying or suppressing selected features to steer recommendations without retraining the recommender. Extensive experiments with two industrial generative recommendation models on a real-world game dataset show that the selected features successfully steer recommendations toward or away from target concepts. We believe this work takes a first step to extend SAE-based steering from LLMs to industrial generative recommendation.|[2611.08790](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08790)|
|**2026-11-09**|**MotionDecipher: LLM-Assisted Clinical Interpretation of Human Motion Signals for Efficient Gait Analysis**|Mingrui Ma et.al.|Clinical gait and motion analysis is widely used to guide targeted interventions for patients with neurological disorders, the outcome of which is typically a report with descriptions of joint movements and forces. Current practices rely on experts' manual effort, which is labor-intensive and time-consuming. Recent advancements in Large Language Models (LLMs) provide opportunities to address this problem, but translating human motion into clinical gait descriptions remains challenging because the quality of the context directly affects LLMs' reasoning capability over continuous motion signals. To tackle this, we introduce MotionDecipher, a novel framework bridging the semantic gap between motion data and clinical interpretations. Specifically, we made 3 key advancements: 1) a Human Motion Tokenizer that converts motion signals into domain-specific tokens for time- and frequency-informed gait interpretation; 2) a Meta Feature Selector that transforms tokens into Structured Representations; and 3) a Hybrid Context Constructor to determine the optimal demonstration-based prompt for In-Context Learning. We evaluated MotionDecipher on data collected at an accredited gait lab from 122 patients and 46 controls. Results show that MotionDecipher achieves 0.91 BERTScore (13% over GPT-5), while expert evaluation ranks MotionDecipher highest in Correctness, Completeness, and Conciseness compared to baselines, highlighting its promise for efficient gait analysis.|[2611.08795](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08795)|
|**2026-11-09**|**What Matters for LLM Persona Simulation: Signal Form in Cold-Start E-Commerce Advertising**|Jiwoo Choi et.al.|Estimating advertising performance in cold-start settings is challenging because historical logs are unavailable before launch. Large language models (LLMs) offer a potential alternative by simulating persona-conditioned user responses, but it remains unclear which design choices actually drive LLM-based persona simulation. Using real Google Ads e-commerce campaign data across five demographic segments, we identify three signal-form dimensions that affect simulation quality: (i) a demographic anchor without noisy rich attributes, (ii) a relative qualitative banner-blindness signal rather than quantitative anchors, and (iii) prompt phrasing. On this campaign, our method attains an MAE of 24.9 and a MAPE of 26.1%, a 51.3% lower MAE than the best tested non-LLM baseline and 77.3% lower than an LLM configuration using explicit CTR anchors. Systematic ablations show that minimal demographic descriptions achieve lower mean error than richer persona configurations, while relative qualitative cues substantially outperform the tested quantitative anchors. We additionally compare these configurations with direct-prior and rule-based baselines and with alternative LLM configurations.|[2611.08840](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08840)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Adaptive Scheduling Pipeline for Multi-Instance Asynchronous Reinforcement Learning**|Salah Chikhi et.al.|We present the Adaptive Scheduling Pipeline (ASP), an offline rollout scheduler for multi-instance asynchronous reinforcement learning (RL) of large language models (LLMs). ASP jointly reduces three sources of rollout underutilization - padding bubbles from intra-minibatch length heterogeneity, skewness bubbles from long-tail outputs, and sorting bubbles from fluctuations in training-queue occupancy induced by length-sorted dispatch - through a single offline preprocessing pass. This enables efficient multi-instance rollout that overcomes the early scaling plateau of single-instance inference while smoothing the arrival of generated samples at the trainer. We integrate the scheduler into an asynchronous architecture that decouples rollout from training while keeping policy lag observable and bounded. Complete ASP improves end-to-end throughput by up to 3.22 times and reduces iteration time by up to 76.8% relative to MindSpeed-RL, while reducing iteration time by up to 62.5% relative to verl. Within ASP, scheduling reduces mean iteration time by 10.9%. ASP also scales more robustly with additional generation resources than these baselines and shows no visible convergence degradation in the measured bounded-lag regime. ASP has since been used in an internal telecom-domain RL post-training workflow, reducing the reported training period from more than three months to about two months and producing a domain-enhanced model for knowledge answering and tool calling.|[2611.08864](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08864)|
|**2026-11-09**|**The Relational Capacity Framework: A Lens for AI-Mediated Relationality**|Emily Tseng et.al.|A growing literature examines the impact of engaging with AI chatbots on our relationality: our ability to form relationships with others, maintain them through life's changes, and exit when needed. Currently, substantial work focuses on purpose-built AI companions (e.g. Replika, Character.AI), with less work investigating socioemotional uses of the more widely-used general-purpose chatbots. In this paper, we present a 100-person investigation into how people engage general-purpose AI for socioemotional needs. Participants' AI uses varied, from practicing specific social skills to seeking additional bandwidth and information as they navigated their social contexts. To explain this heterogeneity, we provide 8 states that describe how AI expands or contracts relationality for people with different levels of skills, bandwidth, and information. We then present a unified conceptual model for the role of technology in mediating human relationality, the Relational Capacity Framework, and outline future research designing appropriate roles for AI in human connection.|[2611.08877](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08877)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Who's Asking? Audience-Conditioned Privatization**|Daniil Filienko et.al.|The abundance of user-generated conversational data makes the question of how to responsibly derive insights from confidential data without putting the individuals in it at risk ever more important. However, the majority of existing differentially private (DP) synthetic text generation (SDG) and anonymization approaches ignore the context of the data's downstream usage, which results in loss of important information. DP-SDG provides the same guarantee regardless of the recipient, and anonymization relies on predefined attribute categories. They both ignore the observation that an attribute that is harmless to one audience can be sensitive to another, and each audience typically needs only some attributes preserved. We propose Ext-Classify-Priv, a framework that (1) extracts the information types present in a corpus, (2) classifies which of them are private for a given audience following contextual integrity, and (3) protects only those attributes, using either anonymization or SDG with Feature-DP approaches. On Reddit data with 3 audiences, our audience-specific releases are more faithful than a single audience-agnostic release for every audience. On bioRxiv data, our SDG method improves MAUVE from 0.097 to 0.420 at epsilon=1 and on the proprietary real estate data, our anonymization method improves MAUVE from 0.698 to 0.959 over the best baselines.|[2611.08884](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08884)|
|**2026-11-09**|**HomeLoop: A Simulation Framework and Benchmark for Robot-IoT Coordination in Everyday Domestic Assistance and Safety**|Ramy ElMallah et.al.|Recent progress in embodied robotics is bringing robots closer to deployment in homes, as vision-language models (VLMs) are demonstrating significant advances in reasoning, making VLM-driven robots promising home assistant agents. These agents will often be introduced into homes that already have cameras, Internet of Things (IoT) devices, and residents with wearables. To fulfill their potential, they need to integrate with existing smart infrastructure and reason through ambiguous scenarios: a smoke alarm may come from a pan about to ignite, a pan already on fire, a resident cooking or a faulty sensor, and each calls for a different response. To that end, we introduce HomeLoop, a simulation framework and benchmark built on Isaac Sim that combines robot bodies, networked devices and wearables, simulated residents and visitors, faulty sensors, and hazards that evolve over time. Our benchmark is composed of 20 tasks across five families grounded in real-world household hazards and needs, and designed so that the same alert can have different causes. A runtime monitor scores each episode with temporal-logic checks on the final outcome and the actions along the way, and every action carries a predefined cost. We evaluate six open-weight and proprietary VLMs. The best achieves a family-balanced score of 61.9%, and across models we see a pattern of over-reaction: failed episodes involving prohibited interventions outnumber those involving missed required interventions by more than two to one, with models often shutting off the stove before investigating the alarm's cause. HomeLoop is an extensible foundation intended for use in research on robot-IoT coordination, long-term memory, proactive assistance, and privacy-safety trade-offs. HomeLoop will be released with 2,100 home layouts that can be automatically furnished in diverse configurations, five robot bodies and 20 networked device types.|[2611.08914](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08914)|
|**2026-11-09**|**On the Incompleteness of do-calculus for Universal Identifiability**|Yaroslav Kivva et.al.|We study the problem of causal effect identification from an arbitrary set of observational and/or interventional distributions, which we call Universal Identifiability (uID). The uID problem generalizes existing classical identifiability (ID) and general identifiability (gID) problems, which considered the identification problem from either mere observational distributions or a specific type of interventional distributions. Prior research has established graphical criteria to determine when a causal effect is identifiable in ID and gID problems. Moreover, algorithms that leverage do-calculus and basic probability manipulations have been proposed to compute these effects. Consequently, it has been shown that do-calculus is both sound and complete in these problems. Despite these well-established results, a formal definition of what it means for the do-calculus to be complete does not exist. We propose a rigorous formalization for the completeness of the do-calculus and show that the completeness results in previous work remain valid. However, for more general problems, such as uID, the rules of do-calculus are no longer complete. This is perhaps the reason why the uID problem has remained open thus far.|[2611.08916](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08916)|
|**2026-11-09**|**DRAFT-Bench: Evaluating Frontier Agents Across the Professional Design Lifecycle**|Rong Pan et.al.|Can frontier general-purpose agents understand client intent, explore creative directions, execute revisions, and prepare production deliverables? We introduce DRAFT-Bench (Design Reliability Across Feedback and Tasks), a lifecycle-oriented evaluation of requirement elicitation, creative exploration, constrained editing, iterative revision, and production handoff. Its Requirement-to-Artifact framework links evolving requirements, protected properties, artifact versions, and delivery obligations. We compare native Codex/Astra and Claude Code across 35 design briefs under 43 conditions, alongside a separate 40-scenario RFQ track. Astra completes 43/43 design conditions and Claude 35/43. All scored core runs receive full structural credit, yet on the 28 core conditions scored for both systems, semantic checks flag violations in 11 for Astra and 5 for Claude; these are model judgments, not expert-confirmed failure rates. RFQ coverage is high, but success ordering changes across cohorts. SKU expansion, revision dependencies, and White ink files expose ambiguity among unmet obligations, missing inputs, and evaluator interpretations. These findings separate execution, requirement compliance, and professional acceptance. Unpaired tracks and different tools preclude claims of RFQ-to-delivery success, factory acceptance, or a pure model ranking.|[2611.08933](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08933)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Ordered Policy Optimization**|Dongkyu Derek Cho et.al.|Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO) stabilizes policy updates by clipping the importance ratio between the new policy and the data-collecting policy. The standard rule uses one fixed clipping range for all samples. While this choice offers simplicity and effectiveness, it fails to distinguish statistically harmful extreme ratio values from large, yet tolerable changes. We study how to adapt the clipping radius to the reliability of the induced importance ratio distribution. Our case study demonstrates that reliable updates require controlling these extreme ratios, rather than merely verifying that individual ratios fall within a fixed interval. Motivated by this observation, we propose Ordered Policy Optimization (\textsc{OPO}). In each minibatch, \textsc{OPO} sorts the importance ratios, selects the extreme ratios that potentially have the largest effect on the update, and assigns them adaptive sample-wise radii instead of using the fixed clipping radius. These radii are derived from a prescribed tail profile, which specifies desired target values for the selected extreme ratios. The resulting objective remains first-order and requires only minibatch sorting and a modified policy loss. Experiments conducted on MuJoCo and Atari benchmarks demonstrate that this simple modification achieves strong performance relative to established baselines.|[2611.08936](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08936)|
|**2026-11-09**|**More with Less: Efficient Parallel Scientific Code Translation via an Agent-Skill Workflow**|Jianuo Shen et.al.|Translating parallel scientific codes between HPC programming frameworks is a critical but labor-intensive task. Existing LLM-based approaches rely on fixed sequential pipelines with undifferentiated repair strategies and correctness criteria that do not match scientific computing practice. We present an agent-skill framework that separates translation, compilation diagnosis, and execution validation into three autonomous agents coordinated by a verdict-driven orchestrator, routing each failure to a targeted repair skill rather than retrying blindly. An automated output check drives the self-correction loop, complemented by an offline LLM-based verification that exposes false positives escaping compiler and runtime checks. We evaluate the framework on 30 HeCBench kernels translated bidirectionally among CUDA, OpenMP, and SYCL with three open-source LLMs. Our framework attains the higher verified correctness rate in all 18 model and direction combinations over a state-of-the-art baseline while consuming up to 52.4% fewer tokens, remaining consistent across models and directions without architectural modification.|[2611.08947](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08947)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Phantom Consensus: Measuring Social Reasoning in Multi-Agent LLM Decision-Making**|Disha Sheshanarayana et.al.|Multi-agent LLM systems increasingly aggregate judgments across model instances, but reasoning about other agents can itself introduce failure. We study a setting in which LLMs shift their public decisions after observing ambiguous social signals while reporting inaccurate estimates of what other agents privately judged, even when the group's recorded private judgments do not support the apparent consensus. We call this failure phantom consensus and study it as a pluralistic-ignorance-style pattern in controlled LLM collectives. To measure this phenomenon, we introduce PHANTOM-Bench, a benchmark that separates isolated private judgments, public decisions after ambiguous social signals, elicited estimates of the group's private judgment, transparency-based recovery, and controls for neutral public text and explicit vote-following. Across nine closed- and open-weight models, ambiguous public signals consistently degrade decision quality and can turn correct private group judgments into incorrect public collective outcomes. Public accuracy drops by as much as 47.8 percentage points, and collective rationality failures reach 47.3% in the most susceptible setting. We observe substantial errors in reported group-private beliefs alongside public-stage failures, while control conditions reveal model-dependent differences in susceptibility to ambiguous and explicit peer signals. Revealing the agents' recorded private decisions repairs many failures without revealing the gold label, although recovery varies across models. Our results show that evaluating multi-agent LLM systems requires measuring not only final agreement, but whether that agreement reflects independently measured private judgments or an apparent consensus.|[2611.08971](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08971)|
|**2026-11-09**|**LEF-JEPA: Denoising-Trained Latent Energy Fields for Spatial Representation Learning**|Zhiyuan Liu et.al.|A joint-embedding predictive architecture (JEPA) that applies prediction supervision only at masked positions struggles to clearly capture object shapes and boundaries in its latent representations. We further observe that some existing methods improve spatial structure while also retaining rich variations in texture and color. However, preserving more low-level visual information does not necessarily make representations better suited to prediction and action. This raises a key question: how can we strengthen the spatial structure in latent representations that supports prediction and action without over-encoding these details? We introduce LEF-JEPA, which learns spatial structure through target-guided latent recovery and transfers this supervision to the visual encoder through a relative-energy constraint; the energy module is used only during pretraining and adds no inference overhead. To reduce the cost of energy optimization during pretraining, we further construct a conditional Ising Hamiltonian that allows low-energy-state search to be performed by specialized Ising solvers. On physical hardware, we validate the feasibility of achieving lower forward latency with fewer local updates. Under a unified CIFAR-10 pretraining and frozen-encoder evaluation protocol, both realizations outperform V-JEPA2.1 in image classification, foreground segmentation, and visual planning.|[2611.08989](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08989)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Same Facts, Different Remedy: Linguistic Profiling and Arbitrariness in LLM and System One Decisions**|Colin Priest et.al.|Large language models are increasingly used to resolve consumer complaints, and the way complainants write varies with age, education and socioeconomic status (SES). We audit three remedy-decision systems using sociolinguistic rewrites of US consumer financial complaints that are instructed to preserve every fact: two generative LLMs that return a single label (GPT-4o-mini and GPT-5-mini) and a probabilistic "System One" model that returns a distribution over remedies (Jev). Using pre-registered hypotheses tested once on held-out complaints, we separate two phenomena that single-label audits conflate. At the group scale, older, college-educated and higher-SES styles receive slightly more generous remedies; these effects replicate but are small, with every monetary-remedy gap within 0.5 percentage points. At the individual scale, two style versions of the same complaint receive different monetary decisions 2.6-6.3% of the time. In a variance-component analysis of the probabilistic model, persona components shared across the two rewrite models account for about 1.6% of within-complaint variation. The persona gaps are associated mainly with delivery (vocabulary, grammar, politeness) rather than content. Positive controls show that all three systems respond 49-387 times more strongly to remedy-relevant facts than to style. Rewriting every complaint into one standard style reduced GPT-4o-mini's education and SES gaps by 94% and 87% while retaining fact sensitivity, but reversed two of the probabilistic model's gaps and did not make individual decisions more stable. Independent LLM auditors flagged material fact changes in 3.3% and 11.7% of rewrites, and instability persisted among unflagged rewrites. |**2026-11-09**|**Seeing vs. Counting: Complexity Twins Show Where Multimodal LLMs Fail on Diagrams**|Yin Liu et.al.|When a multimodal LLM miscounts in a diagram, does it fail at seeing or at counting? Existing visual graph and puzzle benchmarks vary visual density and algorithmic hardness together, confounding the two. We pair counting questions about the same drawing that sit in different counting-complexity classes, such as its spanning trees (a determinant) and its simple paths (#P-complete), and use a transcription probe and a text-only control to separate reading from computing. We test Gemini, Gemma and Qwen3-VL models; for the Gemini models, the answer depends on how the model computes. In chain of thought, most of them read small drawings correctly and then fail at counting, at a rate set far more by the size of the answer than by its complexity class (for the primary model, the odds of an exact answer fall 2.38-fold per decade of the answer). Given code and an exact transcription, the primary model counts correctly even on 10x10 grids, and reading becomes the bottleneck: a single view misses horizontal edges, and a second reading of a rotated copy with crop checks reads 86/96 large grids exactly, against 57/96 when the upright reading is simply repeated.|[2611.08998](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08998)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Capital Efficiency, Isolation, Risk, and Ruin in Generative AI Inference Serving Infrastructure**|Ren\'ee St. Amant et.al.|Generative AI serving is an extremely capital-intensive endeavor that demands rapid, step-change improvements in capital efficiency to prevent industry wide increases in the cost of capital from limiting growth plans. Keeping pace with the economic necessities will increasingly require infrastructure providers to confront operationally-inconvenient realities of production serving, including the extreme heterogeneity of generative AI request costs. We have explored workload isolation as a foundational, fleet-level lever for increasing capital efficiency. Isolation is the practice of intelligently grouping "like" requests before execution. Rather than exposing every replica to the full heterogeneous workload distribution, isolation deliberately reshapes the workload presented to the hardware by assigning requests with different cost characteristics to different portions of the fleet. Workload isolation on its own does not make expensive requests cheaper; it prevents a costly portion of the workload from unnecessarily determining the operating point and capital efficiency of the entire fleet. It additionally lays the foundational groundwork that can serve as a launching point for compounding gains via narrow-band optimizations (tailoring) and custom silicon designs while also informing up on pricing policy. This paper uses representative production data and experience to convey the motivation behind isolation as a fleet-level lever for increased capital efficiency.|[2611.08999](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08999)|
|**2026-11-09**|**FoundaRF: A Physics-Informed Foundation Model Framework for Radio-Frequency Passive Components**|Houbo He et.al.|Designing RF/mmWave passive components is a time-consuming process that relies heavily on expert knowledge and iterative EM simulations. Although recent ML surrogate models have shown great promise in accelerating passive design, they remain task-specific and lack generalization. This paper presents FoundaRF, a foundation model framework that unifies passive layout modeling across diverse layout structures and technologies. It combines a self-supervised encoder with adaptive layout processing and physics-aware decoders to enable few-shot transfer. Experiments across four semiconductor technologies show strong generalization to unseen structures, outperforming baselines with up to a 3.3x reduction in prediction error, reducing labeled-data requirements by up to fourfold, and substantially shortening design turnaround. FoundaRF establishes a scalable and data-efficient solution for RF/mmWave passive design automation.|[2611.09003](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09003)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Did I See It or Think It? Probing Reality Monitoring in Vision-Language Models with Concept Injection**|Tina Khezresmaeilzadeh et.al.|Recent work tests whether language models can introspect by injecting a concept direction into their activations and asking whether they notice it and can name it. These studies are text-only. Vision-language models (VLMs) face an additional problem, known in cognitive psychology as reality monitoring: they must keep internally generated content separate from what they actually see. We therefore ask: can VLMs distinguish injected concepts from image content? We introduce a paradigm that varies the image and the injected concept independently. Across six VLMs, only Gemma-3-27B reliably identifies injected concepts (35% vs. 3% for steering alone), with almost no false alarms. Even then, the source it reports is fragile. |**2026-11-09**|**Optimal Probabilistic Answer Set Programming: Complexity and Implementations**|Damiano Azzolini et.al.|Probabilistic Answer Set Programming (PASP) extends Answer Set Programming with the possibility of representing uncertain data. Among the possible available semantics, we focus here on the credal semantics. In this setting, prior work has focused exclusively on plain ASP programs, i.e., programs without optimization constructs. However, many real world tasks define optimality criteria over the possible solutions, which cannot be conveniently modeled with existing probabilistic ASP frameworks. In this paper, we close this gap and propose optimal Probabilistic Answer Set Programming, which extends PASP by also allowing weak constraints, which are the standard optimization construct of ASP. We define the semantics of such programs, study the computational complexity of the inference and most probable explanation tasks, and propose practical implementations based on Algebraic Model Counting and Answer Set Programming with Quantifiers. An empirical evaluation of the proposed approach on three real world tasks confirms the applicability of our framework.|[2611.09022](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09022)|
|**2026-11-09**|**A Controlled Study of Models, Skills, Feedback, and Curriculum in Agentic Robotics**|Kaiyuan Chen et.al.|Agentic robotics asks coding agents to implement and iteratively improve robot policies from simulated and real-world physical interactions. We study the self-learning loop of agentic robotics through a controlled simulation study spanning nine manipulation tasks, 631 different experiment configurations, and 28K coding agent rollouts, systematically varying model capability, skill priors, feedback structure, and curriculum. The experiment data suggests that the bottleneck is often not the access to sufficient information, but interpreting it into the right policy update. Across these factors, a successful self-learning loop depends on how effectively agents attribute failures, reason task-relevant spatial and geometric structure, and selectively restructure code to improve policies.|[2611.09030](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09030)|
|**2026-11-09**|**ToolDex: An Ego-Exo Human Demonstration Dataset for Creative Physical Tool Use**|Mohamed Elmoghany et.al.|Creative tool use is a fundamental human capability and an important step toward general-purpose embodied intelligence. It requires agents to reason about object affordances, physical constraints, and manipulation strategies, especially when standard tools are unavailable. However, progress in this area is limited by the lack of real-world datasets that capture human tool-use behavior with sufficient scale, fidelity, and diversity. Existing benchmarks are often simulation-based, focus on simplified interactions, or lack detailed observations and annotations. We introduce ToolDex, a large-scale egocentric-exocentric (ego-exo) human demonstration dataset for creative physical tool use. ToolDex captures scenarios in which humans select, adapt, and repurpose available objects as tools. Using synchronized egocentric cameras and eight exocentric cameras, the dataset provides high-fidelity multi-view observations and rich annotations for hands, tools, target objects, and their deformable interactions. Beyond data collection, we establish a benchmark suite characterizing three core capabilities: physical tool-use reasoning, sparse-view 3D and 4D reconstruction, and robot policy learning. ToolDex provides a foundation for studying human tool-use behavior and developing embodied agents that can adaptively use tools in complex real-world environments. Dataset visualizations and benchmarking results are available at https://tooluse-dataset.github.io/.|[2611.09036](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09036)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Failure Profiling and Reachable Trajectory Selection for Reasoning Distillation**|Yijun Zhu et.al.|Training smaller language models on reasoning solutions generated by a larger teacher model is the prevailing approach for transferring mathematical reasoning capabilities. However, the student tends to overfit the output distribution of the teacher and generalizes poorly when its own behavior at inference time deviates from the training data. Recent methods attempt to bridge this gap by incorporating error data, yet the corrective trajectories still reflect the reasoning patterns of the teacher, which the student cannot reliably reproduce, leaving the distribution mismatch largely unresolved. We propose a method, Failure Profiling and Reachable Trajectory Selection (FPRT), built on the insight that effective training data should be jointly determined by what the student fails on and what it can realistically learn. Our method first profiles the failure behavior of the student on each problem through repeated sampling, characterizing it in terms of error rate and error consistency to reveal qualitatively distinct failure modes. Each mode demands a fundamentally different training signal. The diagnosed mode then guides candidate generation from the teacher via distinct prompting strategies tailored to each failure type. Among the correct candidates, our method selects the one whose initial reasoning steps after the point of divergence are most reproducible by the student. These first corrective steps constitute the primary bottleneck, while the remaining steps follow an already corrected context and can therefore be continued more readily. This contrasts with standard reranking approaches, which score the trajectory as a whole and thereby dilute the signal at the critical reasoning transition. Experiments demonstrate that our method achieves the SOTA performance across the five benchmarks on both student backbones.|[2611.09042](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09042)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Equidistribution of Solution Gradients in Physics-Informed Neural Networks via Coordinate Transformations**|Tianchi Yu et.al.|Physics-Informed Neural Networks (PINNs) often struggle to approximate solutions with sharp gradients due to optimization difficulties and spectral bias. Motivated by the principle of gradient equidistribution from the stretching approach in grid generation, which redistributes solution gradients to reduce functional complexity, we propose Coordinate Transformation PINNs (CTPINNs), which incorporate learnable coordinate transformations to map the physical domain into a computational domain where the physical solutions become approximately affine, making them easier for neural networks to learn. We propose two constructions of the transformation: an explicit construction based on Majorants and an implicit construction based on invertable neural networks. Experimentally, the explicit construction can improve accuracy for PDEs with available explicit coordinate transformations, and the implicit construction can be combined with other advanced PINN models as an efficient plug-in module to improve performance across a range of challenging PDE benchmarks.|[2611.09053](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09053)|
|**2026-11-09**|**ProtoCouncil: Multi-Encoder Anatomical Prototypes for Prostate Cancer Detection in Micro-Ultrasound**|Mohammad Mahdi Abootorabi et.al.|Purpose: Micro-ultrasound ($\mu$US) is a promising modality for prostate cancer (PCa) detection, but identifying suspicious tissue depends heavily on clinical experience. Models trained on sparse, noisy core-level pathology offer little insight into their decisions and can fail under domain shift. Clinical use needs a traceable decision process and a flag for uncertain predictions. Methods: We introduce ProtoCouncil, in which three foundation encoders (MedSAM, DINOv3 and V-JEPA 2.1) learn case-based prototypes at three anatomical levels: whole gland, needle track and gland sectors. Prototypes are projected onto real training-core regions, and label-free fusion keeps every class logit as a bias plus an exact sum of prototype similarity contributions. Encoder disagreement triggers selective prediction. Results: In five-fold patient-level cross-validation (6,588 cores, 693 patients), ProtoCouncil reaches 82.9% AUROC for clinically significant PCa (csPCa, Grade Group $\geq$3), 4.3 points above the best previously published method. In zero-shot evaluation on two external cohorts, ProtoCouncil achieves higher all-cancer and csPCa AUROC than all previous methods. Nearest same-class exemplars match test-core tumor involvement better than random same-class cases. Externally, csPCa accuracy falls from 79% when encoders agree to 27% when two oppose the fused call. Abstaining on disagreement raises balanced accuracy by 7 and 15 points, while retaining 58% and 54% of the cores. Conclusion: ProtoCouncil grounds core-level predictions in exact similarity-based evidence from real, anatomically indexed training cases, which makes its reasoning interpretable. It achieves the highest csPCa AUROC across three cohorts and identifies unreliable predictions through encoder disagreement.|[2611.09058](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09058)|
|**2026-11-09**|**UniRGBA: Unifying RGB and Alpha in Pretrained Video Diffusion Models**|Lei Xu et.al.|Recent advances in video diffusion models have achieved remarkable photorealistic RGB synthesis, yet generating videos with a native alpha channel remains an open challenge. We present UniRGBA, a unified framework whose key insight is that RGBA generation does not require a dedicated alpha encoder or complex latent-space restructuring---a minimal, targeted extension of the existing RGB pathway is sufficient. UniRGBA-VAE extends a causal video VAE to 4-channel RGBA via zero-initialized channel expansion, symmetric selective layer unfreezing (5% trainable), and a six-term progressive reconstruction loss anchored by a KL reference loss that prevents latent drift, followed by a two-stage schedule that introduces a lightweight GAN objective. On a visual-effect RGBA benchmark, UniRGBA substantially outperforms Wan-Alpha on RGBA reconstruction while preserving RGB fidelity. For generation, UniRGBA outperforms Wan-Alpha on RGBA text-to-video tasks and matches or surpasses the original pretrained Wan2.1 backbone on RGB-only metrics, demonstrating that native alpha support can be integrated without compromising the underlying RGB prior or the model's architectural versatility.|[2611.09069](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09069)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Large Language Model Deployment on Resource-Constrained Edge Devices: A Practitioners Survey**|Rahul Maliakkal et.al.|Large Language Models (LLMs) have transformed many areas of artificial intelligence (AI), but their substantial resource requirements limit their deployment on mobile and edge devices. Although LLMs have demonstrated impressive capabilities in various applications, the next challenge is deploying these models on resource-limited edge devices. Recent innovations in software optimizations and specialized hardware have made it feasible to deploy LLMs on edge devices. Edge deployment offers benefits including local data processing, cost savings, reduced latency, and personalized responses. This paper reviews the latest techniques that make the deployment of LLMs on edge devices feasible.|[2611.09075](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09075)|
|**2026-11-09**|**GlobalPuzzle: Globally Informed Genetic Search for Eroded Jigsaw Puzzle Reassembly**|Lunyan Wang et.al.|Jigsaw puzzle reassembly remains a highly challenging task, particularly when piece boundaries are irregularly eroded to simulate damage to cultural artifacts. On the GAP benchmark, existing methods achieve a perfect assembly accuracy of only 0.3% on $5 \times 5$ puzzles, whereas humans can solve jigsaw puzzles with remarkably high accuracy. Humans typically approach puzzle solving by identifying potentially adjacent pieces, evaluating the current assembly, and iteratively performing complex operations such as adding pieces, replacing incorrectly placed pieces, and moving entire connected groups of pieces. However, existing methods face a fundamental limitation: approaches that leverage global visual information typically predict piece arrangements directly, making it difficult to explicitly perform such complex iterative operations, while search based methods can rearrange pieces but primarily rely on pairwise boundary compatibility, lacking global visual information to evaluate and guide the search process. To bridge this gap, we propose GlobalPuzzle, a jigsaw puzzle reassembly framework that integrates global canvas level evaluation, action guidance, and genetic search. By incorporating global visual information into the evaluation and optimization of candidate assemblies, GlobalPuzzle aims to resolve ambiguous adjacency relationships that are difficult to determine from irregularly eroded piece boundaries alone, enabling flexible, globally informed reconstruction.|[2611.09077](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09077)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Training-Free Active Test-Time Adaptation for Vision-Language Models**|Jihwan Bang et.al.|Test-time adaptation (TTA) for vision-language models (VLMs) is usually studied in a fully unsupervised regime, where adaptation relies on pseudo-labels, confidence scores, or entropy computed by the model itself. This is a severe restriction on hard shifted samples: the model is asked to repair its own uncertain predictions without any external evidence. At the other extreme, supervised online adaptation assumes labels can directly train or correct the model. We study the intermediate deployment regime between these two extremes. In Active Test-time Adaptation for VLMs (Active VLM-TTA), a pretrained VLM may spend an explicit labeling budget on selected test samples, but the prediction for the current sample must be emitted before its label can be used; queried labels are therefore costed, delayed evidence for future samples rather than free correction for the present one. Under this future-only constraint, the central question is how to reuse sparse labels conservatively. We instantiate this setting with ACTive VLM-TTA for Online Retrieval (ACTOR), a training-free plug-in module that routes uncertain samples among base prediction, label query, and memory-based correction using a consistency-and-reliability check over previously queried labels. Across ten cross-domain benchmarks and a domain-transfer benchmark based on ImageNet, ACTOR consistently improves strong VLM-TTA baselines on ResNet and ViT backbones with negligible inference overhead. Our implementation is publicly available at https://github.com/kaist-dmlab/ACTOR.|[2611.09084](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09084)|
|**2026-11-09**|**The Invisible Gap: Romanized Indic Generation Fails Where Automatic Metrics Succeed**|Madhu Sudhan Rao Beesetty et.al.|Hundreds of millions of Indians communicate with digital systems in romanized Latin characters -- typing "aap kaise hain" rather than native script -- yet romanized Indic generation is absent from NLP benchmarks and evaluation frameworks. We demonstrate that automatic metrics (BGE-M3, BERTScore) systematically misrepresent the quality of romanized Hindi, Telugu, and Kannada generation. We evaluate four LLMs (Claude Opus 4.8, GPT-5.5, Gemini 2.5-flash, Qwen3.5-35B) on 142 OpenAssistant query-response pairs. On a 72-response diagnostic subset, native speakers rate GPT-5.5 1.43 points higher than Claude Opus 4.8 on a 12-point rubric, whereas their full-dataset BGE-M3 means differ by only 0.004. Two distinct model-specific failure modes emerge: Gemini produces script spillage (16.9% of Telugu responses leak native script), while Qwen collapses to English-only output (16.2% of Kannada responses). We introduce a failure-aware evaluation policy that assigns zero to empty outputs, script-corrupted responses, and instruction collapses -- revealing failures metrics alone cannot detect. Our results show that evaluating romanized Indic generation requires a coordinated bundle: semantic similarity, script compliance, failure coverage, and native-speaker acceptability.|[2611.09085](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09085)|
|**2026-11-09**|**When Routine Operations Break Architectural Pattern Relationships in LLM Agent Harnesses**|Boming Xia et.al.|LLM agent harnesses distribute tool mediation, state management and safety guardrails across native services, independently written plugins and configuration. Routine operations can change the runtime order or retained state that architectural pattern relationships require, without any code change. We study five relationships associated with the Guardrail Enforcement Point, Autonomy Controller and Blackboard patterns across five released harnesses, analysing a main sample of fourteen independently written plugins. Using PatternOps, our assessment procedure, we trace requirements from pattern descriptions, provider documentation and deployment profiles to the providers that realise them and the records that decide them. We then compare alternative allocations under supported operations. Such operations broke relationships silently: after a plugin was reapplied or an allow rule saved, policy stopped evaluating edits while every plugin stayed active, and a prohibited edit executed. Which alternatives qualified depended on the requirement. Allocations that kept the required order, or kept or rebuilt the needed state, held on their tested paths and operation sequences, while others prevented prohibited effects but missed edits rejected elsewhere. In these harnesses, a pattern relationship is a property of the running system and its history, so architects should evaluate it against the decision paths, state lifetimes and operation sequences their deployments use.|[2611.09088](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09088)|
|**2026-11-09**|**AdaAlloc: Adaptive Visual Token Allocation for Long-Video Question Answering**|Joungbin An et.al.|Long-video question answering forces multimodal large language models to reason under strict visual-token budgets, creating a tradeoff between broad temporal coverage and fine-grained spatial detail. Uniform sampling preserves coverage but often obscures decisive local cues, while caption-based or frame-similarity retrieval operates over lossy proxies that can miss motion, state changes, and before-after relations. We recast long-video reasoning as a visual-token allocation problem: given a fixed budget, decide how much to spend on global context versus high-resolution local evidence, and where in the video to draw that local evidence from. We introduce AdaAlloc, a training-free inference framework that adaptively allocates visual tokens between a global video overview and localized visual detail. AdaAlloc first plans a global/local allocation policy, then uses temporal grounding to locate candidate evidence segments and refines them into a compact temporal memory. The selected policy controls both frame sampling density and resolution, combining sparse, lower-resolution global frames with denser, higher-resolution local frames drawn from the refined memory. The final answerer reasons over the allocated global context and refined local evidence. Across LVBench, MLVU, VideoMME, and LongVideoBench, AdaAlloc improves over budget-matched uniform sampling by up to 10.5 percentage points and outperforms the compared frame-selection and agentic methods on their reported benchmarks.|[2611.09092](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09092)|
|**2026-11-09**|**AlienBench: A Benchmark of Structural Novelty in Language Model Outputs**|Jonas Oppenlaender et.al.|Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used for creative tasks such as writing, design, and idea generation. For these uses, it matters whether a model's ideas depart from familiar examples or stay close to them. Novelty is one standard measure of creativity, and current creativity evaluations ask human experts or LLM judges to rate how novel an output is. However, these ratings have no fixed reference for what counts as novel (or, in other terms, departure from the familiar). Measuring this departure requires a task with known familiar defaults. The structured imagination task from cognitive psychology is such a task. In this creativity test, people imagine a creature from another planet. The departure from features of Earth animals, such as bilateral symmetry and legs as mode of locomotion, is used as measure of creativity. We introduce AlienBench, a benchmark that adapts the structured imagination task to language models. Models describe alien creatures, and a panel of three LLM judges extracts ten biological features from the description and labels them as either Earth-typical or a departure. The sum of feature departures is the score of a description, from 0 to 10. Each model describes alien creatures under six prompt conditions that vary the framing of the task and the planetary environment. Each model generates 30 descriptions per condition (180 in total), and its overall score is the mean across these descriptions. We evaluate 10 language models, from frontier to open-weight models. Overall scores range from 3.28 (GPT-6 Luna) to 6.55 (Ministral 3 14B). Interestingly, open-weight models score higher than proprietary models in this task (5.40 vs. 4.58). Under the baseline prompt, the models depart from Earth defaults more often on average than 100 human participants on every feature. We release all prompts, generations, judge outputs, and scores.|[2611.09094](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09094)|
|**2026-11-09**|**SPDMX: Large-Scale Open Stem Audio From Symbolic Music**|Phillip Long et.al.|Large public datasets have driven recent progress in machine learning, from text and vision to speech and general audio. Music remains an outlier, as copyright blocks redistribution of training audio, while many modern tasks need individual stems rather than mixtures. Existing multitrack resources trade off realism, scale, and licensing, while synthetic alternatives rely on proprietary VSTs or naive open rendering that yields weak stems. We introduce SPDMX, a listening-guided rendering of public-domain PDMX scores into an openly-licensed stem corpus with aligned scores and an open render pipeline: $253{,}982$ songs, $490{,}159$ mono stems, and $5{,}469$ song-hours at $44.1$ kHz under CC BY 4.0, with paired MIDI and identifiers linking to PDMX metadata. $88{,}403$ songs ($34.8\%$; $2{,}734$ song-hours) provide multitrack supervision. To our knowledge, this is the largest openly-licensed synthetic multitrack subset by song-hours, enabling source separation, stem-conditioned synthesis, MIDI-to-audio, transcription, and joint symbolic-audio modeling. We release the corpus, metadata, and open pipeline for reuse on other symbolic collections. Demos and experimental notes are on the project page.|[2611.09140](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09140)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Circuit Theory-Informed AI for Condition Monitoring in Power Electronics**|Yubo Song et.al.|Artificial intelligence for power electronics is evolving toward a deeper comprehension of system intrinsic behaviors and dynamics. As a novel integration of neural computation and electric circuits, spike-based neuromorphic computing offers promising potential in high energy efficiency and adaptability for data-driven applications. Building on the latency-coding principles, this article proposes a framework for data-light condition monitoring that relies solely on three-phase AC current measurements. The AC current signals are encoded as membrane potentials of RC-based spiking neurons, which are inherently compatible with spiking neural networks that can effectively facilitate the estimation of device parameters. Case studies are presented to validate the performances, while constraints and further optimizations are also evaluated and discussed, and future extensions of the methodology are provided.|[2611.09141](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09141)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Learning Generative Dynamics for 3D Molecule Generation via Sequential Neuro-Symbolic Constraints**|Zhuoran Li et.al.|Diffusion models have recently emerged as a powerful paradigm for generating 3D molecules. Despite showing remarkable promise, they learn atomic distributions implicitly from data, without explicitly encoding physicochemical rules into their generative dynamics. Consequently, generated samples can be statistically plausible yet chemically invalid. To address this problem, we propose Sequential Neuro-Symbolic Constrained Diffusion (SensDiff), a framework that integrates symbolic rules into generative modeling, enabling the model to internalize scientific priors during training. Central to SensDiff is the observation that the diffusion trajectory of 3D molecule generation exhibits a coarse-to-fine evolution: valid molecules arise by first mitigating macroscopic geometric conflicts and then refining structures toward microscopic chemical consistency through iterative denoising. Accordingly, SensDiff mirrors this dynamic by sequentially enforcing constraints with adaptive weighting during training, progressively steering generation toward valid molecular geometries. Moreover, SensDiff translates domain knowledge into interpretable generative controls, grounding opaque neural denoising in scientific principles. Experiments on molecular benchmarks and real-world constrained generation tasks confirm that SensDiff consistently improves chemical validity while adhering to physicochemical rules and user-specified targets. The code is publicly available at https://github.com/WanyuGroup/NeurIPS2026_SensDiff.|[2611.09142](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09142)|
|**2026-11-09**|**FIND: Frequency Invariance Disentanglement for Test-Time Adaptation in LiDAR 3D Detection**|Dongxiao Li et.al.|LiDAR-based 3D Object Detection (L3OD) is a fundamental 3D perception task, yet its performance is often compromised by domain shifts arising from diverse sensor configurations and environmental conditions. Existing Test-Time Adaptation (TTA) methods primarily leverage self-supervision in the spatial domain, yet they frequently confound semantic content with domain style due to the sparse, non-Euclidean nature of point clouds. To overcome this limitation, we propose FIND (Frequency INvariance Disentanglement), a novel TTA framework that precisely disentangles domain-invariant content from domain-specific interference within the frequency domain. Our methodology utilizes B-Spline fitting to construct locally adaptive filters integrated into a dual-stream architecture: an Invariant Stream extracts robust features to guide pseudo-labeling, while a Specific Stream enforces consistency against domain perturbations. By combining invariance-driven alignment with stability-guided regularization, our approach dynamically extracts robust domain-invariant features while suppressing domain-specific interference. Extensive experiments on cross-dataset adaptation and robust corruption benchmarks demonstrate that FIND significantly outperforms state-of-the-art methods. The source code is publicly available at https://github.com/llldx03/FIND.|[2611.09170](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09170)|
|**2026-11-09**|**SIFR: Stable Signed Flow Reinforcement**|Runlong Liao et.al.|Reward-based fine-tuning of flow models requires effectively promoting high-reward and suppressing low-reward samples, while maintaining training stability over many rounds of iterative updates. Iterative reward fine-tuning provides a simple starting point, but the positive reward weights give no explicit unfitting signal, and repeated reweighting concentrates the distribution and causes collapse. We propose Stable Signed Flow Reinforcement (SIFR), which replaces the reward weights by signed advantages to supply the missing unfitting signal, and constrains the velocity drift from a reference model to stabilize the updates, with a Lagrange multiplier that adapts the regularization strength to a prescribed drift budget. We further show that existing flow RL methods fall within this framework: they implicitly minimize the signed loss, differing in their regularization choices. We validate SIFR on SD3.5 Medium (2.5B) and FLUX.2 Klein (9B) and scale it to Cosmos 3 Super Text2Image (64B): across all 12 backbone--reward pairs with preference and rule-based rewards, it matches or exceeds existing methods, reaches their peak rewards much faster, and keeps improving over long training where they plateau or collapse.|[2611.09175](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09175)|
|**2026-11-09**|**RPEG: Training Reasoning Language Models Scalably and Cost-Effectively**|Maciej Besta et.al.|Reinforcement learning (RL) is now central to training Reasoning Language Models (RLMs), but RLM pipelines are difficult to scale and costly to run because each RL step couples multiple large models, heterogeneous stages, cross-step state, and complex asynchronous dependencies. Existing systems typically optimize only parts of this design space. We address this complexity by jointly optimizing the pipeline structure as well as its execution using a novel data-centric graph intermediate representation called RLM Pipeline Execution Graph (RPEG). RPEG exposes structural choices such as bounded asynchrony and actor-critic sharing together with execution optimizations such as stage fusion, fine-grained scheduling, and multi-dimensional model parallelism. It is coupled with a comprehensive analytical performance model for RLM training, jointly capturing compute, communication, memory, and hierarchical rack-, node-, and GPU-level effects.
