Across systems with up to 256 GH200 GPUs, our distributed runtime engine implementing RPEG improves performance by up to 2x over ReaL and reduces step latency by up to 2.48x over verl, while increasing hardware utilization by up to 30%. Overall, RPEG provides a graph-driven foundation for next-generation training pipelines for frontier reasoning LLMs.|[2611.09207](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09207)|
|**2026-11-09**|**On the Limits of Execution Traces for Repairing Assertion Failures in LLM-Generated Code**|Muntasir Adnan et.al.|Iterative repair returns a failed program's error to a large language model (LLM) and asks it to fix it. After iterative repair, the remaining failures are mostly assertion failures: with four Qwen2.5 models, they make up 79% of residual failures on BigCodeBench, all on HumanEval and 93% on LiveCodeBench. We argue, through an information view of the feedback, that an assertion failure says little about where the fault lies. We propose Breakpoint Debugging (BPD), which returns the failed program annotated inline with its variables' runtime values. We take the tasks that iterative repair failed to fix with five repair attempts and compare two ways of spending five further generations on them: BPD, and resampling. On BigCodeBench, BPD repairs 26.7% of these tasks against 22.4%, and its advantage grows with model size. On HumanEval, BPD outperforms resampling but only slightly, and on LiveCodeBench, fresh sampling repairs more (6.5% against 4.9%). Furthermore, BPD reduces unchanged-code resubmissions from 28% to 8% on BigCodeBench. Using only the first failing test's trace, it outperforms tracing all failing tests while using fewer tokens than both all-trace BPD and plain iterative repair. On LiveCodeBench, BPD gives no gain even on the public tests, so a visible failing input is not enough for a trace to help. These results match the information view: a trace helps only when the test exposes a failing input, and the fault is an implementation slip; otherwise, a repair loop should resample rather than trace.|[2611.09212](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09212)|
|**2026-11-09**|**BOXBOX: Recursive Harness Self-Improvement for Long-Running Performance Optimization**|Yuhao Tan et.al.|Automated harness optimization improves agents by learning from trajectories and task feedback. Existing offline methods use reference answers to optimize harnesses for reuse on held-out tasks. However, many real-world tasks are open-ended, while long runs expose new difficulties that offline-optimized harnesses may not address. To study harness optimization in these settings, we introduce PerfRace, a benchmark for long-running performance optimization of software projects, and investigate whether agents can use task-specific experience to recursively improve their harnesses during an ongoing task. To this end, we introduce BOXBOX, which enables agent-triggered harness revision and kernel-managed hot reloading while preserving execution context. We evaluate BOXBOX on PerfRace under matched test-time budgets, comparing it with Frozen Harness, offline Harness Evolution, and Harness Scaling. BOXBOX achieves higher speedup with more efficient use of task time and model cost, reaching a Speedup@4h of 1.70x, compared with 1.54x for the strongest baseline.|[2611.09218](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09218)|
|**2026-11-09**|**HMMF-Net: A Lightweight Hierarchical Multi-Frame Multimodal Fusion Network for Accurate Fish Feeding Intensity Assessment**|Phi-Hung Hoang et.al.|Automated fish feeding intensity assessment (FFIA) is important for precision aquaculture and feed cost optimization. However, unimodal methods are vulnerable to acoustic noise and visual disturbances, while existing multimodal frameworks often rely on computationally intensive 3D convolutions or Transformers, limiting their use on edge devices. To address these limitations, we propose HMMF-Net, a lightweight multimodal architecture that combines early multi-frame visual encoding and multi-axis acoustic modeling through a hierarchical bidirectional messenger cross-modal fusion (BMCF) mechanism. The visual branch captures temporal variations in feeding behavior by stacking temporally distant frames as a multi-channel input to an adapted 2D EfficientNetB0 backbone. The acoustic branch employs adaptive multi-axis residual units (AMARU) to extract temporal, frequency, and local time-frequency representations from log-mel spectrograms. BMCF progressively exchanges information between the two modalities at intermediate representation levels through compact messenger tokens, reducing the computational overhead of dense cross-modal attention. Evaluated on the AV-FFIA dataset using five-fold cross-validation, HMMF-Net achieves 96.77% accuracy and 96.88% F1-score on the held-out test folds, with 3.987M parameters and 1.002 GFLOPs. Compared with the best-performing visual configuration (95.49% accuracy), HMMF-Net reduces the classification error by 28.38%. Deployment on an NVIDIA Jetson Orin Nano using TensorRT with FP32 precision maintains comparable accuracy, with an inference time of 11.54 ms per 2-second sample and a model size of 18.81 MB. These results demonstrate that HMMF-Net combines effective multimodal representation learning with computational efficiency, enabling accurate and practical FFIA on resource-constrained edge platforms.|[2611.09224](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09224)|
|**2026-11-09**|**FondueQA: A Benchmark for Evaluating Parametric Compensation in Multi-Hop QA**|Laurin Schmid et.al.|Large Language Models (LLMs) show remarkable performance based on parametric knowledge, i.e., their ability to recall facts and associations internalized in a model's weights. However, studying this capability on established multi-hop benchmarks may be complicated by benchmark contamination, as many of these benchmarks have been publicly available long enough to plausibly appear in modern pretraining corpora. As a result, correct answers under missing evidence may reflect either general parametric knowledge or memorization of benchmark-specific instances. To study how models use internal knowledge when retrieval evidence is incomplete, we introduce FondueQA, in which every required multi-hop fact is uniquely localized to a designated evidence document. This enables controlled document-level evidence ablations to study parametric compensation under incomplete retrieval. Our empirical evaluation reveals that parametric compensation strongly depends on both entity popularity and the structural role of missing evidence. Generally, models compensate more successfully for facts involving popular entities. On FondueQA, we show that bridging evidence can be more useful than the answer-bearing document itself. Moreover, models may recall a missing fact in isolation but fail to use it under partial evidence, particularly when the context contains plausible competing entities, which can reduce Exact Match by up to 15 percentage points.|[2611.09274](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09274)|
|**2026-11-09**|**AudioSphere: Towards Self-Supervised Spatial Audio Representation Models**|Goksenin Yuksel et.al.|Recent audio embedding models learn representations that transfer across clip- and frame-level tasks, yet they operate almost exclusively on single-channel signals and are blind to spatial structure. Spatial audio models capture that structure but rely on clip-level supervision or contrastive objectives, yielding embeddings unsuitable for frame-level tasks like sound event detection and localization (SELD). We argue this dichotomy is not fundamental: patch-level spatial self-supervision can jointly learn acoustic and spatial features at both temporal granularities. We introduce AudioSphere, a multi-channel masked autoencoder trained on Ambisonics recordings that reconstructs both spectral content and directional cues via a joint spatiotemporal masking strategy that masks both time-frequency patches and intensity vectors. AudioSphere supports frame-level predictions while producing strong clip-level representations via aggregation. Alongside the model, we release RealSELD, a standardized benchmark for evaluating frozen backbones on real-world multi-channel SELD. AudioSphere enables, for the first time, SELD with a frozen self-supervised backbone, while matching or exceeding prior self-supervised audio embedding models on clip-level classification tasks spanning environmental sound, speech, and music, and on frame-level sound event detection. All code and data are available at https://github.com/labhamlet/AudioSphere and https://huggingface.co/labhamlet.|[2611.09275](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09275)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Diversity-Aware Client Selection for Communication-Efficient Federated Learning**|Max Halleryd et.al.|Federated learning (FL) enables collaborative model training across distributed clients without sharing raw data, making it attractive for privacy-sensitive and bandwidth-constrained applications. A practical challenge in deploying FL is reducing communication overhead while maintaining model performance under heterogeneous, non-IID data distributions. Selecting which subset of clients participates in each training round offers a principled way to address both challenges simultaneously, yet its impact on data diversity and representativeness remains underexplored. In this paper, we investigate three client selection strategies, Power-of-Choice, Fisher information, and Centered Kernel Alignment, evaluating them not only by accuracy and convergence, but through the lens of participation diversity using the Gini coefficient and KL divergence. Under non-IID conditions, restricting participation to 15% of clients per round achieves significant communication savings, and when selection promotes diverse and balanced contributions, it also improves generalization over biased alternatives. This work provides a systematic and reproducible analysis demonstrating that diversity-focused client selection renders communication efficiency and model quality complementary rather than competing objectives.|[2611.09278](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09278)|
|**2026-11-09**|**A Stochastic Latent Variable Generalization of Joint-Embedding Predictive Architectures**|Nikos Giakoumoglou et.al.|Joint-Embedding Predictive Architectures (JEPAs) predict the latent embedding of a target signal from a context signal, with an auxiliary variable z conditioning the predictor. Despite its prominent role in the original JEPA blueprint, the content of z has been carried by a fixed deterministic token in every prior instantiation, collapsing the genuinely multi-modal distribution over plausible completions to a point estimate. We present S-JEPA, which realises z as a proper stochastic latent variable for masked image prediction: a learned posterior infers z from context and target during training, while a learned context-conditioned prior approximates the distribution of plausible completions at inference, with no decoders and no reconstruction. In its deterministic limit the objective reduces to the I-JEPA loss in functional form, generalising that objective rather than strictly containing the I-JEPA model. Our central finding is that stochasticity adds capabilities, error-correlated predictive uncertainty and diverse completions, at no cost to representation quality: under matched architectures and schedules S-JEPA improves over the deterministic I-JEPA baseline and exceeds LeJEPA at matched architecture, while additionally producing spatially structured uncertainty maps and semantically diverse completions, neither of which the deterministic predictor produces.|[2611.09302](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09302)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Robust Deep Prototype Classifiers via Lipschitz Training**|Adia Khalid Khan et.al.|Robustness and interpretability are interrelated and desirable properties of machine learning models. A model that changes its prediction under imperceptible perturbations behaves inconsistently with human-recognizable features and, hence, is not interpretable. Prototype-based classifiers such as Generalized Learning Vector Quantization address both at once. They are interpretable by construction because they make the decision by comparing the input to the prototypical example; and their hypothesis margin yields a closed form robustness certificate. However, on high-dimensional data, these shallow models need deep embedding networks to achieve high accuracy, and this voids the robustness certificate. The margin is a statement about distances in the embedding space, and an unconstrained network can map an arbitrarily small input perturbation to arbitrarily large attacks in the embedding space. We close this gap with a theorem: if the embedding network is locally L-Lipschitz around a point, and the classifier is {\epsilon}-robust around its embedding, then the full model is {\epsilon}/L-robust in the input space. Based on this, we propose a Lipschitz training scheme to achieve L-Lipschitz properties for embedding networks. We evaluate three classification heads (linear, linear Support Vector Machine, and GLVQ) crossed with two embedding networks (standard and Lipschitz-constrained) on MNIST, CIFAR-10, and Imagenette-160, under FGS, PGD and C\&W attacks. The evaluation results show that combining a prototype-based head with a Lipschitz-constrained embedding provides the strongest robustness. This suggests that the Lipschitz constraint provides an additional robustness benefit in addition to the advantage provided by the prototype-based head. Since the Lipschitz constraint is enforced by sampling rather than by construction, the reported robustness is empirical rather than certified.|[2611.09327](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09327)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Syn2DRE: Synergistic Dual Teacher-Student Framework for Document-level Relation Extraction**|Khai Phan Tran et.al.|Document-level Relation Extraction (DocRE) aims to identify relations between entities across a document but typically relies on costly large-scale annotations. While pseudo-labelling enables the use of low-quality data, existing methods often suffer from inefficiency and confirmation bias. To address these challenges, we propose Syn2DRE, a semi-supervised framework that combines self-supervised learning with cross-supervision in a lightweight teacher-student architecture to produce more reliable pseudo-labels. Additionally, we introduce a unified end-to-end training strategy to further improve efficiency of the framework. Experiments on multiple benchmarks show that Syn2DRE consistently outperforms prior state-of-the-art semi-supervised DocRE methods.|[2611.09342](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09342)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Reliable Prerequisite-Aware Learning Path Recommendation under Uncertain Knowledge Structures: Long-Horizon Evaluation and Cross-Dataset Validation**|M. M. Golam Hafiz et.al.|Learning-path recommendation often depends on knowledge and prerequisite structures whose reliability is uncertain, while high accuracy in predicting the next resource does not necessarily establish reliable long-horizon path behaviour. This study develops a reliability-oriented prerequisite-aware framework that jointly examines immediate next-resource ranking, uncertain prerequisite structures, hard, soft and hybrid constraint semantics, graph robustness, and autoregressive path generation under course-level confirmatory evaluation and independent cross-dataset validation. Development used 3,187 MOOCCubeX courses, followed by a frozen confirmatory evaluation on 541 held-out courses and 37,747 transitions. The primary MOOCCubeX task is structural and course-prefix-conditioned rather than learner-personalised. Junyi provides independent structure-/hierarchy-aware external validation, uses hierarchy metadata rather than an explicit prerequisite graph and is not a zero-shot transfer setting. On the frozen MOOCCubeX evaluation, XGBoost pairwise was the strongest observed immediate ranker, with mean reciprocal rank (MRR) of 0.9048, whereas the proposed prerequisite-aware graph model achieved 0.7707. In the controlled graph-variant development ablation, the no-graph condition produced the strongest immediate ranking result, and immediate prediction showed strong dependence on permitted title/path-similarity information. Autoregressive evaluation exposed path properties not captured by one-step ranking. On Junyi, Markov achieved the strongest observed MRR of 0.4453, compared with 0.3954 for the proposed graph model, with metric-specific differences across ranking measures. Overall, immediate predictive accuracy, prerequisite-structure reliability, and long-horizon path quality should be treated as complementary rather than interchangeable evaluation dimensions in educational recommendation.|[2611.09345](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09345)|
|**2026-11-09**|**When Feedback Becomes a Backdoor: Tracing Backdoor Skill Formation in Text-Space Skill Optimization**|Louise Nayeong Koh et.al.|Reference answers are a supervision channel in text-space Skill optimization, which produces reusable text artifacts called Skills that guide future agent behavior. Poisoning those answers can induce a persistent trigger-conditioned rule without giving the attacker access to the model, optimizer, or Skill file. We study this reference-feedback poisoning in SkillOpt by tracing an attacker-supplied trigger--payload mapping from reflection to held-out activation. In SearchQA, where the payload replaces a short answer, the natural attack retains an exact rule in all three seeds; each final Skill emits the payload on 200/200 triggered questions and 0/200 clean questions. In seed 42, removing the rule eliminates activation, and an in-run comparison shows similar clean accuracy to benign SkillOpt (169/200 versus 171/200). In SpreadsheetBench, where the payload requires an additional workbook edit, no natural run retains an exact rule across three seeds. Diagnostic interventions locate two successive bottlenecks: the Analyst abstracts away required literals, and, once literal preservation is enabled, edit-budget ranking can remove exact edits before candidate construction. Poisoned references can therefore create persistent backdoors, but exposure alone does not ensure that the induced rule is formed, selected, and retained.|[2611.09348](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09348)|
|**2026-11-09**|**HiMEM: Hippocampus-Inspired Memory Consolidation, Pruning, and Composition for Vision-Language-Action Models in 3D Scenes**|Boran Zhao et.al.|Memory plays a crucial role in long-horizon task execution and cross-task generalization for Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models operating in complex 3D scenes. However, existing VLA memory mechanisms suffer from memory noise, particularly in complex 3D scenes, as stored episodic memories contain extensive redundant details about scene layouts, object configurations, and robot-specific states, which obscure generalizable manipulation principles and hinder cross-task transfer. To address this challenge, we first introduce a hippocampus-inspired memory consolidation mechanism that synthesizes extensive episodic experiences into a compact set of essential semantic memories, enabling VLA models to derive generalizable principles from practical experience. Second, we propose an episodic memory pruning mechanism that removes redundant episodic memories whose contents are captured by consolidated semantic memories, thereby increasing effective memory capacity and improving long-horizon task execution. Finally, to handle complex task scenarios, we develop a flexible memory composition mechanism that jointly leverages multiple episodic and semantic memories to enhance cross-scenario generalization. Experiments on LIBERO, LIBERO-Plus demonstrate the effectiveness of HiMEM across different VLA backbones, improving the average success rate of pi_0 from 91.88% to 98.20% on LIBERO.|[2611.09355](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09355)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Mitigating Covariate Shift in Behavioral Cloning via Robust Stationary Distribution Correction**|Seokin Seo et.al.|We consider offline imitation learning (IL), which aims to train an agent to imitate from the dataset of expert demonstrations without online interaction with the environment. Behavioral Cloning (BC) has been a simple yet effective approach to offline IL, but it is also well-known to be vulnerable to the covariate shift resulting from the mismatch between the state distributions induced by the learned policy and the expert policy. Moreover, as often occurs in practice, when expert datasets are collected from an arbitrary state distribution instead of a stationary one, these shifts become more pronounced, potentially leading to substantial failures in existing IL methods. Specifically, we focus on covariate shift resulting from arbitrary state data distributions, such as biased data collection or incomplete trajectories, rather than shifts induced by changes in dynamics or noisy expert actions. In this paper, to mitigate the effect of the covariate shifts in BC, we propose DrilDICE, which utilizes a distributionally robust BC objective by employing a stationary distribution correction ratio estimation (DICE) to derive a feasible solution. We evaluate the effectiveness of our method through an extensive set of experiments covering diverse covariate shift scenarios. The results demonstrate the efficacy of the proposed approach in improving the robustness against the shifts, outperforming existing offline IL methods in such scenarios.|[2611.09371](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09371)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Can LLMs Debug Robots by Editing Their Weights?**|Jusheng Zhang et.al.|A language model can repair a robot policy's lexical instruction grounding by editing just 65 token-embedding rows. We bring weight-space programming to robot foundation policies: GPT-6 Astra reads weights and uses a CPU workspace and budgeted simulator feedback, without gradient updates or new demonstrations. Three independent sessions on $\pi_{0.5}$ all improve success on 765 held-out paraphrases; the best raises success from 86.3\% to 92.0\% (48 repairs, four regressions; exact paired $p=1.31\times10^{-10}$). Among the 335 instructions containing edited tokens, success rises from 74.3\% to 87.5\%; the other 430 episodes have zero outcome changes. Matched random-direction edits do not help. Full replacement using the editor's own lexicon explains much of the gain but produces more regressions; its direct difference from the editor is not significant at 0.05. Across 13 further robot sessions, the editor declines 12 candidates; the sole retained edit does not show a significant held-out gain. Separately, benchmark metadata lowers FastWAM success from 70.0\% to 48.6\%, while the evaluated VLA's aggregate rate is unchanged. The evidence supports precise lexical repair, not general acquisition of missing robot capabilities.|[2611.09376](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09376)|
|**2026-11-09**|**SPSC: Zero-Shot Cross-Subject EEG-to-Image Reconstruction via Semantic Preservation and Subspace Calibration**|Yutang Lu et.al.|Pretrained vision-language models and diffusion generators have driven rapid progress in brain-to-image generation, while EEG-based reconstruction is still constrained by low signal-to-noise ratio and cross-subject semantic shifts. Zero-shot cross-subject reconstruction poses even greater challenges: EEG semantic embeddings learned from source subjects deviate from the manifold of image embeddings of the unseen target subject, particularly when neither target labels nor target image embeddings are available. To address this issue, we introduce the Semantic Preservation and Subspace Calibration (SPSC) framework, which first uses Object Mask Contrastive Regularization (OMCR) with object identity for backend training, and then constructs Subspace Candidate Gate (SCG) to achieve label-free semantic correction for target subjects during inference. Specifically, OMCR regularizes the conditioning space with object-level positive samples to preserve object-level feature neighborhoods within the reconstruction prior and low-level branch, while SCG exploits training image prototypes and batch statistics of unlabeled target EEG to select candidates for each task within subspaces of image prototypes. Under the leave-one-subject-out (LOSO) setting across ten subjects on the THINGS-EEG dataset, SPSC improves the cross-subject reconstruction from 0.552 to 0.713 on CLIP and from 0.612 to 0.745 on Alex.5. These significant gains indicate that the semantically calibrated conditions can better support generation for the target subjects under label-free inference.|[2611.09380](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09380)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Artificial Shoppers and Attention Competition**|Xiaomeng Guo et.al.|We study how AI shopping interfaces affect price competition, platform profit, and consumer welfare. A platform hosts two of three products of different quality. Without AI, consumers compare the hosted pair under salience distortion and overweight whichever attribute, price or quality, stands out. Under assistance, an AI assistant recommends the two products with the highest true utility, leaving consumers to make the same distorted comparison. Under delegation, an AI agent selects the best product, subject to purchase approval. Both modes access all products and know consumers' preferences; firms price strategically. First, assistance can soften competition despite expanded product access. The middle-quality firm may forgo low-valuation consumers because attracting them requires cutting the price paid by higher-valuation consumers as well. Relative to no AI with the same assortment, assistance lowers both hosted prices under the high- and low-quality pair but can raise both under the high- and middle-quality pair. Second, we identify an alignment paradox: some consumers can become worse off than without AI even though their best option remains recommended and becomes cheaper. Salience can induce a switch to a lower-quality product whose quality loss exceeds the saving relative to the original purchase. The assistant ranks products accurately but does not optimize the shortlist for consumers' realized welfare. Third, assistance can raise both platform profit and aggregate consumer surplus with the high- and middle-quality pair; delegation can do so with the high- and low-quality pair. Fourth, among AI designs, the platform weakly prefers delegation with the high- and middle-quality pair wherever all compared pricing equilibria exist. Yet this preferred design can yield lower platform profit and aggregate consumer surplus than the no-AI optimum.|[2611.09399](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09399)|
|**2026-11-09**|**DataHelix: A CPU-GPU Co-Scheduling Data Plane for Multimodal Model Training**|Yuanhe Li et.al.|Multimodal model training breaks the loose coupling between data loading and model execution assumed by conventional training pipelines. Large per-sample variation in decoding cost, token/latent count, and tensor size causes both CPU preprocessing time and GPU compute time to vary substantially across data-parallel ranks (training processes). In synchronous training, these two sources of heterogeneity interact tightly: GPU execution determines how long the input pipeline has to prepare the next batch, while delayed preprocessing in any rank in turn stalls all GPUs at synchronization. As a result, optimizing CPU throughput or GPU load balance in isolation cannot eliminate pipeline bubbles. We present DataHelix, a metadata-first CPU-GPU co-scheduling data plane for multimodal training. DataHelix jointly reasons about preprocessing demand and GPU execution progress, treating each GPU compute window as the readiness budget for the next batch and coordinating preprocessing across ranks to minimize deadline misses rather than CPU makespan. To avoid expensive data movement, placement is decided while samples remain lightweight descriptors, before decoding and collation amplify them into large tensors. Descriptor-level token-latent-aware batching performs coarse pre-balancing, while cost-gated collective rebalancing corrects residual skew only when its expected benefit exceeds transfer cost. Across 11 video and multimodal workloads on 8 Ascend 910B accelerators, DataHelix reduces mean deadline-miss rate by 67.8% and mean p95 input-wait latency by 87.0%, while achieving 2.72x geometric-mean throughput over rank-local DataLoaders and 1.82x over a Ray actor pool under the same CPU budget.|[2611.09411](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09411)|
|**2026-11-09**|**SmartBloch-Agent: An Agentic Framework for Automated MRI Sequence Design and Optimization**|Jiakai Yang et.al.|Magnetic resonance imaging (MRI) pulse sequences organize radiofrequency excitation, gradient encoding, and signal acquisition, determining magnetization evolution and image formation. Sequence research requires translating imaging objectives into executable protocols meeting physical and acquisition constraints, refined via physical simulation, signal reconstruction, and scientific evaluation. Sequence construction, physical verification, parameter search, and assessment involve many tools and manual decisions, limiting automation from objective to verifiable sequence. We develop SmartBloch-Agent, an integrated framework for automated MRI sequence design and optimization. Given objectives and scientific constraints in natural language, an AI agent interprets goals, plans tasks, orchestrates tools, and makes feedback-informed decisions, drawing on a construction module and optimizers for structural design and parameter search. Our GPU-accelerated SmartBloch engine supplies physical computation for both stages, so the framework configures, per sequence and objective, event validation, Full-Bloch magnetization evolution, signal acquisition, image reconstruction, and scientific evaluation, and closes the loop without external MRI simulators. Results return to the agent to guide sequence revision, parameter optimization, and candidate selection, and the design files carry simulation, reconstruction, and evaluation evidence, forming an output package for a real-scanner workflow. Experiments show the framework automatically designs and optimizes MRI sequences for varied objectives and constraints, refines candidates with physical feedback, and improves several parameter-estimation metrics and downstream quantitative reconstruction. SmartBloch-Agent unifies objectives, design, physical execution, and evaluation into a continuous workflow for end-to-end automation of MRI sequence research.|[2611.09413](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09413)|
|**2026-11-09**|**DexDICE: Reconciling Safety and Performance via Dual-Expert Offline Imitation Learning**|Seokin Seo et.al.|Ensuring high returns while providing strong safety guarantees in offline imitation learning (IL) is a fundamental challenge: existing approaches either rely on explicitly specified cost functions, which are difficult to design for complex real-world safety constraints, or require expert demonstrations that are both safe and high-performing, which are often too costly to collect. We introduce dual-expert imitation learning, which leverages two complementary data sources: (i) safety-compliant demonstrations that faithfully follow safety guidelines but may be suboptimal, and (ii) performance-oriented demonstrations that achieve high returns but may violate safety guidelines. To address this problem, we propose DexDICE (Dual-expert offline imitation learning via stationary DIstribution Correction Estimation). Instead of conventional distribution constraints, DexDICE combines DICE-style offline learning with safe support constraints, enabling agents to exploit high-return behaviors while remaining within safety-compliant regions. We prove that the ideal solution satisfying this constraint exactly has zero unsafe-region visitation with high probability. We evaluate DexDICE on a re-curated DSRL benchmark and validate it on a real-world mobile-robot navigation task trained directly from human teleoperation demonstrations, where it substantially outperforms baselines and remains robust to data scarcity and quality degradation.|[2611.09433](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09433)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Logit Laundering: Evading Data-Use Auditing in Large Language Models**|Ruihan Hu et.al.|Data-use auditing in Large Language Models (LLMs) aims to determine whether the given data has been used for training, thereby supporting data ownership safeguards. A dominant solution is data watermarking, which embeds traceable signals into training data and detects them in model outputs. Recent studies have proposed many watermarking-based auditing methods in LLMs. However, systematic investigation from an adversarial perspective remains limited: non-compliant LLM providers would use unauthorized copyrighted datasets for training their own models to enhance model capabilities, while attempting to evade detection by auditors, thereby avoiding legal liability. To fill this research gap, we propose a novel evasion framework, termed Logit Laundering, where the non-compliant LLM provider manipulates model outputs at inference time to undermine detectable watermark signals. Specifically, we design a logit reshaping module to suppress watermark-induced statistical signals by integrating a statistical bias estimator as a plug-and-play component. This design keeps the model parameters unchanged and performs manipulation only in the logit space, thereby enabling auditing evasion while minimizing the impact on model's learned knowledge. To further balance evasion effectiveness and model utility, we introduce a confidence-aware scaling module to adaptively calibrate the suppression strength based on next-token prediction confidence. Extensive evaluations show that Logit Laundering can successfully evade existing various state-of-the-art auditing methods, significantly attenuating detectable signals, while preserving model utility on downstream tasks. Our work highlights the need for exploring more robust auditing methods to guard against evasion behaviors.|[2611.09446](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09446)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Not All Entropy Is Equal: Reward-Conditioned Entropy Allocation in Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards**|Song Yu et.al.|Policy entropy is widely used to diagnose exploration and collapse in reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR), yet its magnitude does not consistently track reasoning performance across optimization objectives. We argue that this ambiguity arises because global token entropy conflates uncertainty in successful and failed trajectories, diversity across correct solution strategies, and variation among realizations of the same strategy. We introduce \textbf{Reward-Conditioned Entropy Allocation (RCEA)}, a hierarchical diagnostic framework that separates complete-trajectory entropy from average token entropy, conditions uncertainty on verifier outcome and process validity, measures correct-solution-mode retention, and connects these population-level quantities to local covariance-driven updates. In controlled sweeps over six RLVR objectives at two model scales, entropy trajectories do not induce a stable ordering of reasoning accuracy. Token entropy is higher for verifier-failed than successful trajectories under both broad separately aggregated estimates and a common-question paired analysis, whose confidence intervals exclude equality at 10 of 12 displayed endpoints. A local covariance approximation predicts the direction of adjacent-checkpoint entropy change in 95.5\% of nonfailed transitions; a reward-stratified endpoint proxy further shows that positive chosen-token covariance is more tail-concentrated within failed prefixes. Every reportable trained endpoint exhibits some contraction in independently audited correct-solution-mode breadth, whose positive association with large-\(k\) coverage remains exploratory. These results show that entropy collapse should be diagnosed by which uncertainty is removed, rather than by global entropy alone.|[2611.09448](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09448)|
|**2026-11-09**|**From LoRA to Language: Recovering Skill Documents from Adapters**|Saranya Dintakurthy Swadha et.al.|Agent skills are short documents that teach a language model how to handle a class of tasks. To save context, these documents are increasingly compiled into low-rank adapters (LoRA), which keep the behaviour but lose the text: an adapter is hard to read, edit, audit or move to another model. We ask whether the skill document can be written back from the adapter and its frozen base model, and we score each recovered document by the share of the original's key instructions it states and by the tasks it solves. Reading the weights alone recovered little with the methods we tried; a learned map from weights to text passed on about one bit of the document. Running the adapter worked much better. The Executing Writer, a small writer trained on top of the adapted model, recovers 39-44% of the key instructions of held-out skills, against 1.3-1.5% with another skill's adapter, and on adapters fitted to 41 external skills its documents restore 85-91% of the original's task gain. These strongest results are on adapters whose training stored the document; adapters trained on behaviour alone are much harder for trained writers (1-2% of instructions). For these adapters we test an agent that runs the base and adapted models and writes a skill from where they differ. On ALFWorld its skills recover 58-71% of a LoRA's gain and carry over to models that cannot load the LoRA, lifting Llama-3.1-8B from 5% to 55%. Whether such a skill comes from the adapter depends on what the adapter learned: when its behaviour cannot be guessed, the adapter is the source; when it can, a reward loop without the adapter does as well.|[2611.09454](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09454)|
|**2026-11-09**|**FusionCLIP: Multimodal Contrastive Pretraining for Fusion Plasma Diagnostics**|Qiang Chen et.al.|Visible-light videos and multivariate diagnostic signals provide complementary observations of tokamak discharges. However, existing fusion pretraining methods, primarily learn representations by reconstructing or predicting observations, leaving the correspondence between these modalities underexplored. Representation learning is further challenged by the fact that plasma-related regions occupy only part of each video frame and diagnostic variables have substantially different sampling rates. We propose FusionCLIP, a multimodal contrastive pretraining framework. Spatiotemporal Stability Selection retains informative visual tokens, while a Temporal Query Encoder integrates asynchronous diagnostic signals using their observation timestamps. Contrastive learning then aligns the two modality representations. We construct EAST Multimodal Diagnostics (EAST-MMD), comprising 1307 EAST discharges, and evaluate the model on three independently fine-tuned tasks: disruption prediction, edge-localized mode (ELM) classification, and $H_{98}$ regression. FusionCLIP achieves an aggregate score of 89.07\%, exceeding the best general-purpose pretraining configuration and an in-domain contrastive pretraining configuration without Spatiotemporal Stability Selection by 6.27 and 2.12 percentage points, respectively. Ablations and attention visualizations further support the contributions of its key components.|[2611.09493](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09493)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Mosaic:montage-agnostic self-distilled graph learning for subject-independent ieeg seizure detection**|Arya Jalali et.al.|Intracranial EEG provides high signal-to-noise ratio and spatial resolution for detecting epileptic activity, but patient-specific electrode placement leads to substantial variation in channel count and montage across subjects. This heterogeneity limits the generalization of existing detectors under subject-independent evaluation. We propose MOSAIC (MOntage-agnostic Self-distilled Adaptive Inter-channel Connectivity learning), a lightweight graph-learning framework for subject-independent iEEG seizure detection. MOSAIC pairs a shared convolutional channel encoder with a patient-agnostic channel-attention module that infers a weighted inter-channel adjacency matrix from the encoded features, requiring no fixed montage graph. An exponential moving average self-distillation objective regularizes each prediction toward a slowly updated per-patient teacher. Across four public iEEG corpora (SWEC-ETHZ long- and short-term, Mayo, FNUSA), MOSAIC outperforms state-of-the-art baselines in F2 and Cohen's $\kappa$ while learning more stable inter-channel adjacencies, demonstrating that self-distilled connectivity learning transfers across unseen implantations.|[2611.09496](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09496)|
|**2026-11-09**|**e-means: outperforming k-means (in one-tenth the epochs)**|Swann Bessa et.al.|Lloyd's k-means algorithm is a long-running standard for clustering, with applications in data analysis, retrieval, quantization and data curation. In this paper, we introduce an exponential moving average optimization procedure for k-means with mini-batch updates, powered by modern deep learning machinery and new insights. We also establish scaling laws on how decay rate and batch size impact final performance. This gives us a formulaic rule to fix them for multiple problem sizes and embedding datasets, without the need for any hyperparameter sweep. The resulting clustering algorithm, coined e-means, is much faster than Lloyd k-means and converges to lower losses, frequently even beating k-means++. On a wide array of embeddings datasets, it beats the loss of Lloyd k-means at 25 epochs in 1.4 to 2.6 epochs, which translates to 10-45x GPU wall-clock speedups compared to state-of-the-art k-means implementations. As an example, e-means partitions DINO10B, a dataset of 10 billion DINO embeddings, into 16 million clusters in only 2.5 epochs, reaching a loss below Lloyd's at 25 epochs.|[2611.09501](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09501)|
|**2026-11-09**|**BEiTScore++: Reference-free Image Captioning Evaluation with BEiT-3 and Conformalized Quantile Regression**|Gon\c{c}alo Gomes et.al.|Reliable uncertainty estimation is important for deploying evaluation metrics in real-world settings, where both input ambiguity and domain shifts are common. In this work, we propose an uncertainty-aware extension of the BEiTScore image captioning metric, producing conformally calibrated confidence intervals alongside alignment scores. Our approach models predictive distributions over human judgments, enabling both accurate scoring and principled uncertainty quantification in a single forward pass. We evaluate our BEiTScore++ metric across multiple benchmarks with human-annotated ratings, showing that it preserves the ranking correlation of BEiTScore, while producing intervals whose width tracks the prediction error more closely than Monte Carlo dropout on most benchmarks. The estimated uncertainty also grows under image degradation and on out-of-domain artwork images, making BEiTScore++ a practical tool for uncertainty-aware evaluation of image-caption alignment.|[2611.09505](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09505)|
|**2026-11-09**|**V-STORM: A Structure-Aware Grounded Chain of Thought Dataset for Faithful and Fine-Grained Spatio-Temporal Video Reasoning**|Dian Jiao et.al.|While MLLMs can often identify what happens in a video, they still struggle with fine-grained spatio-temporal reasoning, which requires precise grounding when and where the supporting evidence appears and composing such evidence into a coherent reasoning chain. This challenge is exacerbated by the lack of scalable data construction and evaluation pipelines that provide granular, verifiable supervision linking reasoning steps to explicit spatio-temporal evidence.To address this gap, we present V-STORM, a structure-aware framework that transforms videos into Spatio-Temporal Scene Graphs (STSGs) and derives Grounded Chain of Thought (GCoT) from validated evidence paths, making each reasoning step traceable to structured and verifiable spatio-temporal evidence.Under this framework, we construct a 260K-sample training set and a 3K-sample human-verified benchmark, both featuring GCoT annotations that link reasoning to explicit spatio-temporal evidence.Experiments reveal a clear separation between evidence localization and evidence composition: current MLLMs can often identify relevant spatio-temporal cues, yet still fail to integrate them into correct multi-step reasoning, exposing a bottleneck that answer-only evaluation largely obscures. Moreover, fine-tuning with V-STORM substantially improves both answer accuracy and grounding faithfulness, validating its effectiveness as supervision for faithful and evidence-grounded video reasoning. Our dataset, benchmark, and source code will be publicly released.|[2611.09511](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09511)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Active Learning of Robust Models**|Mizu Nishikawa-Toomey et.al.|In many domains from species identification to cancer diagnosis, labelling data to train predictive models remains costly. In these settings, it is advantageous to intelligently select data points to label to achieve the maximum information gain, this is the motivation behind the study of active learning. In addition to the high costs of acquiring data in such domains, there is the added complexity of data distribution shifts that can occur between training and deployment. For example, the features of cancerous skin cells taken in one country may be different in another region of the world. These two challenges - efficient labelling of data, and learning models robust to distribution shifts have been studied separately but not together, even though these challenges often co-occur in real-life settings. We identify failure modes of standard active learning approaches when the distribution of data at deployment is different from the distribution in which the data is sampled for labelling. We propose a new strategy for efficiently learning classifiers that generalise under specific forms of distribution shift, namely subpopulation shift. Drawing on work in learning from heterogeneous data, we aim to train models that perform well across subpopulations of the data, through active learning. To handle this challenging setting, we exploit the fact that many datasets include metadata about data collection that can serve as a noisy proxy for each sample's underlying subpopulation. We demonstrate how to leverage these noisy labels during active learning selection, and demonstrate empirically that our approach improves classifier accuracy across subpopulations.|[2611.09520](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09520)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Multimodal Training with Knowledge Traction for Video Anomaly Detection**|Xiao Tan et.al.|The task of weakly supervised video anomaly detection is challenging. Existing approaches mainly model video frame features and determine whether anomalies are present based on their magnitudes. In this work, we propose a novel method that incorporates external knowledge into training to improve model performance, robustness, and generalization. We leverage textual prompts and a large language model (LLM) to establish prior information for the specific task, and employ dictionary learning to normalize and compress this information. Moreover, during experiments, we observed inconsistencies in the textual descriptions extracted from similar video frames by image caption models. To address this, we devise a method that leverages an LLM and auxiliary information to ensure consistency in the textual descriptions of similar frames within a video. Finally, we introduce a feature processing approach that incorporates prior knowledge and cross-modal attention mechanisms. This approach learns the relationships and characteristics among different modalities to construct more accurate and reliable feature representations, thereby improving the accuracy and stability of downstream tasks. Experimental results demonstrate that our method surpasses other state-of-the-art approaches.|[2611.09536](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09536)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Query-Guided RoPE Modulation for Lost-in-the-Middle Mitigation in Long-Context LLMs**|Peiyu Zhang et.al.|Long-context language models often neglect information in the middle of the input, a phenomenon known as "lost-in-the-middle". Part of this gap may stem from the long-range decay of rotary position embedding (RoPE), which weakens the positional signal between distant tokens. Existing input compression deletes low-importance tokens, and attention reweighting acts only after RoPE has weakened this signal. RoPE-level methods modify positions directly but apply the same remapping to every query. To address these limitations, we propose QGPM (Query-Guided Positional Modulation), a training-free method that reshapes RoPE positions using query relevance from the model's own attention heads. QGPM builds a broad relevance map for lower layers and a sharp one for upper layers. Low-relevance tokens advance by less than one position on fast-rotating planes, pulling relevant evidence toward the query. Slow-rotating planes keep the original layout, with every token in place. Experiments on NoLiMa and RULER with multiple context lengths show that QGPM improves over base models and strong baselines, with the largest gains on retrieval tasks, and generalizes across multiple backbones. Our code is available at https://github.com/iZHpy/QGPM.|[2611.09554](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09554)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Encoding Memory in World Models with Test-Time Model-Based Reinforcement Learning**|Maxime Burchi et.al.|Model-based reinforcement learning (MBRL) approaches have demonstrated strong performance across diverse domains in recent years, learning accurate world models from agent trajectories. However, despite this progress, current approaches still struggle to surpass human performance on tasks that require long-term memory and planning. In this work, we address the problem of long-term memory in MBRL by moving beyond the commonly studied paradigm of architecture design. We introduce Test-Time Model-Based Reinforcement Learning (TT-MBRL), using gradient descent at test time to encode the memory of episode observations into world model parameters. This enables the agent to learn a distinct world model for each episode, improving memory capabilities compared to traditional approaches that solely rely on contextual memory. We first establish a proof of concept on a simplified two-dimensional variant of the Memory Maze environment, which we refer to as Memory Maze 2D, showing consistent improvements across different world model architectures. We then demonstrate the effectiveness of TT-MBRL in the 3D domain, achieving new state-of-the-art results on the Memory Maze benchmark. Finally, we show that TT-MBRL maintains strong performance on non-memory benchmarks such as DeepMind Control and Atari 100k.|[2611.09625](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09625)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Steerable Multi-Objective Group-Relative Preference Optimization with Jointly Trained Low-Rank Adapters**|Constantinos Karouzos et.al.|Language model deployment often requires different trade-offs between competing objectives as user preferences change, yet standard post-training methods typically produce a single policy for a fixed objective weighting. We introduce LambdaMix, a new parameter efficient method for learning a preference conditioned family of policies with group-relative online reinforcement learning. LambdaMix jointly trains one low-rank adapter per objective over a frozen base model. One preference vector selects the adapter mixture that generates responses and weights the separately normalized rewards used to update the adapters. A single training run supports changing preferences at inference without further optimization. We experiment with four models on helpfulness and harmlessness and on solving math problems while following instructions. We also evaluate the two largest models on rewriting comments to preserve wording while reducing clues about personal attributes. On the first two tasks, LambdaMix achieves higher hypervolume than all evaluated conditioned and policy merging baselines across all four models. LambdaMix uses fewer training completions and stores fewer adapter parameters than a set of GRPO specialist policies. With architecture and initialization fixed, varying adapter mixtures and objective weights together gives wider score ranges and higher coverage than varying either alone.|[2611.09634](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09634)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Breaking the Privacy Promise: Gradient Inversion Attacks on Federated Low-Rank Adapted Vision Transformers**|Ritik K. Badiya et.al.|Parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) has been increasingly adopted to fine-tune pretrained models with low computational cost and is therefore also preferred in the federated learning paradigm to reduce computational and communication overhead. In this work, we investigate the sparsely explored problem of the risk of private data leakage in LoRA-adapted Vision Transformers (ViTs), where low-rank updates weaken the gradient signal available to a server with access to individual client updates. We show that quality of reconstruction is limited by two coupled ambiguities: reduced information in LoRA gradients and missing spatial cues from frozen positional embeddings. To address this, we propose a spatially anchored generative inversion framework that incorporates server-prescribed trainable positional embeddings into ViT blocks during fine-tuning at clients. The resulting positional gradients act as layer-wise spatial anchors, providing additional positional cues, while the generative prior keeps reconstructions on a realistic image manifold, reducing duplicate-data and patch-permutation artifacts, {which produce similar gradients as those produced when the target client images are used}. Adding our proposed spatial anchoring to GIAS improves PSNR by 10.14 dB, SSIM by 0.45, and LPIPS by 0.098 on CIFAR-10, as well as PSNR gains of 7.83 and 11.74 dB on high-resolution CelebA and CARS datasets, respectively, in the FedSGD setting. Such gains in reconstructed image quality metrics are also maintained at higher batch sizes and in FedAvg settings. These results indicate that LoRA-based PEFT does not, by itself, prevent data leakage in FL, and carefully incorporated positional embeddings can help in exposing private information.|[2611.09652](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09652)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Differentially Private GraphRAG: Protecting Relation Connectivity and Semantics**|Jie Fu et.al.|Graph Retrieval-Augmented Generation (GraphRAG) connects information across documents through entity relationships to support relational reasoning. However, black-box access to GraphRAG systems can enable adversaries to reconstruct knowledge graphs and recover both structural connectivity between entities and the semantics of their relationships. Existing defenses lack formal privacy guarantees and provide limited protection against such reconstruction attacks. We propose RelGuard, the first differentially private framework for GraphRAG, which provides formal privacy guarantees by mitigating connectivity leakage and protecting relations and their semantic meaning through relation removal. We formalize relation differential privacy (relation-DP), which bounds the distinguishability of mechanism outputs according to the semantic distance between alternative relations while accounting for relation removal. Building on this definition, we develop a four-step defense mechanism that sanitizes source documents before GraphRAG indexing by perturbing relation predicates or removing their associated connectivity using the exponential mechanism. Experiments across three GraphRAG systems and two targeted reconstruction attacks demonstrate that RelGuard provides stronger protection against reconstruction attacks than four existing defense mechanisms while better preserving downstream utility.|[2611.09658](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09658)|
|**2026-11-09**|**How Much Conversation History Does an AI Agent Need?**|Danny Kim et.al.|How does using less conversational history change reader-token use and benchmark scores? We examine this question on 1,100 BEAM questions. We held the retrieval ranking and reader constant across five fixed passage budgets, and mean assigned scores rose as the passage budget grew. The score gain per additional token became smaller at the upper tested budgets. Answerable questions generally received higher scores with more context. Pooled abstention scores sometimes rose as the passage budget shrank. We also tested message retention in a controlled task with explicit conversational-state targets and compared retained user messages with token-matched recency. Qwen matched the full state more often for direct requirements when user messages were retained. Its full-state matching declined when answers depended on references to earlier offers. Mistral produced near-floor results across the retention conditions. A scoring audit leaves the correctness of BEAM answers unresolved. These experiments characterize the token and score consequences of the tested memory budgets. Reliable budget selection depends on retaining the evidence each question requires and validating the measure used to assess answer quality.|[2611.09662](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09662)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Goodhart in the Planner: Sampling-Based Control Exploits Rewards Learned from Human Preferences**|Soovadeep Bakshi et.al.|Robots increasingly choose actions by searching against a reward model learned from human preferences, in model-predictive control or best-of-$N$ selection; we measure what this does to the true objective. On HalfCheetah and Hopper with crowd-sourced labels, and on Meta-World drawer-open and plate-slide with synthetic labels and blind labels from the two authors, harder MPPI search cuts true return by up to 76%: with human labels the drawer opens in 0 of 20 runs against 10 of 10 for the cloned policy the planner starts from, while held-out label accuracy stays at 0.75-0.97. On plate-slide human labels are exploited less than synthetic ones, and on window-open search helps. True return roughly follows how far the planner's weights move from its proposals ($\sqrt{\mathrm{KL}}$) for both weighting rules we test, and best-of-$N$ with a verifier from synthetic labels falls below a single noisy action. We prove that $\chi^2$-regularized MPPI at a temperature set by the scorer's error spread on the proposals is worst-case optimal, that no positive gain can be certified once that spread exceeds the scorer's own, and that an episode's return splits exactly into per-step terms, of which the guarantee covers the selection terms. Calibrated from 100 proposal pairs ranked by true return (an oracle) on start states the evaluation never uses, this rule is the safest fix we test: with each of five independent sets it succeeds in every arm run and keeps on average 86-94% of the cloned policy's return on plate-slide and drawer-open, though it is cautious where search helps. Calibration from a person's comparisons cannot detect a bias the scorer learned from that person; correcting for the pairs people cannot tell apart, or a second person, limits the damage.|[2611.09670](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09670)|
|**2026-11-09**|**SURE:Spatiotemporal Uncertainty Representation for Efficient Vision-Language-Action Learning**|Jingye Zhang et.al.|Vision-language-action (VLA) policies have become a promising paradigm for end-to-end robot control, but their effectiveness still depends heavily on large volumes of demonstration data. In manipulation tasks, this dependence is compounded by structured spatiotemporal uncertainty in the action space: multiple nearby actions or trajectories may successfully solve the same task, while semantically equivalent demonstrations often unfold at different speeds. Standard pointwise supervision treats each demonstrated action as an exact target, imposing an unnecessary learning burden under such uncertainty. We present SURE, a data-efficient VLA training framework that explicitly models both spatial and temporal uncertainty. SURE augments a VLA backbone with a lightweight transposed-convolutional probabilistic action decoder that captures spatial uncertainty through local correlations among neighboring action bins. It further introduces Soft-DTW alignment supervision to accommodate local timing variations between predicted and demonstrated action chunks. Together, these two components reduce sensitivity to demonstrator-specific poses and execution timing, enabling more data-efficient action learning. We evaluate SURE on LIBERO, RoboTwin 2.0, and real-world manipulation tasks. Even with only 10% of the demonstration data or 20% of the training steps, SURE achieves manipulation success rates that match or surpass baseline VLAs.|[2611.09684](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09684)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Verifier Noise Is a Temperature: Testing Tilt Predictions in Finite-Budget LLM-Guided Search**|Xiaomeng Li et.al.|LLM-guided search loops propose a modified candidate, score it, and keep it if it scores better. When both candidates are re-scored at every comparison, as in the minibatch acceptance gates of prompt optimizers, keep-if-better is a reversible Markov chain if the score difference is logistic and the mutation operator is reversible, and its stationary law is the mutation equilibrium tilted by exp(beta f). For any continuous symmetric noise difference the same tilt is the stationary law of a small-step diffusion limit, with beta = 4F'(0); for a minibatch gate beta grows with the square root of the minibatch size. Caching the incumbent's score instead inflates the recorded score. We test the tilt as a prediction for LLM-guided program search, committing each prediction from calibration data before the search runs. The level after 150 steps rises with the minibatch size in six of seven settings, but its predicted value fails: on bin packing, search attains 62-88% of the predicted gain when the proposer edits the parent and 109-119% when it writes new heuristics. The attained fraction falls with the parent-child quality correlation of unselected mutation; a registered test on two new settings confirmed the direction, but not the quantitative agreement after a correction. Further registered tests found that runs started from a good program kept losing quality over 300 steps, more so from starts that search had selected, by a margin that passed its registered rule narrowly. Runs from different starts had not reached a common level, so whether the tilt describes the stationary law of LLM editing remains open. A registered test in prompt optimisation on frozen task-model scores failed: search exceeded the tilt, and the bin-packing relation did not transfer.|[2611.09699](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09699)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Empirical Study of Post-Quantum Cryptography Adoption Status in SSH Infrastructure**|Md Ariful Haque et.al.|With the anticipated arrival of Q-day, quantum computers threaten current cryptographic schemes for encryption and digital signatures. In response, major content delivery networks and cloud service providers are already adopting Post-Quantum Cryptography (PQC). The current state-of-the-art focuses on benchmarking the operational performance of implementing PQC, but the real-world adoption status remains under-researched. This work utilizes large-scale SSH fingerprint data from a proprietary scanning tool to analyze PQC adoption across 1.52 million hosts and 27,012 entities. Our study shows that the PQC transition in the SSH protocol is underway but highly polarized: 45.2% of SSH hosts on port 22 support PQC, while 54.8% remain classical-only. At the entity level, 39.9% have enabled PQC on at least one host, whereas 60.1% have no PQC support. By correlating PQC adoption with industry, geography, and organizational size (digital and financial), this study provides a comprehensive empirical assessment of PQC adoption status in SSH infrastructure.|[2611.09703](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09703)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Empirical Assessment of Post-Quantum Cryptography Adoption Status in TLS**|Md Ariful Haque et.al.|Anticipating Q-day security concerns, major CDNs and cloud providers are migrating to post-quantum cryptography (PQC). While existing literature benchmarks PQC performance, empirical research evaluating real-world adoption data remains scarce. We present an empirical assessment of PQC deployment across seven critical protocols (HTTPS, LDAPS, SMTPS, IMAP-SSL, POP3-SSL, TELNETS, and RDP) using 10,000 target IP addresses per protocol. At the origin-server level, PQC algorithm support reaches 9.1% for HTTPS and 7.39% for LDAPS, while falling below 1% for all other evaluated protocols. At the entity level, PQC support is 11% for HTTPS and 5.4% for LDAPS, while the remaining protocols have less than 5% of entities supporting PQC on the hosted servers. We further correlate these adoption trends against organizational digital and financial footprint, and industry sectors, yielding a multi-dimensional empirical assessment of PQC deployment. Finally, at the domain level, we perform a comparative analysis of PQC adoption in Cloudflare-hosted domains against baseline Cloudflare datasets. Collectively, our findings provide critical insights into the current state of PQC readiness over Transport Layer Security (TLS).|[2611.09707](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09707)|
|**2026-11-09**|**ICare-DT: a digital twin for continuous forecasting of intensive care patients**|Yanke Li et.al.|Digital twins of intensive care patients should update forecasts from new data and predict treatment responses, but few studies test whether routine records support either. We propose ICare-DT, a Transformer-based twin that updates a latent patient state hourly from new measurements without retraining. We trained it on 140,473 eICU-CRD stays to forecast vital signs crossing their training-set means. On test data, later forecasts beat the twin's own hour-6 forecasts of the same windows in all 12 settings (+0.073 to +0.153 AUROC). On validation data, controls that withheld or swapped the new hours traced the gain to the patient's own measurements. Without refitting, the model kept its gain on a second database (8 of 9 settings, +0.025 to +0.114 AUROC) in a prespecified evaluation of the previously analyzed MIMIC-IV test split. Refitted within MIMIC-IV, a twin state fine-tuned on clinical endpoints gave gradient boosting a significant gain for acute kidney injury (+0.0035 AUROC; hypotension +0.0010, not significant). To test response prediction, we built a benchmark that replaces the treatments in real records with simulated doses and injects responses of known size, visible only at measurement times. Ridge models supervised with injected effects recovered them from real records only through frequently measured variables (heart rate) read at their measurement times. We asked standard learners to predict these effects for a dosing regime no patient had received. No standard learner outperformed predicting zero effect at an out-of-support query. When told how the simulated responses were generated, a separate learner recovered the effects from the same records without seeing their true values (R2 0.30 to 0.40 at one effect size). Routine records support forecasts that improve with each patient's new data, and our benchmark is a testbed for models that learn how responses are generated.|[2611.09712](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09712)|
|**2026-11-09**|**MemoryDojo: Benchmarking Poisoning Through LLM Agent Memory**|David H. Yang et.al.|LLM agents benefit from using memory systems to improve performance on long-horizon, multi-session tasks. However, memory introduces a critical attack surface. Malicious instructions encountered in one session may be stored and propagated through memory before being retrieved and acted upon in later sessions. Because memory systems differ in how they process and retrieve information, the downstream effects of poisoning can vary significantly across systems. To quantify the effects of poisoning across different memory architectures, we introduce MemoryDojo, a benchmark for tracing the lifecycle of poisoned information through agent memory and measuring its influence on subsequent agent behavior. MemoryDojo is easily extensible, consists of 155 multi-session long-horizon trajectories covering four domains, and supports five different memory systems. Through our evaluation, we find that all memory systems are susceptible to memory poisoning, with substantial variation in downstream effects. We further propose a memory-aware poison optimization process that increases the poisoning efficacy, highlighting differences between memory architectures. We hope that MemoryDojo will support research on understanding and mitigating agentic memory risks.|[2611.09721](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09721)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Safety Is $\Pi_1$, Provability Is $\Sigma_1$ : One Framework for the Hugging Face Breach and OpenAI's Mathematical Proofs**|Jinho Kang et.al.|OpenAI's agents breached Hugging Face in July 2026, and the same company released more than seven hundred AI-generated manuscripts of mathematical proofs in October 2026. The first event is read as a failure in security, while the second is read as a success in mathematics. We read both through the hybrid framework of our paper (Kim and Kang, 2026, arXiv:2611.01914), in which coding, proving, and learning from data are derivations by one and the same deductive mechanism, distinguished by the kinds of premises from which they are derived. Under this framework, the AI systems behind the two events ran the same loop, in which a statistical proposer produces candidate exploits or proofs and a deductive verifier settles each one by a derivation. What separates the events is the logical form of the statement wanted by each of the two domains, security and mathematics. The safety of a program is a $\Pi_1$ statement, for it says that no exploit exists, which is not assured by any finite list of tests that have been passed by the program. By contrast, the provability of a sentence is a $\Sigma_1$ statement, for it says that a proof exists, which is assured by a single instance, namely a proof that has been checked by the deductive verifier. The theorem behind both is Turing's Undecidability Theorem: the halting set is $\Sigma_1$ but not decidable, so its complement is $\Pi_1$ and not $\Sigma_1$, which is to say that membership has a finite witness but non-membership has none. We prove that attaching a statistical proposer to a deductive verifier does not change this undecidability result, however good the proposer is. We then discuss what does assure safety and where the residual risk goes.|[2611.09736](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09736)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Language as Trait, Politeness as Strategy: Controlled Tests of Linguistic Effects in LLM Social-Deduction Agents**|Yuchen Li et.al.|Large language model (LLM) agents coordinate through conversation, and the same message can be phrased in simpler or more complex language, bluntly or politely. We test whether such differences change how other agents judge and act, using the LURK Werewolf framework, which separates each utterance's speech-act predicate from its natural-language realization. We rewrite only the realization, check that the game content is preserved, and replay each game day from saved checkpoints against a re-sampled control. We vary linguistic range consistently across a speaker's messages and politeness strategy per utterance. Across 30 games and 4,335 replays, neither manipulation changes the trust other agents place in the speaker, and no effect on votes or night actions survives correction for multiple comparisons; the largest uncorrected effect is that shifting range away from a neutral paraphrase changes about 4 percentage points more of the other players' votes. In natural play, 88% of face-threatening speech is bald on-record regardless of role.|[2611.09768](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09768)|
|**2026-11-09**|**ROSE: Risk-Aware Orthogonal Subspace Navigation for Lifelong Knowledge Editing in Multimodal Large Language Models**|Lingyun Song et.al.|Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) require continuous factual updates to stay current, yet lifelong knowledge editing remains a daunting challenge. The primary obstacle is the severe Locality Erosion and Catastrophic Forgetting, where sequential edits inevitably interfere with pre-trained capabilities and prior updates. In this paper, we propose \textbf{ROSE}, a \textbf{R}isk-aware \textbf{O}rthogonal \textbf{S}ubspace navigation framework for lifelong knowledge \textbf{E}diting. ROSE addresses forgetting by aiming to restrict parameter updates to the orthogonal complement of previously edited subspaces. Crucially, it introduces a risk-guided mechanism that utilizes a dynamically computed risk mask to safeguard specific factual parameter subspaces essential for model stability. During inference, ROSE employs a multi-granularity knowledge integration strategy, featuring prototype-anchored semantic gating and perturbation-driven subspace fusion to precisely activate edited knowledge for relevant queries while limiting interference on unrelated inputs. Comprehensive experiments on two prominent multimodal benchmarks show that ROSE is competitive with state-of-the-art approaches across five key metrics, while maintaining outstanding stability over $1,000$ sequential updates. Our code is available at https://github.com/sunlitsong/ROSE.|[2611.09770](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09770)|
|**2026-11-09**|**When Instruction Files Go Stale: Documentation Debt and Hidden Cost in Coding Agents**|Tural Mehtiyev et.al.|LLM-based coding agents such as Claude Code are guided by repository instruction files (also called context files or agent configuration files) like AGENTS.md and CLAUDE.md, which are read in every agent session. As any documentation artifact, these files can fall behind the code and become stale (e.g., a test folder moves, but the file still points to the old one). In technical debt terms, such a stale instruction is documentation debt, and its interest is the cost it adds to every later agent session. Evaluations that check only task success can miss this cost, and the stale file can persist. In this study, we ask what leads agents to act on a stale instruction, how it affects their behavior and at what cost, and how they recover. We study three real-world stale instructions from active open-source repositories in depth, each a different kind of guidance: a test command, a code location, and a procedure for adding a component. Agents fix 22 maintenance tasks under three versions of each instruction (left stale, corrected to the current path, and removed), with the code and the task held fixed, and we analyze 732 trajectories of two models at the level of individual tool calls. We find that agents follow stale guidance when it is relevant to their tasks, sometimes even when correct information is available elsewhere. On the stale test command, Claude Sonnet 5.5 needed about two extra tool calls per run, a third more than with the corrected command; in the other cases, we could not detect a difference. Although agents generally recovered from the immediate failures, none repaired the underlying instruction file. Correcting the instructions eliminated operations on obsolete paths, while removing them prevented most. Our findings show that task success alone can hide the costs of documentation debt, highlighting the need to evaluate agent behavior beyond final outcomes.|[2611.09802](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09802)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Scaling Graph Similarity Learning with Graph Neural Networks**|Ahmet T\"uzen et.al.|Graph similarity computation is an important task in graph mining with many real-world applications, such as chemical compound analysis, program dependence comparison, and social network analysis. The problem is computationally challenging: Graph Edit Distance (GED) and Maximum Common Subgraph (MCS), which quantify the distance between graphs and identify their largest shared subgraph, respectively, are NP-hard to compute exactly. To address these computational challenges, Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) have been applied to this problem. However, existing GNN-based approaches have primarily focused on small-scale graph datasets, while scalability to larger scales remains underexplored. In this work, we propose FiLSim, a scalable GNN-based framework, and investigate whether a simplified GNN pipeline can maintain competitive performance on standard benchmarks while scaling to large graphs. We additionally propose a sequential graph-editing strategy to construct large-scale graph datasets with reliable GED labels to evaluate scalability. The proposed framework is evaluated on four benchmark datasets and four newly created large-scale datasets, demonstrating its effectiveness and scalability. FiLSim remains accurate on graphs with more than 150{,}000 nodes, where the baseline methods run out of memory or fail to learn, and scores all test pairs within seconds on a single GPU.|[2611.09805](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09805)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Discovering formally verified quantum programs with AI**|Sergey Shirobokov et.al.|The timeline by which quantum computers can solve breakthrough problems in science depends on improved quantum algorithms as much as on better hardware. Such algorithmic advances are typically achieved through many years of expert human collaboration. In mathematics and computer science, large language models have accelerated progress through automated discovery loops. However, quantum algorithms can only be evaluated by classical computers on restricted or small instances. Here we demonstrate automated discovery of quantum programs beyond these regimes including order-of-magnitude improvements on scientific applications. To achieve this, we build a quantum program library in the Lean theorem prover in which large language model agents synthesize novel programs against formal problem specifications and verify their correctness and cost. Agent teams collaborate, sharing results and knowledge through wikis and discussing ideas at organized conferences. Our results improve on prior baselines for parallel search (up to 1.4x), quantum arithmetic (290x), and simulations relevant to fusion, photochemistry and drug-metabolism by 11x, 8x and 2.5x respectively. Combining human and artificial intelligence at scale, under the rigor of mathematical proof, lets researchers design algorithms that today's classical and quantum computers cannot test, shortening the path to practical quantum computation.|[2611.09822](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09822)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Grasp-TAGS: Task-Aware Grasp Selection via Critic Distillation for Prehensile Loco-Manipulation**|Xun Tu et.al.|Loco-manipulation, which requires mobile manipulators to coordinate locomotion and manipulation over long horizons, is a key capability for large-object rearrangement in applications such as service robotics and warehouse automation. Despite recent progress, reliably executing these tasks remains challenging because performance depends on complex robot-object and object-environment interaction dynamics.
