Existing approaches address this challenge either through model-based methods, which often require accurate dynamics models, or through learning-based methods, which reduce this requirement but typically assume a fixed or predefined initial grasp. However, different geometrically stable grasps can lead to substantially different downstream manipulation performance. Therefore, we propose Grasp-TAGS, a task-aware grasp selection framework for goal-conditioned large-object rearrangement. We train a unified PPO-based reinforcement learning policy across multiple initial grasp configurations and distill the learned critic into a lightweight grasp utility network that ranks candidate grasps using only the candidate grasp configuration and goal information. At inference time, we select the grasp with the highest predicted downstream utility for the downstream policy to complete the task, before even making a contact. Experiments demonstrate that Grasp-TAGS improves rearrangement success over both fixed single-grasp policies and random grasp selection, achieving an average success rate of 89.84% compared with 79.87% for the best-performing single-grasp policies, an improvement of 9.97 percentage points. Grasp-TAGS also reduces final orientation errors across all evaluated objects. For more qualitative results, refer to the supplementary webpage: https://rpm-lab-umn.github.io/Grasp-TAGS-web-page/|[2611.09864](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09864)|
|**2026-11-09**|**GRAC: Graph-Based Retrieval-Augmented Captioning for Surgical Scene Description**|Rochak Dhakal et.al.|Purpose: Surgical operative reports support procedural documentation, surgical training, performance assessment, and workflow review. However, extracting meaningful information often requires extensive manual review and clinical expertise. Automated surgical scene descriptions could reduce this burden by leveraging recurring surgical activities and prior knowledge. Methods: We propose GRAC (Graph-Based Retrieval-Augmented Captioning), a framework that represents surgical scenes as graphs capturing relationships among instruments, actions, and anatomical structures. Given an input scene graph, GRAC learns to retrieve relevant graph-caption pairs from training data. It compares instrument-tissue interactions between retrieved examples and the input scene, using comparison features to condition retrieved-caption representations during generation. Results: On the EndoVis-18 benchmark, GRAC achieved a CIDEr-D of 5.88, compared with 4.23 for SGT and 3.36 for the strongest conventional Transformer baseline. Exact instrument-action-target triplet-set accuracy reached 52.6%, compared with 32.7% for SGT and 26.2% for the strongest conventional baseline. These results demonstrate improvements in language quality and factual agreement. On TORS, GRAC achieved a CIDEr-D of 5.06, compared with 3.80 for the strongest baseline. Conclusion: GRAC demonstrates that combining structured surgical scene representations with retrieved examples improves surgical scene captioning and produces more contextually meaningful descriptions.|[2611.09866](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09866)|
|**2026-11-09**|**On Agentic Manipulation Policies**|Chen Chen et.al.|Recent work on robot manipulation has focused on specialist policies trained on demonstrations. We show that generalist AI models can control manipulators more reliably than specialist policies such as vision-language-action models (VLAs) and world-action models (WAMs). We systematically analyze agentic manipulation policies (AMPs), in which a generalist AI model commands the manipulator directly, adapting its actions as the task unfolds. We present AMP1, a reference harness that runs unchanged across single-arm, bimanual, and mobile manipulation. AMP1 achieves state-of-the-art results on the RoboLab-120 and CaP-Gym benchmarks, with no demonstrations or task-specific training. On RoboLab-120, AMP1 reaches an 85.6\% success rate zero-shot, compared to 49.2\% and 42.9\% for leading VLA and WAM policies. The agent succeeds zero-shot in 104 of 108 real-world trials spanning twelve tasks and three robot instances, reducing failure rates by 20x and 15x relative to strong VLA and WAM baselines. The basic harness supplies no grasping, pushing, or placing skills; the agent develops its own strategies, such as searching for hidden objects, using objects as tools, and calibrating its cameras. With the addition of a persistent memory store, the agent can also learn from experience: an augmented harness with a memory representation that is carried across episodes lowers the failure rate on RoboDojo bimanual manipulation tasks by 4.6x relative to zero-shot deployment. Our analysis suggests that AMPs are substantially more general and robust than specialist manipulation policies, providing a promising foundation for robot manipulation.|[2611.09892](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09892)|
|**2026-11-09**|**L-MARS: Legal Multi-Agent System with Agentic Search and Citation-Faithfulness Audit**|Boqin Yuan et.al.|Large language models are increasingly deployed for legal question answering, where evaluations typically focus on multiple-choice accuracy. This measure overlooks a common failure: whether the citation source attached to an answer exists and supports the rule the system attributes to it. We present L-MARS, an open multi-agent legal QA system with agentic search and judge-driven evidence checks, and audit it claim by claim against its cited source. Each atomic claim is labelled with a six-class taxonomy and scored with strict-ALCE under cross-provider judging, where the answerer and verifier come from different model families. On a stratified 100-question Bar Exam audit, retrieval barely moves accuracy, yet the multi-turn judge loop lifts strict citation F1 from 0.13 (naive RAG) to 0.25 and cuts the no-citation rate from 34% to 13%. We further introduce Faith-Search, a post-draft step that re-verifies and repairs unreachable citations; it drops the unreachable rate below 1% but does not improve F1 over the multi-turn loop, so we report it as a targeted reachability intervention rather than a faithfulness breakthrough. A 50-question LegalSearchQA case study confirms the picture: retrieve-then-draft pipelines saturate near 0.75 citation F1, while a single-agent web-search baseline collapses to 0.22 under external audit.|[2509.00761](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.00761)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Sentinel Agents for Secure and Trustworthy Agentic AI in Multi-Agent Systems**|Diego Gosmar et.al.|Multi-agent systems (MAS) built on large language models (LLMs) increasingly share a common conversational space, in which a single adversarial or erroneous message can propagate from one agent to many. This paper proposes Sentinel Agents, a dedicated security layer that monitors inter-agent communication with rule-based checks, behavioral analysis and LLM-based semantic inspection, and reports to a Coordinator Agent that enforces policy, for example by quarantining misbehaving agents. We describe the architecture, its deployment patterns and monitoring layers, and we evaluate a continuous-listener prototype on a balanced corpus of 1,000 synthetic messages (500 attacks across five families and 500 benign messages) with two local LLMs. LLM inspection with Gemma 4 12B reached F1 0.86 at a false-positive rate of 0.11, against 0.38 for rules alone. Combining components by logical OR did not improve on it, while a cascade in which only high-precision rules act on their own matched it. Calibrating the thresholds of the more conservative Qwen3 8B on a development split raised its held-out F1 from 0.56 to 0.80, and on multi-turn conversations the behavioral check detected patterns the rules missed but could not tell legitimate repetition from probing. The value of combining detectors therefore depends on the model, on its calibration and on the aggregation rule, and the results yield practical guidance for deploying Sentinel Agents.|[2509.14956](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.14956)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Limit Analysis for Symbolic Multi-step Reasoning Tasks with Information Propagation Rules Based on Transformers**|Tian Qin et.al.|Transformers are able to perform reasoning tasks, however the intrinsic mechanism remains widely open. In this paper we propose a set of information propagation rules based on Transformers and utilize symbolic reasoning tasks to theoretically analyze the limit reasoning steps. We show that the limit number of reasoning steps is between $2^{L-1}-1$ and $\frac{3^{L-1}-1}{2}$ for a model with $L$ attention layers in a single-pass. In addition, we find that, for problems with a fixed number of reasoning steps, Transformers exhibit a preference for generating sequences that satisfy certain ordering relations. Ultimately, we construct a Transformer and prove that the limiting reasoning depth is achievable.|[2509.23178](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.23178)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Comparing Explanations is Not Enough, Explain the Change: New Standards are Needed to Explain Behavioral Shifts in Large Language Models**|Martino Ciaperoni et.al.|Large-scale foundation models exhibit behavioral shifts when subjected to interventions such as scaling, fine-tuning, reinforcement learning with human feedback, or in-context learning. Current explainability methods are structurally ill-suited to explain these shifts, because they either treat models as static objects, as traditional eXplainable AI (XAI) approaches do, or merely compare independent explanations across different checkpoints of a model. As a result, these approaches fail to explain the functional transition between two model instances in which a certain behavior has shifted following an intervention. This gap creates significant governance risks across jurisdictions including the EU AI Act, US state legislation, and Chinese AI regulations, which require documenting causal chains for substantial system modifications. This position paper argues that explaining behavioral shifts in large language models requires a principled approach that treats the shift itself as the primary object of explanation: namely, one that explains how and why an intervention transforms a reference model into an updated model with different behavior. To support this claim, we introduce Comparative XAI (XAI$_\Delta$), a novel XAI paradigm aimed at explaining the difference between two model checkpoints where a behavior has shifted, together with a set of desiderata specifying what XAI$_\Delta$ explainers and explanations must satisfy, including comparability, validity, actionability, and monitoring, with the goal of grounding model auditing in explicit, measurable requirements. Finally, we provide preliminary evidence suggesting the need for XAI$_\Delta$ in practice through illustrative experiments, compiling the resulting findings into a transition report directly usable for governance and incident documentation.|[2602.02304](http://arxiv.org/abs/2602.02304)|
|**2026-11-09**|**OMG-Agent: Toward Robust Missing Modality Generation with Decoupled Coarse-to-Fine Agentic Workflows**|Ruiting Dai et.al.|Data incompleteness severely impedes the reliability of multimodal systems. Existing reconstruction methods face distinct bottlenecks: conventional parametric/generative models are prone to hallucinations due to over-reliance on internal memory, while retrieval-augmented frameworks struggle with retrieval rigidity. Critically, these end-to-end architectures are fundamentally constrained by Semantic-Detail Entanglement -- a structural conflict between logical reasoning and signal synthesis that compromises fidelity. In this paper, we present \textbf{\underline{O}}mni-\textbf{\underline{M}}odality \textbf{\underline{G}}eneration Agent (\textbf{OMG-Agent}), a novel framework that shifts the paradigm from static mapping to a dynamic coarse-to-fine Agentic Workflow. By mimicking a \textit{deliberate-then-act} cognitive process, OMG-Agent explicitly decouples the task into three synergistic stages: (1) an MLLM-driven Semantic Planner that resolves input ambiguity via Progressive Contextual Reasoning, creating a deterministic structured semantic plan; (2) a non-parametric Evidence Retriever that grounds abstract semantics in external knowledge; and (3) a Retrieval-Injected Executor that utilizes retrieved evidence as flexible feature prompts to overcome rigidity and synthesize high-fidelity details. Extensive experiments on multiple benchmarks demonstrate that OMG-Agent consistently surpasses state-of-the-art methods, maintaining robustness under extreme missingness, e.g., a $2.6$-point gain on CMU-MOSI at $70$\% missing rates.|[2602.04144](http://arxiv.org/abs/2602.04144)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Approximating the Reachability Value of Posterior-Deterministic POMDPs**|Nathana\"el Fijalkow et.al.|We introduce posterior-deterministic POMDPs, and we show that for this class of POMDPs, the maximal probability of reaching a given set of states can be approximated up to arbitrary precision. A POMDP is posterior-deterministic if the next state can be uniquely determined by the current state, the action taken, and the observation received. In particular, once the current state is known, it remains known forever. The class contains, for instance, multiple-environment MDPs equipped with a prior distribution over the environments, quasi-deterministic POMDPs, and the episodic version of the classical Tiger POMDP (Kaelbling et al. 1998).|[2602.07473](http://arxiv.org/abs/2602.07473)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Counterfactual Distillation: Internalizing Reflective Experience into LLM Agent**|Rui Ge et.al.|Large language models increasingly act as autonomous agents in long-horizon interactions, learning from final outcomes and environmental feedback. However, optimizing from such interaction experience remains challenging, as feedback is heterogeneous and rarely carries explicit reward information. Existing post-training approaches rely on outcome-driven methods, such as reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR), which primarily exploit final success signals while leaving interaction experience and feedback underutilized. In sparse-reward, long-horizon tasks, this often results in distribution sharpening: the policy reinforces a narrow set of already-successful behaviors, without substantially improving the feedback-grounded agency needed for broader problem-solving capacity (e.g., Pass@k). We propose Counterfactual Distillation, a framework that converts exploration-derived experience into trainable policy supervision. Specifically, we organize exploration as a tree-structured search process, where the agent reflects on past decision points and makes new experience-guided decisions to form alternative branches. These corrections are then distilled as counterfactual targets: we train the model to predict the revised action from the original history without explicitly providing the experience, thereby introducing out-of-distribution behavior patterns and expanding exploration capacity. Across diverse interactive coding and agentic tasks, our method outperforms outcome-driven baselines such as GRPO and experience-based methods such as Early Experience, with gains of up to 14 percentage points over the base model on Pass@128. When interleaved with reinforcement learning updates, it further raises the performance ceiling, improving Pass@1 on Sokoban by 8 percentage points over extended GRPO. Our code is available at https://github.com/motigrez/Counterfactual-Distillation.|[2603.16843](http://arxiv.org/abs/2603.16843)|
|**2026-11-09**|**EvoAgent: An Evolvable Agent Framework with Skill Learning and Multi-Agent Delegation**|Aimin Zhang et.al.|This paper proposes EvoAgent--an evolvable large language model (LLM) agent framework that integrates structured skill learning with a hierarchical sub-agent delegation mechanism. EvoAgent models skills as multi-file structured capability units equipped with triggering mechanisms and evolutionary metadata, and is designed to enable continuous skill generation and optimization through a user-feedback-driven closed-loop process. In addition, by incorporating a three-stage skill matching strategy and organizing the agent as a harness whose only self-modified component is a persistent, user-owned state (profile, memory and skills), the framework supports dynamic task decomposition for complex problems and long-term capability accumulation. Experimental results based on real-world foreign trade scenarios demonstrate that, after integrating EvoAgent, GPT-5.2 achieves substantial improvements in professionalism, accuracy, and practical utility. Under a five-dimensional LLM-as-Judge evaluation protocol, the overall average score increases by approximately 28%. Further model transfer experiments indicate that the performance of an agent system depends not only on the intrinsic capabilities of the underlying model, but also on the degree of synergy between the model and the agent architecture. Code and documentation are released at https://github.com/Focus-AI-Center/Mentarc-EvoAgent; the evaluation corpus contains proprietary business data that cannot be released, so the reported results are for reference only.|[2604.20133](http://arxiv.org/abs/2604.20133)|
|**2026-11-09**|**ClawTrace: Cost-Aware Tracing for LLM Agent Skill Distillation**|Boqin Yuan et.al.|Skill-distillation pipelines learn reusable rules from LLM agent trajectories, but they lack a key signal: how much each step costs. Without per-step cost, a pipeline cannot distinguish adding a missing step to fix a bug from removing an expensive step that never affected the outcome. We use the cost-attribution gap to ask whether the rule types inside a distilled skill transfer the same way to new tasks. ClawTrace records cost-attributed agent traces and compiles each session into a TraceCard; CostCraft reads TraceCards and writes three kinds of skill patches: preserve, prune, and repair. We find a pattern aggregate metrics hide. On 30 held-out SpreadsheetBench tasks across two seeds, removing prune patches roughly tripled the quality-regression count without lowering median cost. Across the full 84-task SkillsBench transfer, CostCraft saves no aggregate cost. All three quality regressions trace to the preserve lane, and both quality wins trace to the prune lane: prune patches act as quality guardrails while preserve patches drive regressions. We argue that reusable agent skills should be evaluated at the rule-type level, not as monolithic instruction packages. To support this, we release ClawTrace, the TraceCard schema, and the full set of typed skills.|[2604.23853](http://arxiv.org/abs/2604.23853)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Taming the Centaur(s) with LAPITHS: a framework for a theoretically grounded interpretation of AI performances**|Matteo Da Pelo et.al.|We introduce a framework called LAPITHS (Language model Analysis through Paradigm grounded Interpretations of Theses about Human likenesS) and use it to show that several major claims advanced by models such as CENTAUR, proposed as an artificial Unified Model of Cognition, are not theoretically or empirically justified. The novelty of LAPITHS lies in making explicit the arguments grounded in two quantitative assessments: (i) the Minimal Cognitive Grid, a theoretically motivated method for estimating the cognitive plausibility of artificial systems, and (ii) a behavioural comparison showing that results similar to those reported for CENTAUR like models can be reproduced by other systems that do not satisfy the structural constraints typically associated with cognitive plausibility, and whose outputs do not provide independent explanatory insight into human cognition.|[2604.27927](http://arxiv.org/abs/2604.27927)|
|**2026-11-09**|**TimeTok: Granularity-Controllable Time-Series Generation via Hierarchical Tokenization**|Seokhyun Lee et.al.|Time-series data are inherently multiscale, spanning diverse temporal granularities from coarse trends to fine-scale dynamics. However, existing time-series generative models provide limited control over the temporal granularity of both inputs and outputs, restricting their ability to condition on user-provided coarse sketches and generate samples at a desired target granularity. To address this, we introduce TimeTok, a unified framework for Granularity-Controllable Time-Series Generation (GC-TSG), which generates time series at any target granularity from any coarser input (e.g., rough sketches) or without conditioning. At the core of TimeTok is a hierarchical tokenization strategy that maps time series into an ordered sequence of tokens, from coarse to fine temporal granularity. Our autoregressive generation process operates across these granularity levels, producing token blocks that are decoded back into continuous time series. This design naturally enables GC-TSG within a single framework, where controlling the number of token blocks provides explicit control over output detail. Experiments show that TimeTok excels at GC-TSG tasks while achieving state-of-the-art performance in standard generation. Furthermore, we showcase TimeTok's potential as a foundational tokenizer by training on multiple datasets with heterogeneous temporal granularities, verifying strong transferability that consistently outperforms models trained on individual datasets. To our knowledge, this is the first unified framework that covers the full generative spectrum for time series, offering a valuable foundation for models that benefit from diverse temporal granularities.|[2605.01418](http://arxiv.org/abs/2605.01418)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Intentmaking and Sensemaking: Human Interaction with AI-Guided Mathematical Discovery**|Alex B\"auerle et.al.|Artificial intelligence offers powerful paradigms for scientific discovery, orchestrating autonomous inner search loops over long horizons. However, discovery remains bottlenecked by the friction of the outer human-AI loop: the cycle where domain experts formulate research objectives, diagnose reward hacks, and extract meaning. We present findings from a study with 11 expert mathematicians using AlphaEvolve, an evolutionary coding agent, across more than 2,300 experiments over three months. We identify and characterize intentmaking, the iterative process of discovering, defining, and refining experimental goals through system interaction, as a reciprocal counterpart to sensemaking. We observe that domain experts effectively alternate between steering the search space (intentmaking) and extracting mathematical insights (sensemaking). Our findings suggest an approach to designing AI tools for scientific discovery that moves beyond the existing question--answer model, treating them as collaborative instruments rather than opaque black-box oracles.|[2605.05921](http://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05921)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Self-Programmed Execution for Language-Model Agents**|Luke J. O'Connor et.al.|At the heart of a language model agent is an orchestrator program which is responsible for the state transition between consecutive turns. In existing systems, this program is implemented in the harness. This paper introduces self-programmed execution (SPE), in which the model completion is itself the orchestrator program, and the harness evaluates this program but does not impose any fixed orchestration policy. Formally, an SPE state of an agentic machine is one from which a model completion can load any successor state up to an embedding, and an instance of an SPE state is one which executes the model completion as a program. In practice, the program requires a language with unusual features, as its source code doubles as the content of the model's context window. I therefore introduce Spell, a Lisp dialect in which programs can edit and re-evaluate themselves, and effectful expressions like model invocations are structured such that re-evaluating an edited program does not replay its side effects. Experiments with existing models, not trained for SPE or Spell, show that frontier models can learn Spell in-context and accomplish challenging agentic tasks. These results demonstrate how an LM can act as an agent without any fixed orchestration policy, and they raise the question of what self-orchestration strategies might be learned by a model trained for self-programmed execution. Code is available at https://github.com/lukejoconnor/spell.|[2605.06898](http://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06898)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Embeddings for Preferences, Not Semantics**|Carter Blair et.al.|Modern AI is opening the door to collective decision-making in which participants express their views as free-form text rather than voting on a fixed set of candidates. A natural idea is to embed these opinions in a vector space so that the substantial literature on facility location problems and fair clustering can be brought to bear. But standard text embeddings measure semantic similarity, whereas distances in facility location problems and fair clustering require what we call preferential similarity: a participant's agreement with a piece of text should be inversely related to their distance from it. Off-the-shelf embeddings inherit a coarse preference signal through a correlation between semantic and preferential similarity, but fail to capture preferences when the correlation breaks. We formalize this as an invariance problem: text embedding models encode both a preference-relevant signal (stance and values) and semantic nuisance (style and wording), and the two are observationally correlated, so a geometry that relies on nuisance can appear preference-correct even when it is not. We show that synthetic training data designed to break this correlation provably shifts the optimal scorer away from nuisance-dominated cosine and significantly improves preference prediction across 11 online deliberation datasets.|[2605.08360](http://arxiv.org/abs/2605.08360)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Agentic AI Scientists Are Not Built For Autonomous Scientific Discovery**|Harshit Bisht et.al.|A growing body of work pursues AI scientists capable of end-to-end autonomous scientific discovery. This position paper argues that although they already function as co-scientists, agentic AI scientists are not built for autonomous scientific discovery. We identify the following challenges in building and deploying autonomous AI scientists: (1) Problem selection is influenced by the McNamara fallacy; (2) Agents are built on large language models (LLMs) whose training corpora omit tacit procedural and failure knowledge of laboratory practice; (3) Preference optimisation during post-training compresses output diversity toward consensus; and (4) Most scientific benchmarks measure single-turn prediction accuracy and lack feedback from physical experiments back to the computational model. These challenges are not just questions of scale and scaffolding; they require revisiting fundamental design choices. To build truly autonomous AI scientists, we recommend scientific simulations as verifiers for training, a persistent, mutable epistemic state that carries beliefs and shifting objectives across an investigation, the establishment of a centralized preregistration repository for all AI-generated hypotheses, and application driven by scientific need rather than tool affordance.|[2605.08956](http://arxiv.org/abs/2605.08956)|
|**2026-11-09**|**NeuroMAS: Multi-Agent Systems as Neural Networks with Joint Reinforcement Learning**|Haoran Lu et.al.|Multi-agent language systems are often built as hand-designed workflows, where agents are assigned semantic roles and communication protocols are specified in advance. We propose NeuroMAS, a framework that treats a multi-agent language system as a trainable and scalable neural-network-like architecture with hidden textual intermediate signals. In NeuroMAS, language-agent nodes are role-free but structure-aware: the topology determines how information can flow, while reward-based training determines how nodes communicate, specialize, and coordinate. This formulation shifts multi-agent design from workflow engineering toward architecture design, where depth, width, connectivity, and growth protocol become scalable sources of capability. Experiments show that NeuroMAS improves over both inference-time collaboration methods and trained baselines, and that its gains are not explained by trainable parameter count alone. We further find that organizational scaling is path-dependent: larger systems can fail when trained from scratch, but become stronger when grown progressively from smaller trained systems. These results suggest that learned multi-agent organization is a promising scaling axis for language-model reasoning and generation.|[2605.16757](http://arxiv.org/abs/2605.16757)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Learning to Solve Compositional Geometry Routing Problems**|Mingfeng Fan et.al.|We study the Compositional Geometry Routing Problem (CGRP), a unified superclass of traditional routing problems that covers point-only, line-only, area-only, and arbitrary hybrid task geometries, providing a broad abstraction for real-world routing scenarios. Beyond standard point-based routing, CGRP with non-point tasks can be inherently asymmetric, tightly coupled travel routes with the intrinsic path, and enlarges the action space with numerous feasible yet often irrelevant options, thereby posing significant challenges for both representation learning and decision-making. To address these challenges, we propose DiCon, a differential attention-assisted solver with contrastive learning, as a plug-and-play framework that tackles the problem from two complementary angles. Second, we design a double-level contrastive learning objective to promote robust global instance representations and regularize geometry-aware task representations. Extensive experiments demonstrate that DiCon achieves strong performance, broad versatility, and superior generalization across diverse CGRP instances with different compositions.|[2605.18094](http://arxiv.org/abs/2605.18094)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Gated DeltaNet-2: Decoupling Erase and Write in Linear Attention**|Ali Hatamizadeh et.al.|Linear attention replaces the unbounded cache of softmax attention with a fixed-size recurrent state, reducing sequence mixing to linear time and decoding to constant memory. We derive a fast-weight update view, a chunkwise WY algorithm with channel-wise decay absorbed into asymmetric erase factors, and a gate-aware backward pass that preserves efficient parallel training. At 1.3B parameters trained on 100B FineWeb-Edu tokens, Gated DeltaNet-2 achieves the strongest overall results among Mamba-2, Gated DeltaNet, KDA, and Mamba-3 variants across language modeling, commonsense reasoning, and retrieval. Its advantage is most pronounced on long-context RULER needle-in-a-haystack benchmarks, where it improves the evaluated multi-key retrieval setting and remains strong in both recurrent and hybrid settings. Code is available at https://github.com/NVlabs/GatedDeltaNet-2.|[2605.22791](http://arxiv.org/abs/2605.22791)|
|**2026-11-09**|**SuiChat-CN: Benchmarking Contextual Suicide Risk Assessment in Chinese Group Chats**|Xiangyu Wang et.al.|Suicide is a critical global public health challenge, causing approximately 720,000 deaths each year and calling for timely, effective prevention strategies. Existing computational studies primarily focus on post-based social media platforms such as Twitter and Weibo, leaving instant messaging environments such as Telegram underexplored. We introduce SuiChat-CN, a Chinese group-chat benchmark for contextual suicide risk assessment. We collect public Telegram group-chat data, construct coherent conversational segments through signal-word extraction and bidirectional context expansion, and annotate user risk levels with an expert-validated, LLM-assisted paradigm. SuiChat-CN contains 13,312 contextual segments from 1,406 users, covering 258,228 raw chat messages. Extensive experiments with PLMs and more than 40 LLMs demonstrate that contextual information is essential for reliable risk assessment, while fine-tuning and partial-context evaluation further reveal the challenges of early detection in multi-party conversations. |**2026-11-09**|**WorldCoder-Bench: Benchmarking Physically Grounded 3D World Synthesis**|Shuo Lu et.al.|Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly asked not only to write static interfaces, but to construct executable interactive worlds from natural language. Existing web-generation benchmarks and evaluators, however, largely observe only pixels or DOM nodes, while the mechanics of a Three.js world unfold inside an opaque <canvas>. The best verification coverage is 27.8% across nine models on WorldCoder-Benchcore and 19.9% across five on WorldCoder-Benchrob, with failures dominated by state-schema drift and broken interaction chains rather than missing scene elements. |**2026-11-09**|**Uncertainty Aware Functional Behavior Prediction and Material Fatigue Assessment for Circular Factory**|Nehal Afifi et.al.|Returned products in circular factories re-enter production with heterogeneous degradation states, usage histories, and remaining capability. |**2026-11-09**|**From Senses to Decisions: The Information Flow of Auditory and Visual Perception in Multimodal LLMs**|Wish Suharitdamrong et.al.|Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) can listen and see, but how do audio and visual signals actually travel through the network to shape an answer? |**2026-11-09**|**Agentic Abstention: Do Agents Know When to Stop Instead of Act?**|Han Luo et.al.|LLM agents are expected to act over multiple turns, using search, browsing interfaces, and tools to complete user goals. |**2026-11-09**|**Coachable Agents for Interactive Gameplay**|Roberto Capobianco (Sony Europe Ltd) et.al.|Reinforcement learning has proven to be a valuable tool in the creation of advanced AI and robotic systems, contributing to everything from game playing (Silver et al., 2016; 2017; Wurman et al., 2022) to robotics (Levine et al., 2016; Andrychowicz et al., 2020; Luo et al., 2025; D\"urr et al., 2026) to foundation models (Ouyang et al., 2022; Noukhovitch et al., 2024; Sun et al., 2024; Guo et al., 2025). |**2026-11-09**|**EVOQUANT: Self-Evolving Verifier-Guided Strategy Optimization for Robust Quantitative Trading**|Jie Mao et.al.|Quantitative strategy optimization remains largely manual, requiring domain experts to identify weak signals, tune risk-control rules, and repeatedly validate iterative revisions. |**2026-11-09**|**GABench: A Comprehensive Benchmark for Evaluating LLM Agents on Graph Analysis Tasks**|Jiarui Tan et.al.|Large language model (LLM) agents are increasingly capable of planning, using tools, and interacting with external environments. However, existing graph benchmarks for LLMs provide limited coverage of graph tasks and graph types, making it difficult to comprehensively evaluate LLM agents. Our experiments reveal three key findings: (1) Existing LLM agents still struggle with complex graph analysis tasks. |**2026-11-09**|**Recursive Synthesis for Long-Horizon Terminal Tasks**|Zhongzhi Li et.al.|High-quality long-horizon training data for terminal agents is costly and difficult to scale, as manual authoring is expensive and direct LLM generation often produces inconsistent instructions, environments, solutions, and verifiers. |**2026-11-09**|**Improving Generalization Robustness of Multimodal RLVR**|Pengfei Zhou et.al.|Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR) makes Multimodal Large Language Models more accurate, but the gains are brittle: simply paraphrasing a question or changing the prompt template can degrade them, which challenges reliable deployment in high-stakes scenarios like medical VQA. |**2026-11-09**|**Who Speaks Matters: Authority-Aware Multi-View RAG over Italian Parliamentary Proceedings**|Mirko Tritella et.al.|Parliamentary proceedings are a primary record of democratic deliberation, yet their volume and fragmentation make multi-perspective access difficult for citizens, journalists, and researchers. The system achieves higher coverage across political groups (0.97 vs. 0.95), perfect quotation faithfulness (1.00 vs. 0.95), and stronger expert preferences on source-related dimensions, while NotebookLM remains stronger on prose-oriented dimensions.|[2608.13410](http://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13410)|
|**2026-11-09**|**TailSieve: Partial-Rollout-Guided Tail Routing for LLM Rollouts**|Tianqi Xu et.al.|Large-scale rollouts have become a core component of modern LLM systems, spanning reinforcement learning (RL) post-training, on-policy distillation, and sampling-heavy evaluation. Unlike online serving, which typically targets request-level latency and throughput, synchronous rollouts must collect a complete batch before proceeding. A few long-tail generations can therefore dominate an entire rollout step, and uniform routing can slow these generations further by placing them in high-concurrency decoding batches. We present TailSieve, a partial-rollout-guided framework that jointly controls tail routing and replica allocation. Motivated by the observed persistence of prompt-level tail tendencies across policy updates, TailSieve uses partial-rollout completion status as a training-free signal for identifying candidate tail groups. Selected prompts are combined with newly sampled prompts, and all responses are generated from scratch under the current policy. A hierarchical controller uses response-work history and a serving model: a fast inner loop adjusts the number of isolated groups to balance completion times, while a slower outer loop adapts how many replicas serve these groups. |**2026-11-09**|**Agentic AI uncovers conserved cross-tissue protein co-abundance programs inaccessible to single-dataset analysis**|Dehao Wu et.al.|Protein co-abundance clusters preserved across tissues can nominate shared disease mechanisms and therapeutic targets, particularly where proteins implicated in organ-confined diseases converge in other tissues. We present an LLM-agent framework for large-scale, evidence-grounded comparison of tissue-specific protein co-abundance networks. From sample-level proteomic data, it constructs tissue networks, derives conserved clusters for each tissue pair, integrates evidence from expression atlases, interaction and complex databases, pathways, disease associations and the literature, and proposes mechanistic hypotheses in which every conclusion cites its evidence. Applied to all 231 pairs among 22 human tissues and sample groups, it identified 1,825 conserved clusters in 216 tissue pairs. Co-abundance relationships were shared beyond chance in 76% of pairs, including unrelated organs, and shared clusters were only weakly associated with abundance similarity. Clusters were enriched 3.7- to 6.9-fold for known interactions and complex memberships, and 379 of 793 testable clusters replicated in independent CPTAC proteomes. Code: https://github.com/jacob20030316-crypto/Protein_Co_Abundance_Agent|[2608.28990](http://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28990)|
|**2026-11-09**|**AgentGrad: Intervention-guided Prompt Optimization for Multi-Agent Systems**|Jaewon Chu et.al.|Large language model (LLM)-based multi-agent systems (MAS) achieve strong performance by employing specialized multiple agents, yet their performance depends on the prompt design of each agent. In this paper, we identify limitations in two stages of existing textual gradient approaches: gradient extraction and gradient aggregation. In gradient extraction, previous works select a prompt to update without verifying whether modifying it resolves the failure, and derive gradients without agent-level supervision over the corresponding agent's intermediate output. In gradient aggregation, individual gradients are randomly grouped and concatenated, often mixing unrelated failure modes and producing prompts that fail to generalize. To address these limitations, we propose AgentGrad, a prompt optimization framework for multi-agent systems based on sequential intervention and semantic textual gradient abstraction. For each failure, sequential intervention injects a hint into one agent at a time to identify the target agent whose modification resolves the failure. The modified output of the target agent then serves as agent-level supervision for extracting a fine-grained gradient. Semantic textual gradient abstraction clusters semantically similar gradients to prevent mixing unrelated failure modes, and abstracts each cluster into a generalized gradient that captures the shared corrective pattern. Experimental results show that AgentGrad achieves state-of-the-art performance across five MAS benchmarks while reducing wall-clock optimization time by 2.5 times and optimization cost by 21.8% on average compared to the next-best baselines. Our code is available at https://github.com/mlvlab/AgentGrad.|[2609.08572](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08572)|
|**2026-11-09**|**LLMs as Post-hoc Auditors of Physiological Plausibility in Symbolic Regression: A Clinician-Evaluated Case Study**|Jorge L\'opez-Varela et.al.|Genetic programming and its variants, such as grammatical evolution, are widely used in symbolic regression to derive mathematical expressions from multivariate data. In this study, we explore whether large language models (LLMs) can assist in improving the interpretability of symbolic regression models generated by evolutionary computation methods. Building on our previous work on estimating body fat percentage using grammar-based genetic programming, we investigate LLMs as post-processing tools to analyze and rank evolved expressions by interpretability and physiological plausibility. However, the LLMs also produced physiologically and mathematically questionable explanations, indicating that they are better suited to comparative auditing under expert oversight than to autonomous validation.|[2609.11431](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11431)|
|**2026-11-09**|**MoMHa: Multi-Objective Optimization of LLM Harnesses over Accuracy, Safety, and Tokens**|Subhojyoti Mukherjee et.al.|Most work on improving large language models treats accuracy as the sole objective. We argue that the harness, the Python code surrounding the model that constructs prompts, routes calls, and parses outputs, is a first-class design surface whose quality is inherently multi-objective: an accurate harness that refuses no unsafe request, or that consumes an order of magnitude more tokens, is not a good harness. We present Meta-Harness, a system that casts harness design as search over three per-domain objectives (accuracy, behavioural safety, and token cost) solved by an agentic proposer (Claude Code) with full filesystem access to prior harness source, execution traces, and scoring artifacts. Our central finding is that a singlephase joint-reward proposer (MoMHa) outperforms every alternative, including a two-phase "accuracy then tokens" ablation, scalar-only feedback, and an accuracy-only baseline. We evaluate on seventeen domains: seven synthetic capability suites, seven real-world public benchmarks (HumanEval, MBPP, Spider, FEVER, MMLU-Pro, LawBench, NuminaMath), and three U-SafeBench-derived user-specific safety domains, using a 12-model fleet spanning four families. On the synthetic track MoMHa achieves a joint mean of 0.482 versus 0.198-0.422 for ten baselines, winning $7 / 10$ per-domain columns; on the real-world track it scores 0.461 versus 0.377 for the strongest baseline (DSPy), winning 5/7 columns, demonstrating that harness strategies transfer to unseen benchmarks without retraining on 8 of 12 target models. We will release all harness code, evaluation infrastructure, and crossmodel logs.|[2609.30967](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30967)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Programs-of-Layers in LLMs through the Lens of Cortical Areas**|Justus Westerhoff et.al.|Inference in LLMs is conventionally a fixed-depth, fixed-order forward pass through every layer, regardless of how difficult the input is. The human brain does not work this way. Using the thalamus as a central hub, it routes information flexibly to all regions of the cortex according to demand. Li et al. (2026) recently showed, with program-of-layers (PoLar), that transformers can be given an analogous flexibility if their layers are treated as a library of functions rather than a fixed sequence. Performance improves over the standard forward pass when each input is dynamically routed through an adaptive sequence of skipped or repeated contiguous layer blocks. Reconstructing PoLar's diagnostic Monte Carlo Tree Search in more detail across 6 models, we reproduce several of its findings: skipping outperforms the standard pass, repeating outperforms skipping, and combining both outperforms either alone. Shorter programs suffice for easier questions, while harder questions require more layer repeats. For their main claim, the learned router's gain, we find what drives it. Under their setup, the router gains by repairing the format of the model's answers, which a single fixed program, selected on validation questions, does better. In our setup, with a prompt that already fixes the format, a larger data split and our search, its top prediction collapses to the standard pass on every model, although its top-5 programs together still gain. Beyond reproduction, we find in our setup that a small fixed menu of programs solves far more questions than the standard pass, and that programs which correct errors are brittle: undoing a single edit typically breaks the correction. PoLar mirrors principles of thalamo-cortical coordination between cortical-area-like transformer layers, although a working thalamus analogue, a router that picks a better program per input, remains to be found.|[2609.31360](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31360)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Structured Sparse Memory for Recurrent Reasoning**|Zixuan Zhao et.al.|Recurrent models trained from scratch have recently become competitive on ARC-style reasoning tasks, but the usual framing around small recurrent backbones overlooks two important parts of the system: task-conditioned memory and synthetic augmentation data. In existing approaches, task-conditioned memory supplies a large hidden source of capacity, reaching more than 30x the size of the recurrent backbone. We introduce a compositional sparse embedding (CoSE) for task conditioning that reduces learned task-memory parameters by over 90% while improving pass@2 in controlled ARC ablations. We also introduce Re-ARC2, a set of programs that generate and verify new examples for all ARC-AGI-2 training tasks. Combining these ingredients, our system reaches 84% pass@2 on ARC-AGI-1 and 46.7% pass@2 on ARC-AGI-2 public evaluation. Structured memory also enables adaptation with only 512 parameters per unseen puzzle, and its benefits generalize to other domains. Our code, dataset, and model checkpoints are available at https://github.com/water-vapor/charm.|[2609.33270](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.33270)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Code Owns the Simulation, Jev Owns the Evaluation**|Yaodong Yang et.al.|Judgment models such as \jev{} return, in a single call and without reasoning text, a probability for each described option. This makes them attractive as an agent's action-selection layer, but it is unclear which decisions they can be trusted with. We test \jev{} on reflection tests, one-shot matrix games, the text game ALFWorld and robot control, and find a sharp boundary. \jev{} succeeds when the right option can be judged from what the input describes, which we call \emph{evaluation}. Specifically, it solves 99\% of the counterintuitive Cognitive Reflection Test questions. However, it fails when the right option depends on \emph{simulation} (i.e., predicting something not in the input), such as the opponent's action or the subgoal that must come first. In games, \jev{} plays suboptimally as if its rational opponent acted at random, because the opponent's action is not given. In ALFWorld, \jev{} favors commands that mention an object or place named in the task description. For example, given the task ``put a clean knife in the drawer'', \jev{} carries an unwashed knife straight to the drawer instead of first washing it at the sink. Surprisingly, many of these failures are not due to a lack of knowledge. Asked separately what the opponent will do, \jev{} usually answers correctly, and it responds well given the opponent's action. When code supplies it, such as a lookahead in ALFWorld and physics simulation in robot control, \jev{} becomes an expert controller through its general evaluation ability.|[2610.01834](http://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01834)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Continual Graph Memory and Community Infrastructure for AI-Assisted Mathematical Discovery**|Eric Hanchen Jiang et.al.|Mathematical discovery requires preserving partial ideas and the evidence supporting their reuse. We present Ansatz, a research harness whose continual graph memory separates model-accepted claims and dependencies from curated exploration, and Ansatze, a community platform for inspecting, guiding and checking this work. On First Proof, Ansatz recorded nine local verifier closures in each of two ten-problem batches; a partial result in the Second Batch also counted as a pass under the study's criterion. These model-assessed outcomes include continued searches and differ from expert validation. Component studies and re-review expose disagreements and their dependency reach. Open-problem investigations produced candidate results, with five Lean formalizations reported. The platform connects problem discovery, research records, manuscript analysis and formal checking while preserving distinct evidence states. Together, these systems support cumulative human-AI research through persistent investigation and inspectable contributions.|[2610.02945](http://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02945)|
|**2026-11-09**|**HarnessNet: Feedback-driven Hierarchical Joint Harness Evolution for Multi-agent Systems**|Zhaoyuan Qiu et.al.|AI agents can evolve by using environment feedback to revise their harnesses, the software managing model inputs, tool use, memory, and execution, while keeping foundation models frozen. We propose HarnessNet, a framework for hierarchical harness evolution in MAS. Its basic unit, Agentron, comprises an evolvable forward harness, a foundation model, and a local optimizer that can observe and modify only that harness. Agentrons form a layered graph through which environment feedback propagates backward, with upstream optimizers receiving node-specific feedback from the downstream layer. This decomposes joint harness evolution into smaller, coordinated tasks with separate contexts, reducing individual optimizers' context and workload while keeping access local. Optimizers need not access all agent trajectories or raw environment feedback. Experiments on AgentBench with multiple foundation models demonstrate the feasibility of training HarnessNet. Compared with a single global optimizer, HarnessNet achieves comparable performance gains while respecting local privacy and requiring fewer tokens and less time per optimizer call. |**2026-11-09**|**AuthGateBench: Benchmarking LLMs as Authorization Gate**|Zhixiong Zhuang et.al.|Automated approval systems let coding agents execute high-risk actions with minimal human oversight by delegating the authorization decision to a classifier, typically a large language model (LLM). Evaluations show accuracy degrades unevenly across failure modes as models over-rely on internal safety alignment, and models still block most actions even when explicitly authorized, revealing unstable rather than deterministic decisions; retrospectively retrieved environment signals, however, substantially improve authorization accuracy.|[2610.16426](http://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16426)|
|**2026-11-09**|**SteadyThought: Mitigating LLM Under-Thinking via Thought-Level Preference Optimization**|Zhenyue Peng et.al.|Flexible switching between reasoning trajectories (i.e., thought switching) has significantly enhanced the reasoning capabilities of Large Reasoning Models (LRMs). To address this challenge, we propose Steady Thought (ST), a novel thought-level preference optimization framework. ST formalizes under-thinking as a preference issue at switching points, forcing single-path continuations from identified points to yield high-quality reasoning. Then, ST performs thought-level preference optimization by treating the newly generated response as preferred and the original one as dis-preferred. Experiments across multiple models and datasets show that ST effectively reduces token consumption, achieving reductions of up to 63.8%, while maintaining strong reasoning accuracy. Code is available at https://github.com/MIRALab-USTC/SteadyThought.|[2610.25045](http://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25045)|
|**2026-11-09**|**CoRS: Fast Neural Flexible Job-Shop Scheduling via Composite Interval State Reuse**|Sutong Jiang et.al.|Neural solvers for the Flexible Job-Shop Scheduling Problem (FJSP) typically refresh graph representations and update feasibility after every operation--machine decision. However, only feasibility must remain exact at every step, whereas graph encoding is substantially more expensive and can potentially be refreshed less frequently. |**2026-11-09**|**REFLEX: RNA Ensemble Generation via Flexibility-Calibrated Stochastic Bridge**|Wanli Ma et.al.|RNA molecules often function through multiple experimentally observed conformational states. A central challenge is to capture ensemble diversity without sacrificing structural fidelity: samples may collapse toward averaged representative structures or drift into unsupported conformational regions. |**2026-11-09**|**HarnessMM: Recursive Self-Improvement for Harness Token Efficiency**|Yuhua Jiang et.al.|HarnessMinusMinus (HarnessMM) is a research project on improving the efficiency of agent harnesses, the software coordinating model reasoning, tool execution, and feedback. RSI refines author prompts, context settings, and adapter rules using training feedback, with validation selecting deployment configurations. At inference time, a controller reuses resolved decisions while keeping actor weights fixed. Executable Decision Graphs encode actions, arguments, dependencies, and guards. Guarded Decision Reuse activates eligible decisions from observed feedback, while Certified Callback Substitution executes them through native tool interfaces, replacing redundant reasoning callbacks. The original actor resumes when new reasoning is required or reuse conditions are unmet. Code is available at https://github.com/YuhuaJiang2002/HarnessMM.|[2610.32813](http://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32813)|
|**2026-11-09**|**DecoyCount: Evaluating Visual-Reasoning Misalignment in Counting**|Qiuyi (Richard) et.al.|Visual counting benchmarks report whether vision-language models produce the correct number, but not whether errors arise from perception or reasoning. Despite being highly capable on general reasoning, frontier models remain below 50% accuracy on our benchmark, while alignment gaps range from 12.1-22.1 percentage points; strikingly, we observe that more capable models, greater thinking budgets, and agentic processes often induce larger gaps. |**2026-11-09**|**Radial Kernel Attention for Global Feature Exchange in Polymer Machine Learning Force Fields**|Wenbo Fu et.al.|We introduce Radial Kernel Attention (RKA) for long-range modeling in polymer machine learning force fields. Existing approaches often rely on local neighbor graphs, which limit communication across polymer chains and their surrounding environments, whereas explicit all-pair attention incurs quadratic computational cost. |**2026-11-09**|**A Discrete-Time Variational Objective for Degenerate Diffusion and Equivalence to Flow Matching**|Rares Dolga et.al.|Diffusion models and more recently Flow models have widespread application. In this work we consider degenerate Diffusion models in which the per-step generator is deterministic. Our work therefore provides a unified perspective, showing that discrete-time implementations of flow matching and the discrete-time Degenerate Diffusion model can be considered within the same simple variational framework, removing the need for the continuous-time machinery normally used to justify flow matching models.|[2611.00800](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00800)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Seeing What Matters, Verifying What Pays: Load-Adaptive Speculative Decoding for Vision-Language Models**|Hong Nie et.al.|Speculative decoding accelerates vision-language model (VLM) inference without altering the output distribution, yet two factors limit its gains. Existing drafters either lack the image evidence required for visually grounded tokens or pay to condition on every visual token, although a short draft block typically depends on only a few image regions. To allocate verification, a marginal-cost criterion selects the structure per request and per round: draft trees exploit idle verifier capacity under light load, whereas a dynamic head truncates linear drafts under heavy load. |**2026-11-09**|**LUCA: A Synthetic World for Experimental Reasoning**|Anna Gogleva et.al.|An experimental agent can succeed for the wrong reasons. Evaluations, however, often focus on the outcome, not the experiments, evidence, and inferences that produced it. We introduce LUCA, a configurable synthetic world for evaluating this process under fully known ground truth. Agents learn unknown regulatory systems through budgeted experimentation, then predict unseen interventions, diagnose hidden faults, and attempt rescues. This setup allows us to evaluate experiment selection, prediction, diagnosis, and intervention separately. Across seven frontier LLMs and three baselines, these capabilities dissociate sharply. Agents frequently rescue malfunctioning systems while diagnosing the wrong fault; diagnosis varies 7x across models while prediction varies only 1.4x. Models also adopt surprisingly different experimental strategies, and greater exploration does not reliably yield better performance. In one failure mode, agents often perform the experiment needed to distinguish competing causes, but still fail to use the resulting evidence. LUCA turns these hidden failures into measurable behaviours, providing a controlled environment to study where experimental agents succeed and fail. Code: https://github.com/RelationRx/luca.|[2611.03100](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03100)|
|**2026-11-09**|**AceAction-5M: Hierarchical Goals for Generalist Action Learning Across 3D Game Worlds**|Kailing Li et.al.|Video games provide a practical setting for collecting vision-language-action (VLA) data, whose acquisition in physical environments is costly. Existing datasets, however, remain limited by restricted game coverage, sparse language supervision, or temporal hierarchies that do not necessarily reflect changes in task objectives. We introduce AceAction, a dataset of human gameplay across multiple 3D games for learning skill composition and subgoal transitions. AceAction organizes demonstrations around complete tasks, linking overall instructions, ordered subgoals, and local skill instructions to temporally aligned visual observations and keyboard-and-mouse actions. To construct these annotations, we develop a vision-language model pipeline that identifies subgoals from changes in objectives or interaction targets, anchors navigation goals to visually recognizable waypoints, and composes adjacent subgoals into task instructions. The resulting demonstrations cover navigation, reconnaissance, combat, interaction, and recovery, with explicit stage boundaries that connect task progression to fine-grained control. We assess annotation quality through model-based verification and human inspection. An audit of 500 trajectories yields a 95.8% pass rate across independent reviewer assessments. AceAction provides a data foundation for studying how agents execute, combine, and switch skills to follow complex instructions in dynamic game environments.|[2611.04241](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04241)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Treating the Symptom, Missing the Cause: Anatomy of a Scientific Reasoning Failure**|Anna Gogleva et.al.|Scientific agents can find interventions that work without understanding why they do. We study this failure mode in controlled synthetic regulatory systems, where agents must experimentally identify a hidden fault and rescue the resulting phenotype. Across seven LLMs, every model restores the healthy phenotypes more often than it identifies the fault. Claude Opus 4.6 and Sonnet 4.6 rescue every organism while diagnosing only 55% and 35% of them. This failure is not simply due to insufficient exploration or unavailable evidence. In most cases, the cause is experimentally identifiable, and agents encounter evidence that distinguishes competing causes in 91% of relevant sessions, and still frequently misdiagnose the system. Targeted probes reveal where experimental reasoning breaks down. Models reliably interpret decisive evidence and revise hypotheses when the causal comparison is explicit, and can locate that evidence within a full experimental record. However, they struggle to predict the outcome of the experiment under each competing cause, a necessary step in using the observed result to distinguish between them. Agents can therefore gather the right evidence and still infer the wrong cause. As AI systems take on a growing role in synthesising scientific evidence, these findings highlight the need to evaluate how they use evidence to reach causal conclusions, alongside whether their interventions succeed.|[2611.04473](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04473)|
|**2026-11-09**|**A Definition of Catastrophic Performance**|Henrik Marklund et.al.|A central challenge in AI is specifying objectives that align with human preferences. While existing theoretical results elucidate how misspecification impacts performance, they typically do not distinguish between merely suboptimal and catastrophic outcomes. The first, baseline value, captures the value attained if no agent is deployed. The second, orthogonal value, captures the value of a trajectory that is unrelated to human preferences, in a sense we describe. Orthogonal value is typically far worse than baseline value. We interpret these reference values through examples in which past human activity has altered the state of the environment to human benefit. Starting at the resulting state, an AI agent is deployed with the goal of accelerating further progress. Catastrophic performance represents this agent's undoing of benefits afforded by human activity.|[2611.06219](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06219)|
|**2026-11-09**|**AdaFloor: Adaptive Floor Planning and Rank-Time Reuse for Long-Tail MoE RL Rollouts**|Jia Wei et.al.|Synchronous MoE reinforcement-learning post-training suffers from response-length imbalance. Existing methods reduce request tails, yet completed ranks remain bound to Expert Parallelism (EP) collectives and expert ownership. In a representative 16-accelerator Qwen3-30B-A3B rollout, this dummy run traps 32.83% of rank-time. AdaFloor converts this EP-coupled capacity into bounded leases for auxiliary work to improve system resource efficiency through rank-time reuse. Historical prompt-tail prediction and a calibrated guard construct the KV-safe layout. AdaFloor then selects the floor-specific safe quorum schedule with the largest predicted release area. Its runtime shrinks the EP group and leases detached ranks until full-world restoration. Across three updated-policy seeds on DeepScaleR mathematical reasoning rollouts, its capacity-oriented Natural floor2 configuration with TailGuard reduces Vanilla's 29393.9 rank-seconds of dummy run by 96.15%. Rollout shortening eliminates 18693.6 rank-seconds, while coordinated exits convert another 9568.6 rank-seconds into reusable capacity, leaving 1131.7 rank-seconds. For sidecar reuse, its Planned residency floor4 configuration without TailGuard serves 3134 Qwen3-8B GSM8K queries per epoch at 99.46% accuracy and incurs only a 2 to 3% primary throughput reduction against the same configuration with sidecar execution disabled. Its latency-oriented Planned residency floor4 configuration with TailGuard reduces rollout time by 24.09% relative to Vanilla and by 2.31% relative to LengthSort with TailGuard. On public Eurus-2-RL code rollouts with the same model and 16-rank topology, AdaFloor reduces rollout time by 20.56% and dummy run by 89.13% while generated work and mean reward remain stable.|[2611.06984](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06984)|
|**2026-11-09**|**BabyTheorist: A Benchmark for Learning to Theorize the World from Observation Alone**|Doojin Baek et.al.|How would we know whether a machine truly understands the world? We argue that one key capability is observational theory learning: acquiring reusable theories from pure observations and applying them to new situations. Current visual reasoning benchmarks focus primarily on the latter, presenting each task as a few-shot puzzle grouped by a common rule. We introduce BabyTheorist, a benchmark for controlled training and evaluation of observational theory learning. BabyTheorist generates continual streams of before-after visual observations from hidden compositional programs; during training, learners see only the resulting transitions, with no program labels, task identities, or support sets. To support theory learning and evaluation, BabyTheorist controls the program language, a curriculum over program complexity, recurring frequent subprograms, and a continual train-test protocol with level skipping. Seven representative baselines plateau well below ceiling, with the gap widening as the curriculum advances---evidence that BabyTheorist isolates a capability current program-learning approaches do not yet address.|[2611.08177](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08177)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Stay on the Manifold: Hierarchical Bayesian Trajectory-Family Transfer for Sparse-Target Reconstruction and Sampling**|Elvis Han Cui et.al.|Where should reconstructions and new samples lie when a few observations reveal only fragments of a trajectory? We propose hierarchical Bayesian trajectory principal-manifold sampling (HB-TPM), which learns a family of smooth curves from ordered source tasks and adapts it to sparse targets. For unordered vectors, alternating progression assignments and coefficient updates estimate trajectory structure between and beyond the observed shots under the learned source family. Observation-aware extensions connect the trajectory prior to photon arrivals and image-encoded cardiac cycles. Our analysis characterizes the coefficient directions supplied by the prior and bounds sampling error conditional on curve recovery. Across 30 held-out vector tasks, HB-TPM reduces mean sliced-Wasserstein distance by 21.0-41.8% relative to a source-adapted VAE. Prior-strength and sampling ablations identify coefficient shrinkage as the main supported mechanism. On 100 simulated photon records, adaptation lowers mean relative trajectory RMSE by 50.2% versus target-only fitting and 23.6% versus source-mean prediction. Across three training seeds and five folds on 98 cardiac sequences, HB-TPM with latent-MSE training achieves the best mean reconstruction among the tested configurations. Together, the results demonstrate trajectory-family transfer for sparse-target reconstruction and trajectory-centered sampling when source and target tasks share comparable progression structure.|[2310.07801](http://arxiv.org/abs/2310.07801)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Differentiable Nonlinear Model Predictive Control**|Jonathan Frey et.al.|The efficient computation of parametric solution sensitivities is a key challenge in the integration of learning-enhanced methods with nonlinear model predictive control, as their availability is crucial for many learning algorithms. This paper discusses the computation of solution sensitivities of general nonlinear programs using the implicit function theorem and smoothed optimality conditions treated in interior-point methods (IPM). We introduce sequential barrier quadratic programming (SBQP), which generalizes SQP with an IPM quadratic programming (QP) solver by allowing the barrier parameter to approach a prescribed nonnegative target value, recovering classical SQP when this target is zero. Previous works are limited to convex or unconstrained formulations, or lack an implementation for efficient sensitivity evaluation. The publication is accompanied by an efficient open-source implementation within the `acados` framework, providing both forward and adjoint sensitivities for general optimal control problems, achieving speedups exceeding 3x over the state-of-the-art solvers `mpc.pytorch` and `cvxpygen`.|[2505.01353](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.01353)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Equivariant Spherical Transformer for Efficient Molecular Modeling**|Junyi An et.al.|Equivariant Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) have significantly advanced the modeling of 3D molecular structure by leveraging group representations. However, message passing built only from exactly equivariant operations can have restricted practical expressiveness at a fixed representation degree. We introduce the Equivariant Spherical Transformer (EST), a plug-and-play module that applies Transformer attention to spherical spatial representations obtained from group representations. EST explicitly mixes information across sampled orientations, unlike prior orientation-wise spherical operations, while retaining a strong approximate equivariant inductive bias through near-uniform spherical sampling. As demonstrated by our experiments on challenging benchmarks like OC20, MPtrj and QM9, EST-based models achieve state-of-the-art performance. For the complex molecular systems within OC20, small models empowered by EST can outperform some larger models and those using additional data. We characterize the ideal closed-domain condition under which the continuous operation is exactly equivariant and empirically quantify the finite-sampling accuracy--equivariance--memory trade-off used in practice.|[2505.23086](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23086)|
|**2026-11-09**|**From Images to Signals: Are Large Vision Models Useful for Time Series Analysis?**|Ziming Zhao et.al.|Transformer-based models have gained increasing attention in time series research, driving interest in Large Language Models (LLMs) and foundation models for time series analysis. As the field moves toward multi-modality, Large Vision Models (LVMs) are emerging as a promising direction. In the past, the effectiveness of Transformer and LLMs in time series has been debated. When it comes to LVMs, a similar question arises: are LVMs truely useful for time series analysis? To address it, we design and conduct the first principled study involving 4 LVMs, 8 imaging methods, 18 datasets and 26 baselines across both high-level (classification) and low-level (forecasting) tasks, with extensive ablation analysis. Our findings indicate LVMs are indeed useful for time series classification but face challenges in forecasting. Although effective, the contemporary best LVM forecasters are limited to specific types of LVMs and imaging methods, exhibit a bias toward forecasting periods, and have limited ability to utilize long look-back windows. We hope our findings could serve as a cornerstone for future research on LVM- and multimodal-based solutions to different time series tasks.|[2505.24030](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24030)|
|**2026-11-09**|**BNPO: Beta Normalization Policy Optimization**|Changyi Xiao et.al.|Reinforcement learning with rule-based, binary-valued reward functions has been shown to significantly enhance the reasoning capabilities of large language models. These models typically rely on REINFORCE-based policy optimization methods, such as REINFORCE with baseline and group relative policy optimization (GRPO). However, a key limitation is that existing methods either ignore reward normalization or use static normalization schemes, which fail to adapt to the evolving policy during training. This mismatch can lead to unstable gradient estimates and hinder training stability. To address this issue, we propose Beta Normalization Policy Optimization (BNPO), a novel method that adaptively normalizes rewards using a Beta distribution with dynamically updated parameters. By aligning normalization with the changing policy distribution, BNPO enables more accurate, lower-variance gradient estimation and promotes stable training dynamics. We provide theoretical analysis showing that BNPO reduces variance and generalizes both REINFORCE and GRPO under binary-valued rewards. In addition, we introduce an advantage decomposition mechanism to extend BNPO to more complex reward settings. Experimental results demonstrate that BNPO achieves state-of-the-art performance among policy optimization methods on reasoning tasks.|[2506.02864](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.02864)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Subjective Experience in AI Systems: What Do AI Researchers and the Public Believe?**|Noemi Dreksler et.al.|Whether AI systems are perceived to have subjective experience has scientific, ethical, and governance implications. Yet little is known about how AI researchers and the public view this possibility. We surveyed 582 AI researchers who published in leading AI venues and 825 nationally representative US adults about the likelihood, identification, and governance of AI systems with subjective experience. We find that both AI researchers and the public think it is unlikely that AI systems have subjective experience now, but believe it is more likely than not they will by century's end. Both groups believe multidisciplinary expertise will be important in identifying AI subjective experience, support developers implementing safeguards now, and agree that, if created, subjectively experiencing AI systems should have moral responsibilities and be held accountable. Both groups also show substantial disagreement on many moral and policy questions. Our results underline the importance of building more detailed, evidence-based understanding of public and expert opinion on this contentious issue.|[2506.11945](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.11945)|
|**2026-11-09**|**AblationBench: Evaluating Automated Planning of Ablations in Empirical AI Research**|Talor Abramovich et.al.|Language model agents are increasingly used to automate scientific research, yet evaluating their scientific contributions remains a challenge. A key mechanism to obtain such insights is through ablation experiments. To this end, we introduce AblationBench, a benchmark suite for evaluating agents on ablation planning tasks in empirical AI research. It includes two tasks: AuthorAblation, which helps authors propose ablation experiments based on a method section and contains 83 instances, and ReviewerAblation, which helps reviewers find missing ablations in a full paper and contains 350 instances. For both tasks, we develop LM-based judges that serve as an automatic evaluation framework. Our experiments with frontier LMs show that these tasks remain challenging, with the best-performing LM system identifying only 45% of the original ablations on average, below human-level performance. We observe an inverse performance trend between the author and reviewer tasks, which we attribute to differences in model grounding. Lastly, we analyze the limitations of current LMs on these tasks, and find that chain-of-thought prompting outperforms an agent-based approach. Our data is available on https://huggingface.co/collections/ai-coscientist/ablationbench, and our code is available on https://github.com/ai-scientist-bench/ablation-bench .|[2507.08038](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.08038)|
|**2026-11-09**|**End-to-End On-Device Quantization-Aware Training for LLMs at Inference Cost**|Qitao Tan et.al.|Quantization reduces the deployment cost of large language models (LLMs). However, post-training quantization (PTQ) cannot fine-tune model parameters and often degrades accuracy in low-bit settings, while quantization-aware training (QAT) requires backpropagation and incurs high memory overhead. We propose ZeroQAT, a zeroth-order QAT framework supporting both weight and activation quantization. We further introduce a lightweight variant of ZeroQAT for quantized fine-tuning, which freezes and pre-quantizes most parameters to further cut memory usage. For example, ZeroQAT enables fine-tuning of a 13B model at extremely low bit-widths (e.g., 2-4 bits) on a single 8GB GPU, and even allows fine-tuning a 6.7B model on a OnePlus 12 smartphone, demonstrating its practicality for end-to-end QAT on resource-limited edge devices.|[2509.00031](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.00031)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Align Your Query: Representation Alignment for Multimodality Medical Object Detection**|Ara Seo et.al.|Medical object detection suffers when a single detector is trained on mixed medical modalities (e.g., CXR, CT, MRI) due to heterogeneous statistics and disjoint representation spaces.
