We find that standard deep learning can violate this intent: each generated trajectory can seem plausible on its own, but the aggregate distribution over the physical quantity is wrong. We call this failure physical misgeneralization. In this work, we identify and formalize the problem, develop and validate an account of its mechanism, and use that account to compare and design mitigations. Using controlled synthetic tasks, we show that physical misgeneralization arises when local errors typical of the model class propagate through the physical measurement to shift the recovered distribution. We estimate these errors with a data deviation kernel, and we use it to predict which physical quantity values gain or lose mass under real diffusion sequence models trained on both toy and applied trajectory generation tasks. Finally, our mechanistic interpretation helps identify which mitigations are structurally promising. We first compare interventions that modify the dataset and generative interface, then show that the mechanistic understanding we develop throughout this paper suggests a novel way to construct data representations informed by the kernel. We show that our proposed mitigation, which transforms the coordinate system under which the generator learns and samples, effectively reduces quantity drift.|[2605.20299](http://arxiv.org/abs/2605.20299)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Benchmarking Patent Embeddings: A Multi-Task Evaluation of 22 Models Across Retrieval, Classification, and Clustering**|Amirhossein Yousefiramandi et.al.|Two questions regarding practitioners' use of patent embeddings arise: (i) Does one fine-tuning recipe suffice for all downstream applications? (i) The optimal fine-tuning recipe depends on the downstream task: cross-sectional alignment (recipe R3) provides the largest improvements to retrieval performance (+7.1% nDCG@10), whereas a combined signal recipe (recipe R4) is better suited to classification (+7.1 F1) and clustering (+10.9 V-measure); a matched data control confirms that differences in training dataset size are not a contributing factor. (ii) Single-landscape fine-tuning hampers cross-landscape information retrieval: fine-tuning on one landscape significantly degrades cross-domain retrieval for 5 of 8 model-recipe combinations on the DAPFAM corpus, with the stronger zero-shot models suffering most. While within-family scaling is consistent (Qwen3 0.6B->4B->8B; Llama-Nemotron 1B->8B), cross-family scaling is erratic; the 12B KaLM-Gemma3 is ranked 8th on TAC retrieval performance, following prefix modification. Title+Abstract+Claims is the ubiquitous best text view, and all models suffer from a 55-65% gap between IN and OUT-of-domain performance which cannot be mitigated by hybrid BM25-dense fusion. Code and evaluation framework are publicly available.|[2605.24297](http://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24297)|
|**2026-11-09**|**LAPLEX: The FFT of Learnable Laplace Kernels**|{\L}ukasz Struski et.al.|Fast matrix--vector multiplication in deep learning usually comes with a choice: fixed geometry and exact computation, as in the fast Fourier transform (FFT), or adaptive geometry paid for by dense parameters, random features, or low-rank surrogates. A LAPLEX layer is a typically full-rank dense matrix, implicitly defined by learnable coordinate anchors, with FFT-like scaling. Consequently, it supports trainable matrix-vector operations at vector dimensions up to $10^9$ on modern GPUs. As a neural layer, it yields compact projections and classification heads interpretable as soft, trainable routing models. The same primitive also serves as an efficient Gram operator, enabling high-dimensional covariance models on flattened images of dimension $3 \cdot 10^6$ that preserve visible spatial structure without imposing convolutional bias. |**2026-11-09**|**Evidence-Linked Radiology Reporting: A Human-Supervised Reference Architecture for Structured Imaging Intelligence**|Houman Kazemzadeh et.al.|Radiology reports remain the primary mechanism by which imaging findings are communicated to clinical teams. However, much of the structured information behind these reports, including measurements, image evidence, prior comparisons, lesion identity, uncertainty, and terminology, often remains trapped in free text or fragmented across picture archiving and communication systems, radiology information systems, reporting workstations, worksheets, advanced visualization tools, and electronic health records. This paper proposes a human-supervised, evidence-linked reference architecture for structured radiology reporting. The framework combines exam-specific templates, speech-to-structure processing, measurement and segmentation capture, controlled AI-assisted drafting, and standards-based interoperability using DICOM, DICOM Structured Reporting, DICOM Segmentation, HL7 FHIR, RadLex, SNOMED CT, LOINC, and UCUM. |**2026-11-09**|**Jailbreak susceptibility prediction and mitigation via the behavioral geometry of models**|Hayden Helm et.al.|Evaluating and mitigating a generative system's susceptibility to jailbreak attacks is critical to its safe deployment. Given the number of deployable systems, full per-configuration evaluation and optimization is impractical. In this paper, we formalize the behavioral geometry of a population of models that, by leveraging previously evaluated and defended models, supports both efficient susceptibility prediction and effective defense transfer across a population. We apply the framework to 82 models spanning 24 providers and to 100 system configurations of a single base model. Simple methods that use the behavioral geometry reach an AUPRC of $0.94$ for susceptibility detection with $\approx98\%$ fewer probes relative to a full evaluation. Using the behavioral geometry to select which model to transfer an optimized defense from outperforms same-provider assignment ($+4.1\%$, $p = 0.004$) at no additional probe cost, with a set of three models sufficient to cover the population. |**2026-11-09**|**Measuring Form and Function in Language Models**|H\'ector Javier V\'azquez Mart\'inez et.al.|We introduce quantitative metrics for child language acquisition to evaluate language models. Our focus is on the formal syntactic and functional discourse properties of determiners in English, which young children acquire early and accurately. We propose Contextual Alternative Choice (CAC), a new prompting method which provides targeted tests for both syntactic and discourse knowledge of language. The method enables direct comparison of language models against children, and more importantly, against statistical benchmarks independently established in empirical research. No current model trained on a comparable amount of data simultaneously meet both formal and functional benchmarks like human children, but some very large models do. We present our results as methodological and technical contributions, with specific emphasis on cognitive status of language models.|[2605.28616](http://arxiv.org/abs/2605.28616)|
|**2026-11-09**|**SCDBench: A Benchmark for LLM-Based Smart Contract Decompilers**|Kaihua Qin et.al.|Smart contract decompilation aims to recover high-level source code from bytecode, but evaluating decompilers remains difficult because existing studies use narrow datasets, inconsistent metrics, and limited semantic consistency checks. This gap is increasingly important as large language models (LLMs) begin to generate source-like Solidity that may compile and appear plausible, even when its semantics diverge from the original contract. We introduce SCDBench, a dataset and benchmark methodology for LLM-based smart contract decompilation. The dataset contains 600 real-world Solidity contracts with paired bytecode inputs, ground-truth source code, and replayable semantic checkpoints. SCDBench evaluates decompiler outputs through four cumulative stages: format completeness, compilability, Application Binary Interface (ABI) recovery, and semantic consistency via differential replay. We evaluate Claude Opus 4.7, GPT-5.3-Codex, and GLM-5 in a zero-shot decompilation setting, including GLM-5 variants with and without extended reasoning and a zero-shot compilation-repair setting. The results show that frontier LLMs can often produce structured and compilable Solidity, but achieving semantic consistency remains far from solved: the best-performing frontier model perfectly decompiles only 42/600 contracts. We further show that introducing same-model compilation repair substantially improves performance at modest additional cost. |**2026-11-09**|**Fine-Tuned Language Models Exhibit Improved Information Conveyance**|Yuwei Cheng et.al.|Fine-tuning is often believed to reduce uncertainty and diversity in large language models, but existing analyses overlook output length, a key confounder, and therefore fail to capture how uncertainty is distributed across an entire generation rollout. To address this, we propose Canopy Entropy ($\mathrm{CE}^\star$), a measure that views language generation from a tree perspective, where "canopy" represents the space of all possible rollouts, making $\mathrm{CE}^\star$ naturally quantify the effective size of the generation space. $\mathrm{CE}^\star$ jointly captures uncertainty in both the output length $N$ and the generated sequence $Y_{1:N}$ -- indeed, we show that it equals total Shannon entropy $H(N, Y_{1:N}\mid X)$, where $X$ denotes the prompt. This formulation yields interpretable metrics, including a length-entropy correlation term $\rho(N, r_N)$, where $r_N$ is the entropy rate, quantifying information conveyance efficiency by indicating whether longer outputs are more or less informative per token. Empirically, across tasks and model families, we find that fine-tuned models predominantly exhibit more positive correlation $\rho(N, r_N)$, even when total entropy decreases. Furthermore, after controlling for model family, task, prompt, and output-length effects, we find that fine-tuning nearly triples the correlation strength between entropy rate and semantic diversity, suggesting that aligned models convert token uncertainty into semantic diversity more efficiently. Overall, these results demonstrate that fine-tuning does not simply reduce uncertainty, but fundamentally reorganizes it into more informative and semantically meaningful generations. Our code is available at https://github.com/WeiyiTian/canopy-entropy.|[2605.30844](http://arxiv.org/abs/2605.30844)|
|**2026-11-09**|**STEP: Learning STructured Embeddings for Progressive Time Series**|Lucas Thil et.al.|We present a novel method for learning interpretable representations of progressive time series, that is, data capturing irreversible state transitions such as degradation or task completion. Our approach uses a self-supervised contrastive objective to learn a low-dimensional latent space whose geometry is itself the interpretation: each observation becomes a point on a manifold anchored between two fixed orthogonal prototype vectors, and a trajectory becomes a path across that manifold. From this structure we read a latent compass, the polar coordinates ({\theta}, r) of the latent vector, in which {\theta} tracks the progression of the underlying state (e.g., from healthy to failed) and r identifies the active mode (e.g., the operating condition), without any proxy labels. We evaluate the approach against the state of the art on diverse domains, including industrial degradation, robotic tasks, and neural activity, validating three key capabilities: (1) end-state prediction, (2) multi-step forecasting, and (3) interpretable phase separation. A simple linear regressor on top of the latent compass coordinates is competitive with deep architectures, direct quantitative evidence that the underlying state is encoded in a geometrically accessible form.|[2605.31061](http://arxiv.org/abs/2605.31061)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Subspace-Aware Sparse Autoencoders for Effective Mechanistic Interpretability**|Seyed Arshan Dalili et.al.|Sparse Autoencoders (SAEs) are widely used for mechanistic interpretability in large language models, yet their standard formulation assigns each latent feature a single decoder direction, implicitly assuming features to be one-dimensional. We show that this assumption can induce feature splitting when model features are multi-dimensional. Geometrically, accurately reconstructing a feature under a fixed co-activation budget can require exponentially many decoder directions as its dimension grows. From an optimization perspective, adding decoder directions can strictly lower the $\ell_1$-regularized population risk, even when a basis can already reconstruct the feature exactly. Motivated by these results, we introduce Subspace-Aware Sparse Autoencoders (SASA), which replace single-vector decoders with learned subspaces, enforce sparsity through Top-$s$ group gating, and adapt group rank with nuclear-norm regularization. Under an idealized single-feature model, we prove that the SASA objective is minimized by a single group whose reconstruction map recovers the feature subspace. We also derive polynomial sample complexity bounds for subspace estimation within a group and a lower bound on the samples needed to observe every atom in a fixed fragmented dictionary. Experiments on GPT-2 and Mistral-7B show reduced absorption compared with SAEs. Matched-budget GPT-2 comparisons show lower absorption for concept families and more accurate prediction of their members using a small number of latent coordinates, at a reconstruction cost. The code is available at https://github.com/arshandalili/sasa.|[2606.06333](http://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06333)|
|**2026-11-09**|**RiskFlow: Fast and Faithful Safety-Critical Traffic Scenario Generation**|Jie Li et.al.|Safety-critical traffic scenario generation is essential for evaluating autonomous driving systems under rare but high-risk interactions. Existing diffusion-based methods offer strong controllability in closed-loop generation, but their iterative denoising process is computationally expensive and may accumulate sampling and guidance errors over long rollouts, causing unrealistic motion artifacts such as abnormal acceleration and off-road behavior. To address these issues, we propose RiskFlow, a closed-loop safety-critical traffic generation framework that reformulates future behavior generation as one-step flow transport in the action space during training. At test time, RiskFlow generates safety-critical interactions through a selective guidance procedure in which time-to-collision-based critical-agent ranking selects high-risk agents and an adversarial interaction objective directly refines their output actions. Vehicle-dynamics rollout and map-aware regularization then translate guided actions into feasible trajectories while discouraging off-road behavior. By intervening on selected agents in the output space, RiskFlow avoids repeated corrections of intermediate denoising states, thereby mitigating accumulated guidance errors and accelerating closed-loop scenario generation. Experiments on the nuScenes dataset under closed-loop evaluation show that RiskFlow achieves a strong adversariality-realism trade-off across multi-agent and long-horizon settings. |**2026-11-09**|**Characterize Then Distill: Mechanistic Reasoning in Large Output Spaces**|Debjyoti Saha Roy et.al.|Reasoning-trained language models can select, zero-shot, a small set of relevant labels from thousands to hundreds of thousands of candidates. Attribution, exact mean-ablation, knock-in and a null calibration give individual attention heads causal standing. On clinical coding of MIMIC-IV discharge summaries, with all 5,651 candidate codes in context, a small, global, phase-structured set of heads is necessary and sufficient on essentially every summary; distinct head families read the candidate region and the earlier near-miss, and blocking what they read removes their effect; for mentions elicitable in the reasoning, a disjoint mid-layer set makes the region elicitable several tokens before the code. The structure recurs in a second reasoner. We introduce MISTILL, which, unlike chain-of-thought distillation, also supervises the student's pooled attention at these decision events. Read on heads found after training, it nearly doubles the causal recovery of the contrastive decision in a cross-family student and adds a small, seed-stable gain in one that already carries most of it, with no detected task difference at matched precision; under matched selection it exceeds a frozen random-head pool on both recovery and task score. On two corpora with human-annotated evidence (one read zero-shot, one in domain), a MISTILL student's decision heads attend to each code's evidence more, and more specifically, than a chain-of-thought-distilled student's. A pre-registered evidence-flip test detected no improvement in evidence-dependent decisions through the tested routes.|[2606.06840](http://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06840)|
|**2026-11-09**|**The Standard Interpretable Model: Design interpretable machine learning methods from elementary symmetries**|Pietro Barbiero et.al.|As Artificial Intelligence models grow in complexity, interpretability has become an indispensable tool for understanding, debugging, and controlling their computations. To fill this gap, we introduce the Standard Interpretable Model (SIM), a general theory that enables the deductive design of interpretable methods from first principles. Specifically, the SIM summarises, in a set of premises, what interpretability is for a target entity. From these premises, the SIM systematically derives interpretability symmetries and corresponding constraints. One can then build interpretable models by either introducing constraints in the optimisation problem or by compiling constraints into an interpretable architecture. The most important finding, both theoretical and empirical, is that full expressivity and interpretability symmetries can coexist in the same model, showing that the trade-off between accuracy and interpretability is an artefact of model design. We demonstrate this on language models (LMs), where SIM-based LMs match the performance of equivalent opaque LMs without making any compromise on interpretability. Beyond this, the SIM shows how to improve the interpretability of existing pre-trained models, highlights underexplored research directions, and unifies a discipline that has long been fragmented, offering pedagogical grounding for interpretability curricula.|[2606.12289](http://arxiv.org/abs/2606.12289)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Real-Time Execution with Autoregressive Policies**|Sangkyu Lee et.al.|Real-time execution, enabled by asynchronous inference that ensures both smooth action trajectories and fast reactivity, is critical for realistic deployments of large-scale Vision-Language-Action models. In contrast, we demonstrate that autoregressive policies can achieve real-time execution by adjusting the tokenization horizon and applying constrained decoding, thereby guaranteeing strict latency bounds that enable multi-trajectory decoding to maximize performance. Coupled with the inherent advantages of autoregressive policies, such as faster convergence and better generalizability in instruction-following, these results confirm that autoregressive policies can remain a competitive policy type supporting real-time execution.|[2606.13355](http://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13355)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Embedded Arena: Iterative Optimization via Hardware Feedback**|Zhihan Zhang et.al.|As AI moves into the physical world, from robots to environmental sensors to medical wearables, it increasingly relies on local inference to meet latency, communication, or privacy constraints. Optimizing models for heterogeneous edge hardware such as microcontrollers (MCUs) requires simultaneously satisfying hard physical constraints on memory, power, and temperature while preserving accuracy, a multidimensional optimization that is today performed manually by experts. We ask whether an LLM agent can autonomously navigate this complex, multi-turn pipeline guided by real hardware feedback, and introduce a hardware-in-the-loop agent arena in which the agent iteratively refines both model and firmware by compiling, flashing, and measuring on real hardware, enabling closed-loop optimization. Frontier models, including Claude Opus 4.7 and Gemini 3.1 Pro, fail entirely without hardware feedback (0% deployment success), whereas our hardware-in-the-loop formulation achieves the first successful deployment within three iterations and can surpass human expert results within seven. This agentic co-optimization achieves 250x compression for vision models with <3.3% accuracy loss and 400x for audio with <6% Feature Error Rate loss, enabling battery-free operation on a commercial MCU via solar harvesting. We demonstrate practical impact in two real-world systems: an elk-detection camera trap (96.7% accuracy) and a phonetic-transcription wearable (8.37% FER) for child development research.|[2606.16190](http://arxiv.org/abs/2606.16190)|
|**2026-11-09**|**SDS-LoRA: Overcoming Anisotropic Gradient Scaling in Low-Rank Adaptation**|Junghun Oh et.al.|Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) enables efficient adaptation of large pretrained models to downstream tasks by parameterizing weight updates with low-rank matrices. In this paper, we investigate the limitations of the LoRA parameterization from a geometric perspective. Specifically, we show that when a full fine-tuning gradient is backpropagated to the low-rank matrices, it undergoes anisotropic scaling driven by their singular values. We argue that this phenomenon is undesirable because it distorts the full fine-tuning gradient by skewing it toward dominant singular directions while suppressing others. Our analyses demonstrate that anisotropic gradient scaling reduces the effective rank of the low-rank matrices' gradients and fails to provide the best possible alignment between the full fine-tuning gradient and its low-rank approximation in LoRA for arbitrary gradients, thereby exacerbating the gap to full fine-tuning. To address these limitations, we propose a new low-rank parameterization, SDS-LoRA, which Structurally Decouples Singular values from the backward pass. Our method ensures that the full fine-tuning gradient backpropagates only through the orthonormal bases of the low-rank matrices' subspaces, independent of their scales. The code is available at https://github.com/JungHunOh/SDS-LoRA_NeurIPS2026.|[2606.16454](http://arxiv.org/abs/2606.16454)|
|**2026-11-09**|**AI out-persuades expert humans in text-based dialogue**|Kobi Hackenburg et.al.|Many societal decisions are settled by contests of persuasion. Conversational AI is a powerful new entrant in these contests, but whether it can out-persuade skilled and highly incentivized humans has remained unclear. Here, in a series of four preregistered experiments (n = 18,978 conversations from 6,923 people), we pitted AI systems against a range of human persuaders, including laypeople, winners of a separately preregistered four-round online persuasion tournament, professional canvassers, and world championship debaters. We found that AI systems were reliably more persuasive than expert humans, even when expert humans chose their issues, researched in advance, underwent hours of live, structured practice, and were incentivized with {\pounds}1,000 cash bonuses. In a follow-up study, AI's advantage persisted after experts received a coaching tool that let them practice against the AI that beat them, review their performance history, and see what AI would have said at key moments. We found converging evidence that AI's advantage stemmed from rapidly deploying larger quantities of information: after coaching, expert humans could tie an AI constrained to respond at human speeds and with human-length messages. In a final study, we show that AI's advantage extends to consequential real-world behavior: AI was more than 3x more effective than professional canvassers from a UK fundraising firm at raising real-money donations to Save the Children. Together, these results establish that frontier AI systems out-persuade expert humans in text-based conversation, with significant implications for political communication.|[2606.16475](http://arxiv.org/abs/2606.16475)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Exposing the Illusion of Erasure in Knowledge Editing for LLMs**|Advik Raj Basani et.al.|Knowledge Editing (KE) has emerged as a frontier for updating specific facts in LLMs without costly retraining, but its reliability and underlying mechanisms remain poorly understood. To explain this behavior, we conduct a mechanistic analysis of popular KE methods. We show that low-rank updates do not overwrite existing knowledge but instead redistribute it within the model's representation space. Furthermore, we find that these methods act as targeted suppression mechanisms that reduce the likelihood of expressing original facts, rather than removing them from the model. Analysis of the loss landscape reveals that edited knowledge lies in narrow, anisotropic regions that are highly sensitive to perturbations, making them highly vulnerable to indirect prompting and adversarial attacks. By exposing these profound architectural vulnerabilities, our work proves that KE algorithms are inherently bypassable and motivates a fundamental reevaluation of how we deploy post-hoc updates in several LLM applications.|[2606.23276](http://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23276)|
|**2026-11-09**|**NoContactNoWorries: Estimating Contact through Vision and Proprioception for In-Hand Dexterous Manipulation**|Soham Patil et.al.|Perceiving physical contact is fundamental to dexterous manipulation. While robots often rely on dedicated hardware tactile sensors, humans exhibit a remarkable ability to infer contact by integrating visual information with an innate sense of their body's pose and movement. Inspired by this embodied perceptual skill, we investigate whether a robot can learn to infer contact from vision, an approach that also offers a scalable alternative to tactile hardware specifically for binary contact estimation, which faces practical challenges in cost, fragility, and integration. We present NoContactNoWorries, a transformer-based multimodal framework that fuses RGB-D vision with the robot's proprioception to infer binary contact states as a pseudo-tactile signal for hand-object interactions. We validate by training a single contact prediction model on multiple objects and show that the inferred contact signal supports downstream reinforcement learning agents for in-hand object reorientation, generalizing to novel objects. Project Page: https://soham2560.github.io/no-contact-no-worries/|[2606.24450](http://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24450)|
|**2026-11-09**|**OperatorSHAP: Fast and Accurate Shapley Value Estimation for Neural Operators**|Joshua Stiller et.al.|Understanding model predictions is essential for physical applications, where outputs often inform safety-critical decisions, such as structural load assessment, weather warnings, and clinical diagnosis. Shapley values satisfy many desirable properties as an attribution method, but their computational cost during inference hinders their practical use. |**2026-11-09**|**MCP Server Architecture Patterns for LLM-Integrated Applications**|Carson Rodrigues et.al.|The Model Context Protocol (MCP), introduced by Anthropic in November 2024, defines a standardized interface for connecting large language models (LLMs) to external tools, data sources, and services. Within months of release, hundreds of community-built MCP servers appeared on GitHub, but no software-maintenance literature has yet described how the ecosystem is being structured in production. This industry experience paper catalogues five recurring MCP server architectural patterns observed across an enumerated corpus of fifteen independently developed servers (five production servers from the ANSYR voice AI platform plus ten public servers from the official MCP registry): Resource Gateway, Tool Orchestrator, Stateful Session Server, Proxy Aggregator, and Domain-Specific Adapter. Each pattern is described in the structured form established by Gamma et al.: context, problem, solution, and consequences. We also document four anti-patterns and a set of cross-cutting concerns around authentication, versioning, and observability. Quantitative evaluation contributes two measurements: inter-rater reliability of the taxonomy across two independent LLM raters on 54 held-out servers (Cohen's kappa = 0.76), which also localizes three pattern-boundary ambiguities; transport overhead measured end-to-end on loopback (stdio: 0.01 ms p50; streamable-http: 0.39 ms p50) and modeled for cross-host paths from same-region network baselines (~30 ms p50 baseline plus protocol overhead). Version 2 withdraws the tool-count analysis of version 1, whose numbers could not be traced to production records. Code, corpus, and prompts are released at https://github.com/rodriguescarson/mcp-patterns-icsme2026.|[2606.30317](http://arxiv.org/abs/2606.30317)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Steering Training Trajectories in Diffusion Representation Learning**|Rajat Rasal et.al.|Across training runs of a diffusion autoencoder, we observe similar image reconstruction quality, but substantially different levels of disentanglement in the learned representations. These regimes differ in how reconstruction and disentanglement evolve, despite similar reconstruction at the end of training. In the reconstruction regime, models improve image fidelity before disentanglement, while in the disentanglement regime, models improve both reconstruction and disentanglement more gradually. We hypothesise that modulating shortcut pathways in the diffusion U-Net and controlling early noise-level exposure can shape trajectories. To that end, we introduce SteeringDRL, combining gated residual U-Nets with a noise curriculum to steer models towards better disentanglement. Across attribute disentanglement benchmarks, SteeringDRL has competitive disentanglement, with reduced seed sensitivity in many settings. Our method extends to spatial disentanglement with object-centric learning, improving unsupervised segmentation on synthetic and real-world datasets.|[2607.05319](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05319)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Decomposing Yes-No Bias in LLM Moral Judgment: Stance, Position, Word, and Verdict**|Haonan Huang et.al.|Large language models (LLMs) increasingly issue binary verdicts - safety gates, judge decisions, value probes - and the answer is read as the model's judgment. The largest apparent yes-no biases are almost purely surface (word and position), while several of the newest models show the reverse: near-zero flip contrasts concealing verdict attachment up to +0.42, a component no surface counterbalance can expose, replicated across label alphabets. |**2026-11-09**|**Beyond Bayesian Nash: Learning Minimax-Regret Equilibria for Adversarial Team Games under Asymmetric Information**|Naman Aggarwal et.al.|Adversarial team games (ATGs) with asymmetric information, such as adversarial path-finding, goal search, and reachability games on graphs, require strategies that are robust to hidden opponent types, such as a hidden goal flag, and to deception. We show that, for finite normal-form Bayesian games, approximate PR-MRE computation can be formulated as a robust bilinear program and derive a tractable semidefinite relaxation. We then adapt this relaxation into a novel meta-solver within a robust double-oracle framework, PRMRE-PSRO, enabling population-based learning of approximate PR-MRE strategies via deep reinforcement learning best responses. |**2026-11-09**|**PRISM: Stress-Testing Alignment-Faking Claims**|Aman Mehta et.al.|We judge whether AI systems are safe largely by watching how they behave. |**2026-11-09**|**MATE: Asynchronous Multi-Rate Diffusion Policy Composition**|Zihao He et.al.|Diffusion policies have shown strong potential for robotic imitation learning, and recent extensions incorporate additional modalities to improve manipulation performance. However, these modalities often differ not only in information content but also in sensing rates and inference latencies. |**2026-11-09**|**Curved Knowledge in Flat Models: Building Expert AI at Scale**|Kwan Soo Shin (ComplexSystems Research) et.al.|Intelligence that must act before outside correction arrives must carry the structure of its expert knowledge, and hyperbolic geometry represents hierarchy with a fidelity no Euclidean space of comparable dimension matches. Here we show that it is also evidence about arithmetic. The constraint defining the manifold is a difference of two terms that grow together; for the unclamped map at unit curvature and temperature it fails in every format we measured, double precision included, at the deep-layer norms of the language models measured here, beyond the limit previously derived. A common remedy, a norm bound, leaves the executed map no radial sensitivity where it acts. In a prespecified test, a tighter bound cost a Lorentz system more of the held out structure it started with than a flat one. Structural supervision has run in six expert systems whose retained input archives and source pools hold at least 13,787,131 records. In paired runs with a published recipe's objective held fixed, removing its starting scale remedy prevented every update and removing its single precision regions raised the radius reached early in training. Arithmetic bounds the computable region, and within it, alongside the objective, arithmetic and its remedies select which runs leave a record and the states they reach on the way, so a flat surviving checkpoint cannot by itself establish that the geometry is inert.|[2607.17513](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.17513)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Broken Gates: Re-evaluating Web Bot Defenses in the Age of LLM Agents**|Behzad Ousat et.al.|The rapid emergence of web automation across commercial crawlers, solver services, and LLM-based browser agents has created new challenges for defenses that must distinguish legitimate automated activity from scalable abuse. Controlled experiments across browser configurations show that an established consumer account and persistent Chrome state strongly affect reCaptcha v2/v3 acceptance in our deployments, whereas Cloudflare Turnstile exhibits substantially greater sensitivity to the agent's browser control path. |**2026-11-09**|**Reasoning Before Translation: Enhancing Legal Machine Translation with Structured Reasoning**|Aixiu An et.al.|Neural machine translation (NMT) in the legal domain is a linguistically and conceptually demanding task, primarily due to the complexity of legal language and the high level of precision it requires. We focus on the Swiss legal system, which -- with its unique multilingual statutes -- offers a particularly challenging testbed for reasoning-augmented models. Our results show that the quality of small ``base'' models can be greatly enhanced, and that reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards can be applied to NMT in the legal domain and surpasses the translation quality of supervised fine-tuning. The code and models are publicly available at https://github.com/aixiuxiuxiu/Legal-MT-SFT-RL.|[2607.19181](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.19181)|
|**2026-11-09**|**From Trajectories to Prefixes: Reusing Teacher Trajectories via Replayed Prefixes and Online Continuation**|Yihan Wang et.al.|Small language models are attractive backbones for interactive agents, but direct distillation from strong teacher trajectories often turns rich multi-turn behavior into one-shot imitation targets. This is inefficient in long-horizon environments, where early decisions shape later states and rewards. We propose Prefix-GRPO, a reinforcement learning framework that decomposes teacher trajectories into replay-aligned prefix queries and online continuations. Each prefix is replayed in the environment to recover a valid intermediate state, after which the student continues online interaction and receives task reward. Experiments on TextCraft, BabyAI, and ALFWorld show that Prefix-GRPO improves small-model agents over distillation and standard RL baselines, while ablations show that replay alone is insufficient without explicit prefix-token optimization. The implementation and reproduction scripts are available at https://github.com/HappynessI/Prefix_GRPO.|[2607.19395](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.19395)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Detecting Knowledge Inconsistencies Across Text, Tables, and Knowledge Graphs**|Fanfu Wei et.al.|Wikipedia and Wikidata are widely used for information access, LLM pre-training, and retrieval-augmented generation. Their knowledge is deeply connected but scattered across text, tables, and knowledge graphs. This raises a practical question: when these modalities disagree, how can we detect and explain the conflict? We study this problem as modality-level inconsistency detection. We first introduce a taxonomy of cross-modal knowledge inconsistencies, covering information granularity differences, direct conflicts, temporal changes, and KG incompleteness. We then present Kontrast, an automatic framework that uses Text-to-SPARQL and LLM reasoning to compare table-based answers with KG evidence and categorize the resulting inconsistencies. They reveal not only true knowledge conflicts, but also missing KG structure and temporal mismatches while being limited by Text-to-SPARQL errors and noise. Our analysis shows that text, tables, and KGs can complement and correct one another through systematic comparison. Kontrast provides a practical tool for large-scale knowledge auditing and establishes a benchmark for future work on cross-modal knowledge consistency. Code and data are available at https://github.com/ECLADATTA/KONTRAST.|[2607.25959](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25959)|
|**2026-11-09**|**VibeLifeBench: Can Your Life Agent Be Proactive and Persistent in a Living World?**|Xiaohongshu Dots Studio et.al.|Large language model (LLM) agents are increasingly deployed as personal assistants. Existing evaluations, however, mostly use short, self-contained requests in static environments. Everyday life assistance is different. A task runs for weeks rather than minutes. The world keeps changing while the agent is not being prompted. Many constraints are never stated outright. An agent that merely answers the request in front of it will fail at such a task. What is needed instead is an agent that stays proactive and consistent. It decides on its own when to act, when to ask, and when to stay silent. It notices changes that nobody announced. It keeps one plan coherent from the first day to the last. No current benchmark measures this. We introduce VibeLifeBench, a benchmark of 200 long-horizon tasks across ten everyday-life domains. Each task is a scripted multi-week timeline in a simulated world of 22 mock services. |**2026-11-09**|**Learning to Persuade Exposes How Easily LLMs Abandon Correct Beliefs**|Nimet Beyza Bozdag et.al.|Persuasion is a core dynamic of natural language communication, shaping how large language models (LLMs) update beliefs, resolve disagreements, and reach decisions. Second, we find that these learned strategies generalize across four out-of-distribution benchmarks, maintaining 89-95% persuasion success against the training-time persuadee. |**2026-11-09**|**LittleLearner: Language Models Under Pedagogically Controlled Knowledge Exposure**|Fanfei Li et.al.|Modern language models are trained on heterogeneous web-scale text corpora. |**2026-11-09**|**Discretizing Continuous Time Series for Imputation with Masked Diffusion Training**|Dongbin Kim et.al.|Time series imputation is a crucial area for reliable time series analysis, yet it remains challenging due to the complex temporal dynamics and noise of real-world data. The [MASK] token is structurally orthogonal to valid observations, and because the corrupted state is the partially observed series itself, the model is trained to predict the missing values directly from the observed ones, naturally aligning both the representation and the learning objective with the imputation task. To bridge the gap between discrete masked diffusion and the continuous, ordinal nature of time series, we further introduce Stochastic Discretization, which maps continuous values to ordinal-aware tokens while preserving continuous dynamics. Our experiments on diverse benchmarks confirm that MDTIM achieves superior robustness and scalability, consistently outperforming state-of-the-art deterministic and generative baselines across various missing scenarios. Code and project page are available at https://dongbeank.github.io/MDTIM/.|[2608.19119](http://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19119)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Finding and using interpretable latents in a neutrino foundation model with sparse autoencoders**|Rapha\"el Bonnet-Guerrini et.al.|We present a first application of sparse-autoencoder-based mechanistic interpretability to particle physics. |**2026-11-09**|**Benchmark Contamination: A Taxonomy Organized by Defeated Mitigation**|Johanna Angulo et.al.|A benchmark score is a joint property of the model, the evaluation harness, the elicitation budget, the sampled population, and contamination status. The fifth is acquired during the evaluation itself; because it is a property of one run, it must be recorded with the reported score rather than with the benchmark release. Disagreement concentrates on when a variable applies rather than on what a document states. Elicitation budgets are reported in 13% of documents, and no document addresses all five types. |**2026-11-09**|**BiG-SURE - Bipartite Graph for Semantic Uncertainty and Reliability Estimation of LLMs**|Debarpan Bhattacharya et.al.|Reliable uncertainty estimation is a crucial requirement for deploying large language models (LLMs) and vision-language models (VLMs) in safety-critical settings, especially when the model parameters are not accessible (black-box). |**2026-11-09**|**Anatomy of LLM Sycophancy: What a Flip Rate Hides**|Haonan Huang et.al.|A model under pushback can correct itself, capitulate, or hold, and one flip rate counts a correction and a capitulation alike. |**2026-11-09**|**Learning to Simulate Individuals from Macro Social Signals**|Yining Zhao et.al.|Large language models are increasingly used to simulate how individuals respond to new situations, yet the behavioral reasoning behind these responses is either inherited from pretraining or learned from individual-level annotations, which offer limited behavioral diversity and little supervision of the reasoning itself. The learned reasoning applies to user simulation without further training. On SWM-Bench, macro2mind achieves state-of-the-art directional accuracy and correlation on Polymarket. |**2026-11-09**|**VOMMI: Collecting and Leveraging Portable Demonstrations for Mobile Manipulation**|Yutian Zhang et.al.|Portable mobile-manipulation demonstrations can help alleviate data scarcity for embodied intelligence, but obtaining reliable, low-cost, and robot-free motion supervision from RGB observations remains challenging. |**2026-11-09**|**MetaEncoder: Exploring the Limit of Bi-Encoders for Multimodal System One Decision Making with Natural Language Interface**|Jianpeng Cheng et.al.|System One models prioritize fast, low-latency decision-making and calibrated probability distributions over token generation. |**2026-11-09**|**Diffusion-Steering-Based MPC with a JEPA World Action Model**|Shashank Hegde et.al.|World action models (WAMs) predict actions and future observations, typically from a reconstruction-based representation that carries noisy, redundant information which can complicate downstream predictions. |**2026-11-09**|**PriorityFEC: Deadline-Aware Streaming FEC for KV-Cache Handoff in Edge Multi-Agent Systems**|Han Shen et.al.|Collaborative edge large language model (LLM) systems often require repeated prefill computation when task contexts are transferred between agents. |**2026-11-09**|**Explore Separately, Adopt Selectively: Execution-Guided Tool Use over Large Tool Libraries**|Anna Mazhar et.al.|Enterprise LLM agents using growing tool catalogs must surface capabilities as workflows evolve without flooding context or committing to the wrong tool. |**2026-11-09**|**The As-If Machine: Personalized AI counterfactuals make distant policy risks feel close**|Joshua Ashkinaze et.al.|Life experiences can shape policy attitudes, but we only live one life. |**2026-11-09**|**What Makes a Question Curious? Measuring Model Curiosity in Human-LLM Interactions**|Angana Borah et.al.|Large language models increasingly act as partners in everyday communication. |**2026-11-09**|**Making Sense of Audience Change: Causal Attribution and Boundary Management on Xiaohongshu**|Erin Zhou et.al.|Recommendation-driven platforms can reshape not only what users see, but also who becomes visible and how community boundaries are negotiated. |**2026-11-09**|**Prompt Lottery in LLM-as-a-Judge: Measuring and Mitigating Sensitivity to Syntactic Rephrasing**|Ilija Subasic et.al.|LLM-as-a-Judge has become a standard for evaluating large language models, yet most approaches implicitly assume that a single judge prompt functions as a stable evaluation instrument. |**2026-11-09**|**LLMs as Judges and Examinees for Medical Item Difficulty Prediction**|Xiaomeng Xiong et.al.|Predicting medical item difficulty from item text alone remains challenging. |**2026-11-09**|**STAR: Steering Action Representations with Proprioception for VLA Inference**|Guangyu Zhao et.al.|Vision-language-action models (VLAs) map language instructions and visual observations to robot actions, showing strong capabilities across robotic manipulation tasks. |**2026-11-09**|**When Disclosure Is Compulsory: Evaluating Ride-Hailing Rebalancing Policies with Self-Interested LLM Driver Agents**|Xinyu Wang et.al.|The EU Platform Work Directive, to be transposed by December 2026, requires ride-hailing platforms to state in plain language the main parameters of their automated decisions and how a worker's behaviour affects them. |**2026-11-09**|**Behavior Graphs: Unsupervised Graph Structures for Interpreting Robot Deployment Data**|Erik Bauer et.al.|Data flywheels promise continuous improvement via insights from deployment data. As the number of deployed robots grows, behavior graphs lend structure to high-dimensional policy rollouts and make deployment data easier to interpret and reason about.|[2611.00405](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00405)|
|**2026-11-09**|**A Spectral Renormalization-Group View of Learning**|Charles H. Martin et.al.|We study how the layers in deep neural networks (NNs) converge, using Wilsonian Renormalization-Group (RG) ideas to organize the spectral picture. During training, these layers often develop heavy-tailed power-law (PL) spectra, and the strongest checkpoints often appear near the PL exponent $\alpha\approx 2$. This pattern raises a simple question: why should this value mark a well-trained layer? The answer is both statistical and physical. Our statistical hypothesis is that $\alpha=2$ marks the boundary between stable if not optimal learning and overfitting via non-self-averaging. The physical answer is that the same value makes the spectral energy gain scale evenly across logarithmic spectral scales, which is the proposed Wilsonian marginal condition. We then apply the ERG trace-log condition, the proposed SETOL theory, which we justify by taking volume-preserving, scale-invariant transformations to remove arbitrary layer scale along the proposed RG flow. The resulting spectral RG flow connects the HTSR and SETOL theories to optimizers such as Muon, and motivates a new optimizer extension, WW-PGD. Together, these results point toward a spectral Renormalization-Group view of learning, in which NNs may approach shared spectral structure in the settings studied here.|[2611.00422](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00422)|
|**2026-11-09**|**CuriousTogether: Implications of Curiosity and Cultural Grounding in Multi-Agent LLM Interactions**|Angana Borah et.al.|Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed in multi-agent settings, exhibiting emergent collective behaviors, yet the role of curiosity as a driver of such dynamics remains understudied. We introduce CuriousTogether, a framework evaluating curiosity-driven multi-agent LLM interactions across three complementary dimensions: novelty-seeking, epistemic and social curiosity. Across eight tasks spanning reasoning, creative generation, and social bias mitigation, curiosity-guided multi-agent settings yield improvements over non-curious ones on seven out of eight tasks, with gains of up to 20.6% in story generation (semantic diversity) and a 14.9 pp reduction in stereotype rates. Furthermore, country-conditioned cultural grounding show benefits for story generation and social tasks, but can hurt precision-oriented reasoning. Finally, we show that inference-time multi-agent curiosity-based approach offers favorable quality-token trade-offs, compared with single-agent baselines. Together, these findings support curiosity-guided interaction as a practical mechanism for improving multi-agent collaboration, with downstream benefits for reasoning, creative, and societal tasks.|[2611.01301](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01301)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Iterative Gumbel Planning for Continuous Control**|Shaohuai Liu et.al.|Search-based planning remains a powerful policy improvement paradigm in reinforcement learning, driving milestone successes in discrete domains. However, extending this paradigm to continuous control exposes a structural mismatch: search evaluates a finite set of action candidates, whereas continuous RL typically relies on Gaussian policies. We show that winner-action distillation into a continuous Gaussian induces a \textit{projection error} and a stepwise variance-expanding gradient tendency when the selected action is sufficiently far from the current mean. Empirically, this tendency is associated with elevated terminal variance and a return gap on the evaluated tasks. To address this, we propose \textbf{Iterative Gumbel Planning (IGP)}. By adopting a per-dimension factorized categorical policy, IGP naturally aligns the policy support with the search candidates, eliminating the projection error and reducing the residual approximation to a bounded discretization error. To bypass the combinatorial explosion of high-dimensional discrete action spaces, IGP pairs Gumbel Top-$K$ candidate sampling with an MPPI-style multi-round iterative search to refine the policy at each state. Empirically, IGP achieves state-of-the-art aggregate performance and sample efficiency on DMControl and HumanoidBench. Furthermore, it produces more stable action sequences with lower temporal variance, demonstrating its practical potential for real-world robotic deployment.|[2611.02236](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02236)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Maswao: An automatically collected, prepared, and benchmarked dataset for South African Sign Language**|Thapelo Sindane et.al.|Recent advancements in sign language processing can be attributed to developments in both large-scale dataset collection and algorithmic complexity in areas such as pose estimation techniques, signal processing through Transformer models, and model optimization. However, only a handful of sign languages, such as the American, British, and German, from over 200 globally recognized sign languages, reap the benefits of such advancements, while the rest lag behind. In this study, we aim to address this technological disparity by proposing a framework for the automatic collection and preparation of sign language datasets, with very minimal human-labour intervention, using the South African Sign Language (SASL) as a testbed. Specifically, we automatically collect SASL video content using public resources such as news broadcasts. Additionally, we convert their accompanying audio streams into text using available Automatic Speech Recognition (ASR) tools. Furthermore, we crop the sign language area in the videos and use gradient descent to locate valleys of non-motion to determine end-of-sentence points within long videos to create our weakly sentence-aligned parallel corpora -- the Maswao (Signs) dataset. Finally, we benchmark the weakly aligned dataset on the task of Sign-to-text Translation using Recurrent Neural Networks and Transformer models to compare their individual intricacies. Our results show that Transformer models proposed in the literature outperform recurrent baselines, with the best-performing Transformer achieving BLEU-1 to BLEU-4 scores of 13.78, 5.21, 2.52, and 1.54, respectively, as well as ChrF = 18.09 and ROUGE-L = 10.51. These results indicate that the task remains promising and warrants further investigations.|[2611.02903](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02903)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Beyond Accuracy: What Language-Subspace Interventions Capture, Remove, and Improve**|Shaanil Punglia et.al.|Language-subspace interventions aim to improve multilingual reasoning by suppressing language-related components in mid-layer activations. Using MATH and MGSM across four open-weight LLMs, we ask three questions: do estimated language subspaces capture translation-associated variation? Does projecting them out remove language information? And do they improve accuracy without compromising response language? For the first question, we build subspaces using the existing Mean-SVD construction and our DiffPCA. Both capture substantial translation-associated variation and show similar patterns across model depth. For the second, we project out each subspace and train linear probes to classify language from the projected activations. The probes still identify language almost perfectly, showing that neither subspace exhaustively captures language information. For the third, we evaluate several intervention configurations on Qwen2.5-7B, by varying subspace creation methods and intervention operators. We find that the intervention that most improves MGSM accuracy sacrifices response-language fidelity, with only one intervention improving joint accuracy$\unicode{x2014}$answers that are correct and in the target language$\unicode{x2014}$slightly, by 1.60 points. |**2026-11-09**|**DARE: Difficulty Analysis for Repair Enhancement**|Jikwang Lee et.al.|LLM-based coding agents are primarily evaluated by their solve rates. However, this metric does not reveal how difficult the solved tasks are or account for differences in task difficulty across benchmarks. Determining the difficulty of individual tasks by repeatedly running multiple agents is costly, while a predicted difficulty score alone provides limited guidance on what support an agent needs. To address these challenges, we propose DARE (Difficulty Analysis for Repair Enhancement), which integrates repair-task difficulty measurement, prediction, feature-group contribution analysis, and task-specific repair support. We define continuous task difficulty as the observed failure rate across multiple coding agents. The DARE difficulty model predicts this difficulty using 151 static features extracted from benchmark artifacts. The framework also analyzes the contributions of seven Repair-Task Feature Groups to identify the characteristics most salient to each task and uses this information to select task-specific agent support. We evaluate DARE on four benchmark partitions comprising 2,594 tasks. The difficulty model's aligned predictions achieve Spearman's $\rho$ = .476-.621 on held-out partitions, and the most salient Feature Groups vary across tasks and benchmarks. Furthermore, coding-agent experiments show that matching support to each task's salient Feature Group raises repair success from 54.7% to 79.6% on nine high-difficulty tasks. These findings demonstrate that difficulty prediction and interpretation can lead to practical improvements in automated program repair (APR), while also providing a basis for difficulty-stratified agent evaluation, cross-benchmark comparisons, and identifying areas of repair capability that warrant further improvement.|[2611.05797](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05797)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Today's Work, Tomorrow's Workforce: Incentivizing Learning in AI-Assisted Work**|Moritz Diener et.al.|At work, people develop skills through experience: novices acquire skills by completing tasks, while experts retain theirs through continued practice. AI assistance changes this process by offering workers a choice between solving tasks independently and accepting the AI's answers. While relying on AI saves immediate effort, it can also displace the practice needed to acquire and retain skills, including those required to oversee the AI. We extend an existing accept-or-solve model to a sequence of tasks in which workers' skills evolve through independent practice and reliance on AI. We compare myopic workers, who maximize immediate utility, with strategic workers, who also value their future skills. We show that novices can become trapped at low skill levels, while experts may lose skill, even as AI-assisted outcome accuracy remains high. Strategic workers, by contrast, may choose to solve independently even when AI is more accurate, recognizing the future value of acquiring and retaining their skills. We then study incentives that reward correct independent work and characterize the payments needed to encourage skill acquisition and retention. We show that independent work can become self-sustaining once workers acquire sufficient skill, eliminating the need for continued incentives. Counterintuitively, supporting a higher skill target can sometimes reduce long-run incentive costs, either by enabling self-sustaining independent work or by lowering the payments needed to maintain proficiency. Our findings thereby shift the focus from whether workers should rely on AI on individual tasks to how organizations can sustain human expertise over time, and which skills are worth investing in.|[2611.06346](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06346)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Route First, See Later: Enabling Vision Agents to Reason Scientifically by Separating Routing Policy from Action Spaces**|Shubhabrata Mukherjee et.al.|Scientific image-analysis workflows require agents to select among diverse capabilities like description, detection, counting, and segmentation. Current multi-tool architectures conflate intent interpretation with visual execution, masking whether a failure arises from flawed reasoning (misrouting) or failed perception (invalid tool execution). This lack of auditability renders agents unreliable for high-stakes scientific automation. We introduce ZETA, an extensible architecture that operationalizes the decoupling of routing policy from action execution. ZETA resolves scientific intent before invoking specialized visual tools, treating measurement as a deterministic operation grounded in explicit geometry rather than generative output. This architecture exposes the "black box" of agent decision-making, allowing routing correctness and downstream execution to be evaluated as independent failure modes. To our knowledge, we provide the first controlled study in scientific vision-agent routing that isolates explicit routing-policy specification while holding the legal action interface fixed. For Qwen 3.8, explicit routing-policy specification improves segmentation routing accuracy from 6.2% to 75.0% and detection routing accuracy from 60.0% to 100%. ZETA's execution traces make routing decisions and downstream artifacts separately observable, providing a basis for distinguishing intent-selection errors from visual execution failures. ZETA establishes the necessary infrastructure to isolate execution errors from interpretive ones, a critical requirement for deploying reliable scientific vision agents.|[2611.06760](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06760)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Oscillatory Phase Coding Emerges in Adaptive RNNs for Flexible Working Memory**|Kehan Lu et.al.|Oscillatory phase coding (OPC) is widely observed in neural systems and has been implicated in cognitive functions such as memory and sequence processing. However, its computational role and neural substrates remain largely unclear. Here, we studied OPC by training recurrent neural networks (RNNs) with adaptation to perform sequence working memory tasks with variable delays. After training, we found that OPC emerges in the RNN spontaneously: the memorized items recur at distinct phases of a shared rhythm, with oscillation amplitude encoding item identity and phase encoding serial order. Ablation studies identified three key elements required for OPC: adaptation in neural dynamics, variable delay in memory retention, and flexibility in reading out information. Neuron-type analysis revealed the emergence of content and time cells in the trained RNN, and they process item identity and sequence order separately. Recurrent connectivity analysis revealed phase-organized connections among time cells and structured connections within digit-selective content-cell groups. Inspired by these findings, we proposed a neural circuit model which reproduces OPC through interactions between content units and an adaptive time ring. Together, this study reveals how OPC arises from network structures and task demands, offers insights into the implementation and computational mechanisms of OPC in biological systems, and inspires us to develop computational models for human-like cognitive functions.|[2611.06932](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06932)|
|**2026-11-09**|**Archetypal contexts in the neurons of language models: an architecture for intelligence?**|David Nordfors et.al.|We validate our previous hypothesis that a language model holds relational patterns apart from the subject domains that instantiate them (Nordfors, 2026). These archetypal contexts are patterns of relations that people recognize across many fields, such as the tipping point or winner takes all, held once and applied across domains. We show this inside the model, Qwen3.8-27B. One set of neurons carries the archetype, say the tipping point; another carries the topic, say politics, ecology or cell biology; a third carries the language, such as English, Spanish or Chinese. Silence the tipping point's neurons, and the model no longer identifies the pattern but still knows what the text is about. Silence the topic's, and it loses the topic but still names the pattern. In the neuron maps the layers organize themselves into a sandwich: the outer layers carry languages, the layers inside them topics, the layers at the heart archetypes. The sandwich emerges spontaneously during pretraining and appears in every model we mapped. Archetypes are the architecture for analogy, and we strongly suggest that this structure of archetypes and topics is the mechanism of a language model's intelligence: we see it self-organize as the model is trained. The neurons, the texts and the code are released with this paper as its reproduction package, so that anyone can repeat the test inside the model.|[2611.07234](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07234)|
